===Original===
He raised his dark, gentle eyes to my face and seemed surprised.

===Chinese===
他抬起那双温和的黑眼睛望着我的脸，似乎吃了一惊。

===Original===
“Yes! Perfectly.”

===Chinese===
「明白！完全明白。」

===Original===
This was all I was to hear from his lips concerning the merits of *Almayer’s Folly*. We never spoke together of the book again. A long period of bad weather set in and I had no thoughts left but for my duties, whilst poor Jacques caught a fatal cold and had to keep close in his cabin. When we arrived in Adelaide the first reader of my prose went at once upcountry, and died rather suddenly in the end, either in Australia or it may be on the passage while going home through the Suez Canal. I am not sure which it was now, and I do not think I ever heard precisely; though I made inquiries about him from some of our return passengers who, wandering about to “see the country” during the ship’s stay in port, had come upon him here and there. At last we sailed, homeward bound, and still not one line was added to the careless scrawl of the many pages which poor Jacques had had the patience to read with the very shadows of Eternity gathering already in the hollows of his kind, steadfast eyes.

===Chinese===
关于《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》的好坏，我从他唇间听到的全部，就是这些。我们后来再也没有一同谈起过这本书。一段长长的坏天气开始了，我除了职务再无别的心思；而可怜的雅克染上致命的风寒，只得闭门守在他的舱房里。我们抵达阿德莱德后，我散文的第一位读者立刻上了内地，末了死得相当突然——或在澳大利亚，也可能在取道苏伊士运河归家的航途中。如今我已说不准是哪一种，也不记得曾听得确讯；虽然我向几位回程乘客打听过他的下落，他们趁船停港四处游荡「看看内地」，曾在这里那里碰见过他。我们终于启航，向家乡去，而那一大摞潦草纸页——可怜的雅克怀着何等的耐心读过的那些——再没有添上一行字，虽然永恒的影子那时已经落进他那双和善、坚定的眼睛的深处。

===Original===
The purpose instilled into me by his simple and final “Distinctly” remained dormant, yet alive to await its opportunity. I dare say I am compelled, unconsciously compelled, now to write volume after volume, as in past years I was compelled to go to sea, voyage after voyage. Leaves must follow upon each other as leagues used to follow in the days gone by, on and on to the appointed end, which, being Truth itself, is One﻿—one for all men and for all occupations.

===Chinese===
由他那句简单而最终的「当然值得」注入我心中的目标，一直蛰伏着，却活着，等候它的时机。我敢说，如今我是被驱使着——无意识地被驱使着——一卷接一卷地写，正如往年的我，是被驱使着一次接一次出海。书页必须一页跟着一页，像往昔岁月里的海程一里格接一里格，一路向前，直到那命定的终点；那终点既是真实本身，便是「一」——对一切人、对一切行当，都是同一个一。
===Original===
I do not know which of the two impulses has appeared more mysterious and more wonderful to me. Still, in writing, as in going to sea, I had to wait my opportunity. Let me confess here that I was never one of those wonderful fellows that would go afloat in a washtub for the sake of the fun, and if I may pride myself upon my consistency, it was ever just the same with my writing. Some men, I have heard, write in railway carriages, and could do it, perhaps, sitting cross-legged on a clothesline; but I must confess that my sybaritic disposition will not consent to write without something at least resembling a chair. Line by line, rather than page by page, was the growth of *Almayer’s Folly*.

===Chinese===
这两种冲动，哪一种在我眼里显得更神秘、更奇妙，我说不上。只是，写作同出海一样，我也得等待我的时机。让我在此承认：我从来不是那种了不得的角色，为了好玩肯坐一只浴盆下海；倘若我可以为自己的始终如一自诩，那么我的写作也从来正是如此。我听说有些人在火车车厢里写作，也许还能盘腿坐在晾衣绳上写；但我必须承认，我这贪图安逸的性子，少了一把至少像椅子的东西，是不肯动笔的。《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》便是这样一行一行——而不是一页一页——长起来的。

===Original===
And so it happened that I very nearly lost the MS., advanced now to the first words of the ninth chapter, in the Friedrichstrasse railway station (that’s in Berlin, you know) on my way to Poland, or more precisely Ukraine. On an early, sleepy morning changing trains in a hurry, I left my Gladstone bag in a refreshment-room. A worthy and intelligent *Kofferträger* rescued it. Yet in my anxiety I was not thinking of the MS. but of all the other things that were packed in the bag.

===Chinese===
于是就有了这么一桩事：我差点丢了手稿——那时它已进展到第九章的头几句话——地点在腓特烈大街火车站（那是柏林，你知道），我正在去波兰的路上，说得更准些，去乌克兰。一个清早，睡意惺忪，赶着换车，我把格拉德斯通旅行袋落在了一家茶点室里。一位可敬而机灵的 *Kofferträger*（德语：搬运夫）把它救了回来。可笑的是，着急的我心里想的并不是手稿，而是袋子里装着的所有别的东西。

===Original===
In Warsaw, where I spent two days, those wandering pages were never exposed to the light, except once to candlelight, while the bag lay open on a chair. I was dressing hurriedly to dine at a sporting club. A friend of my childhood (he had been in the Diplomatic Service, but had turned to growing wheat on paternal acres, and we had not seen each other for over twenty years) was sitting on the hotel sofa waiting to carry me off there.

===Chinese===
在华沙盘桓的两天里，那几页漂泊的纸从不曾见过天日，只有一次见了烛光，那时旅行袋敞着口躺在椅子上。我正匆匆更衣，要去一家狩猎俱乐部吃饭。我童年的一位朋友（他进过外交界，后来改在祖传的田庄上种麦子，我们彼此已二十多年未见）坐在旅馆沙发上，等着把我押赴那里。

===Original===
“You might tell me something of your life while you are dressing,” he suggested kindly.

===Chinese===
「趁你穿衣裳，不妨给我讲讲你的生活，」他好意提议道。

===Original===
I do not think I told him much of my life-story either then or later. The talk of the select little party with which he made me dine was extremely animated and embraced most subjects under heaven, from big-game shooting in Africa to the last poem published in a very modernist review, edited by the very young and patronised by the highest society. But it never touched upon *Almayer’s Folly*, and next morning, in uninterrupted obscurity, this inseparable companion went on rolling with me in the southeast direction towards the Government of Kiev.

===Chinese===
我不觉得我当时或后来对他讲过我平生的多少事。他领我去同席的那一小桌精选人物的谈话，热闹极了，天上地下无所不谈：从非洲猎巨兽，一直谈到一份极摩登的评论刊物上最新发表的诗——那刊物由非常年轻的人编辑、最受上流社会垂顾。可它从没碰到《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》一个字。次日清晨，这份须臾不离的伴侣又在一成不变的隐匿中，随我乘上车，朝东南方的基辅省滚去。

===Original===
At that time there was an eight-hours’ drive, if not more, from the railway station to the country house which was my destination.

===Chinese===
那时候，从火车站到我目的地的那座乡宅，坐车要走八个钟头，只多不少。

===Original===
“Dear boy” (these words were always written in English)﻿—so ran the last letter from that house received in London﻿—“Get yourself driven to the only inn in the place, dine as well as you can, and some time in the evening my own confidential servant, factotum and majordomo, a Mr. V. S. (I warn you he is of noble extraction), will present himself before you, reporting the arrival of the small sledge which will take you here on the next day. I send with him my heaviest fur, which I suppose with such overcoats as you may have with you will keep you from freezing on the road.”

===Chinese===
「亲爱的孩子」（这几个字向来是用英文写的）——这是我在伦敦收到的、发自那座宅子的最后一封信——「叫人把你送到本地唯一的那家客栈，尽力吃顿好饭，晚上某个时候，我自己那位心腹仆人、总管兼大管家 V. S. 先生（我警告你，他有贵族血统），会到你面前报到，说接你的小雪橇到了，明天就接你到这儿来。我随他给你捎去我最重的一件皮袄；我想，加上你自己随身可能带的几件大衣，路上冻不着你。」

===Original===
Sure enough, as I was dining, served by a Hebrew waiter, in an enormous barn-like bedroom with a freshly painted floor, the door opened and, in a travelling costume of long boots, big sheepskin cap and a short coat girt with a leather belt, the Mr. V. S. (of noble extraction), a man of about thirty-five, appeared with an air of perplexity on his open and moustachioed countenance. I got up from the table and greeted him in Polish, with, I hope, the right shade of consideration demanded by his noble blood and his confidential position. His face cleared up in a wonderful way. It appeared that, notwithstanding my uncle’s earnest assurances, the good fellow had remained in doubt of our understanding each other. He imagined I would talk to him in some foreign language. I was told that his last words on getting into the sledge to come to meet me shaped an anxious exclamation:

===Chinese===
果不其然，我正用着晚餐——伺候我的是位犹太侍者，在那间谷仓般的大卧室里，地板是新漆过的——门开了，V. S. 先生（贵族血统的那位）走进来：三十上下的年纪，长靴、大羊皮帽、束着皮腰带的短上衣这一身行路装束，那张开朗的、留着小胡子的脸上带着一点为难的神气。我从桌边起身，用波兰语向他致意——我希望，用的是他那贵族血统和心腹地位所要求的、恰当分寸的敬重。他的脸以一种奇妙的方式豁然开朗了。原来，尽管我舅舅一再郑重担保，这位好人始终拿不准我们能不能彼此听懂。他料想我会用某种外国语同他讲话。后来我听说，他上雪橇动身来接我时说的最后一句话，是一声忧心忡忡的感叹：
===Original===
“Well! Well! Here I am going, but God only knows how I am to make myself understood to our master’s nephew.”

===Chinese===
「唉！唉！我是去了，可天知道我怎么才能让咱们老爷的外甥听懂我的话。」

===Original===
We understood each other very well from the first. He took charge of me as if I were not quite of age. I had a delightful boyish feeling of coming home from school when he muffled me up next morning in an enormous bearskin travelling-coat and took his seat protectively by my side. The sledge was a very small one and it looked utterly insignificant, almost like a toy behind the four big bays harnessed two and two. We three, counting the coachman, filled it completely. He was a young fellow with clear blue eyes; the high collar of his livery fur coat framed his cheery countenance and stood all round level with the top of his head.

===Chinese===
我们从一开头就彼此听得极好。他照应我，仿佛我还未成年。第二天早晨，他用一件硕大无朋的熊皮旅行大衣把我裹起来，护着人似的在我身边落座，我心里有一种快活的、放了学回家的孩子气的感觉。雪橇很小，看着小得可怜，套在两两成对的四匹高大的骝马后面，简直像个玩具。连车夫算上，我们三个把它坐得满满的。车夫是个蓝眼睛晶亮的年轻人；号衣皮领高高竖起，框住他红润快活的脸，一圈齐到了头顶。

===Original===
“Now, Joseph,” my companion addressed him, “do you think we shall manage to get home before six?” His answer was that we would surely, with God’s help, and providing there were no heavy drifts in the long stretch between certain villages whose names came with an extremely familiar sound to my ears. He turned out an excellent coachman with an instinct for keeping the road amongst the snow-covered fields and a natural gift of getting the best out of the horses.

===Chinese===
「喂，约瑟夫，」我同伴招呼他道，「你看我们赶得上六点前到家么？」他的回答是：靠上帝保佑，准赶得上，只求那几个村子之间的一长段路上别积起太厚的雪——那些村名，在我听来耳熟得很。他果然是位出色的车夫：在被雪盖没的田野间认路，靠的是本能；从马身上榨出最大的力气来，靠的是天分。

===Original===
“He is the son of that Joseph that I suppose the Captain remembers. He who used to drive the Captain’s late grandmother of holy memory,” remarked V. S. busy tucking fur rugs about my feet.

===Chinese===
「他就是那位约瑟夫的儿子——船长想必还记得——从前给船长那位已故的、愿她安息的祖母赶车的那位，」V. S. 说这话时，正忙着往我脚边掖毛毯。

===Original===
I remembered perfectly the trusty Joseph who used to drive my grandmother. Why! he it was who let me hold the reins for the first time in my life and allowed me to play with the great four-in-hand whip outside the doors of the coach-house.

===Chinese===
我清清楚楚记得那位给我外祖母赶车的忠厚的约瑟夫。可不是么！我这辈子头一回握缰绳，就是他让给我的；在车房门外，他允许我玩耍那根四马大车用的大长鞭。

===Original===
“What became of him?” I asked. “He is no longer serving, I suppose.”

===Chinese===
「他后来怎么样了？」我问。「想必不再当差了吧。」

===Original===
“He served our master,” was the reply. “But he died of cholera ten years ago now﻿—that great epidemic we had. And his wife died at the same time﻿—the whole houseful of them, and this is the only boy that was left.”

===Chinese===
「他伺候咱们老爷来着，」回答是这么说的。「可十年前他得霍乱死了——咱们闹的那场大瘟疫。他老婆也是同时死的——一屋子人死绝了，就剩下这一个男孩。」

===Original===
The MS. of *Almayer’s Folly* was reposing in the bag under our feet.

===Chinese===
《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》的手稿，正安卧在我们脚底下的袋子里。
===Original===
I saw again the sun setting on the plains as I saw it in the travels of my childhood. It set, clear and red, dipping into the snow in full view as if it were setting on the sea. It was twenty-three years since I had seen the sun set over that land; and we drove on in the darkness which fell swiftly upon the livid expanse of snows till, out of the waste of a white earth joining a bestarred sky, surged up black shapes, the clumps of trees about a village of the Ukrainian plain. A cottage or two glided by, a low interminable wall and then, glimmering and winking through a screen of fir-trees, the lights of the master’s house.

===Chinese===
我又看见了平原上的落日，同我童年旅途中所见的一样。它落得清明而红艳，当着人的面浸进雪里，就像沉进大海似的。我上一回看见那片土地上的落日，已是二十三年前了；我们在黑暗里赶着路——黑暗倏地落在青灰色的茫茫雪原上——直到后来，从那片白色的荒地、那与缀满星辰的天空相接的白地里，涌出一团团黑影：乌克兰平原上一座村子四周的树丛。一两座农舍滑了过去，一道望不到头的矮墙，然后，隔着杉树的屏障，主人宅子的灯火在眨眼、在闪亮。

===Original===
That very evening the wandering MS. of *Almayer’s Folly* was unpacked and unostentatiously laid on the writing-table in my room, the guestroom which had been, I was informed in an affectedly careless tone, awaiting me for some fifteen years or so. It attracted no attention from the affectionate presence hovering round the son of the favourite sister.

===Chinese===
就在当晚，那份漂泊的《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》手稿被打开行李取出，不声不响地摊在我房间的书桌上——那间客房，有人用一种故作随便的口气告诉我，已经等了我十五年上下。它丝毫没有引起那团慈爱的存在——它正围着宠姊的爱子转来转去——的注意。

===Original===
“You won’t have many hours to yourself while you are staying with me, brother,” he said﻿—this form of address borrowed from the speech of our peasants being the usual expression of the highest good humour in a moment of affectionate elation. “I shall be always coming in for a chat.”

===Chinese===
「你在我这儿住着，不会有几个钟头归你自己的，兄弟，」他说——这种称呼借自我们农民的言语，是亲热欢畅的时刻里表示最高兴致的惯用语。「我会时不时过来聊聊。」

===Original===
As a matter of fact we had the whole house to chat in, and were everlastingly intruding upon each other. I invaded the retirement of his study where the principal feature was a colossal silver inkstand presented to him on his fiftieth year by a subscription of all his wards then living. He had been guardian of many orphans of land-owning families from the three southern provinces﻿—ever since the year 1860. Some of them had been my schoolfellows and playmates, but not one of them, girls or boys, that I know of has ever written a novel. One or two were older than myself﻿—considerably older, too. One of them, a visitor I remember in my early years, was the man who first put me on horseback, and his four-horse bachelor turnout, his perfect horsemanship and general skill in manly exercises was one of my earliest admirations. I seem to remember my mother looking on from a colonnade in front of the dining-room windows as I was lifted upon the pony, held, for all I know, by the very Joseph﻿—the groom attached specially to my grandmother’s service﻿—who died of cholera. It was certainly a young man in a dark blue, tailless coat and huge Cossack trousers, that being the livery of the men about the stables. It must have been in 1864, but reckoning by another mode of calculating time, it was certainly in the year in which my mother obtained permission to travel south and visit her family, from the exile into which she had followed my father. For that, too, she had had to ask permission, and I know that one of the conditions of that favour was that she should be treated exactly as a condemned exile herself. Yet a couple of years later, in memory of her eldest brother who had served in the Guards and dying early left hosts of friends and a loved memory in the great world of St. Petersburg, some influential personages procured for her this permission﻿—it was officially called the “Highest Grace”﻿—of a four months’ leave from exile.

===Chinese===
事实上，我们有一整座宅子好聊天，还永远在互相打搅。我侵入他书房的清静，那里最主要的物件，是一座巨大的银墨水台——他五十岁那年，由当时在世的他全部受监护人集资送给他的。他做过南方三省许多地主孤儿的监护人——从 1860 年起。其中几位曾是我的同学和玩伴，但据我所知，无论男孩女孩，没有一个后来写过小说。有一两个比我还大——还大得多。其中一位，我记得是我幼年时来家的客人，是头一个把我扶上马背的人；他那一套四马的单身汉车驾、他完美的骑术和他在种种男子技艺上的全套本领，是我最早的钦慕之一。我仿佛记得，我被抱上小型马背时，母亲正从餐室窗前的一道柱廊里望着——牵马的，说不定正是那位约瑟夫——专属于我外祖母的马夫——后来得霍乱死掉的那位。牵马的必定是个穿深蓝无尾上衣、套肥大哥萨克裤的年轻人，那正是马厩仆役的号衣。那必定是在 1864 年；但若按另一种计时法，那一年肯定是母亲获准南下省亲的一年——她此前随父亲踏入了流放之地。为了这件事，她也得先请求许可，而我知道，那项恩典的条件之一，是她须被当作一名定罪的流放犯来对待。然而两年之后——为了纪念她的长兄：他曾在禁卫军中服役，早逝之后，在圣彼得堡的大世界里留下众多朋友和一片受人爱念的回忆——几位有势力的人物为她弄到了这个许可——官方文书里称作「最高恩典」——一次为期四个月的流放假期。

===Original===
This is also the year in which I first begin to remember my mother with more distinctness than a mere loving, wide-browed, silent, protecting presence, whose eyes had a sort of commanding sweetness; and I also remember the great gathering of all the relations from near and far, and the grey heads of the family friends paying her the homage of respect and love in the house of her favourite brother who, a few years later, was to take the place for me of both my parents.

===Chinese===
也是在这一年，我记忆中的母亲才开始不再只是一个可爱的、宽额的、沉默的、护佑着人的存在——她的眼睛有一种发号施令般的甜美；我也记得远近的亲戚们全体大聚会，记得家族故交们的灰白头颅，在她最宠爱的兄长的宅子里向她致以敬与爱的敬意——那位兄长，几年之后，便要在我这里顶替我双亲两人的位置。

===Original===
I did not understand the tragic significance of it all at the time, though indeed I remember that doctors also came. There were no signs of invalidism about her﻿—but I think that already they had pronounced her doom unless perhaps the change to a southern climate could reestablish her declining strength. For me it seems the very happiest period of my existence. There was my cousin, a delightful, quick-tempered little girl, some months younger than myself, whose life, lovingly watched over, as if she were a royal princess, came to an end with her fifteenth year. There were other children, too, many of whom are dead now, and not a few whose very names I have forgotten. Over all this hung the oppressive shadow of the great Russian Empire﻿—the shadow lowering with the darkness of a newborn national hatred fostered by the Moscow school of journalists against the Poles after the ill-omened rising of 1863.

===Chinese===
我当时不懂这一切的悲剧含义，虽然我确实记得，医生们也来过。她身上没有病人的征象——但我想，他们早已宣了她的死刑，除非换到南方的气候能挽回她日衰的体力。在我看来，那似乎是我一生最最快活的时期。有我的表妹，一个可爱的、急性子的小姑娘，比我小几个月；她那被钟爱地呵护着的生命——仿佛她是一位王室的公主——到十五岁便完结了。还有别的孩子，许多如今已经死了，更有不少连名字我都忘了。笼罩着这一切的，是俄罗斯帝国那片沉沉的阴影——1863 年那场不祥的起义之后，莫斯科的新闻记者一派对波兰人培植起一种新生的民族仇恨，阴影便带着这黑暗压了下来。

===Original===
This is a far cry back from the MS. of *Almayer’s Folly*, but the public record of these formative impressions is not the whim of an uneasy egotism. These, too, are things human, already distant in their appeal. It is meet that something more should be left for the novelist’s children than the colours and figures of his own hard-won creation. That which in their grown-up years may appear to the world about them as the most enigmatic side of their natures and perhaps must remain forever obscure even to themselves, will be their unconscious response to the still voice of that inexorable past from which his work of fiction and their personalities are remotely derived.

===Chinese===
从《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》的手稿追溯回来，这已是很远的路了；但把这些塑造性的印象记录在案，并非一颗不安分的唯我之心的一时任性。这些也是人间的事，它们发出的呼声已经遥远了。小说家的孩子们，理应得到比他辛苦赢来的创造物的色彩与形象更多的东西。在他们成年之后，在周围世界眼中或许显得是他们天性中最费解的一面、也许连他们自己也永远参不透的那一面，将是他们对那严酷往昔之寂静声音的无意识回应——他的小说作品与他们的个性，都是遥远地从那往昔里派生出来的。

===Original===
Only in men’s imagination does every truth find an effective and undeniable existence. Imagination, not invention, is the supreme master of art as of life. An imaginative and exact rendering of authentic memories may serve worthily that spirit of piety towards all things human which sanctions the conceptions of a writer of tales, and the emotions of the man reviewing his own experience.

===Chinese===
唯有在人的想象里，每一条真理才找得到一种有效的、无可否认的存在。想象，而非虚构，是艺术正如生活的至高主宰。对真实记忆的一种富于想象而又精确的呈现，或许能相称地服务于那种对万事万人虔敬的精神——那种精神，认可一个讲故事者的种种构想，也认可一个人回顾自身经历时的种种情感。

## II / II

===Original===
As I have said, I was unpacking my luggage after a journey from London into Ukraine. The MS. of *Almayer’s Folly*﻿—my companion already for some three years or more, and then in the ninth chapter of its age﻿—was deposited unostentatiously on the writing-table placed between two windows. It didn’t occur to me to put it away in the drawer the table was fitted with, but my eye was attracted by the good form of the same drawer’s brass handles. Two candelabra, with four candles each, lighted up festally the room which had waited so many years for the wandering nephew. The blinds were down.

Within five hundred yards of the chair on which I sat stood the first peasant hut of the village﻿—part of my maternal grandfather’s estate, the only part remaining in the possession of a member of the family; and beyond the village in the limitless blackness of a winter’s night there lay the great unfenced fields﻿—not a flat and severe plain, but a kindly bread-giving land of low rounded ridges, all white now, with the black patches of timber nestling in the hollows. The road by which I had come ran through the village with a turn just outside the gates closing the short drive. Somebody was abroad on the deep snow-track; a quick tinkle of bells stole gradually into the stillness of the room like a tuneful whisper.

===Chinese===
如前所说，从伦敦来到乌克兰之后，我正开箱整理行李。《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》的手稿——它陪伴我已有三年上下，其「年纪」当时正当第九章——被我不事张扬地安放在两张窗户之间的写字台上。我并未想到要把手稿收进写字台配有的抽屉里，我的目光倒被那抽屉黄铜把手的雅致制式吸引了。两座烛台各插四支蜡烛，把这间多年来等候着浪游侄儿的房间照得喜气盈盈。百叶帘低垂着。

距我坐着的座椅五百码开外，立着村里第一座农舍——那是外祖父庄园的一部分，也是家族成员手中仅存的一部分；村外，在冬夜无边无际的黑暗里，铺展着大片没有围篱的田野——不是一片平坦肃杀的旷野，而是一片低缓浑圆的田垄、一片仁厚赐人面包的土地，此刻通体皆白，黑色的林木斑斑，栖卧在洼地里。我来的那条路穿过村子，在关着短短宅前车道的院门外不远处拐了个弯。有人正赶在深深的雪辙上行走；一串急促的铃声渐渐潜入房内的寂静，像一声悦耳的低语。

===Original===
My unpacking had been watched over by the servant who had come to help me, and, for the most part, had been standing attentive but unnecessary at the door of the room. I did not want him in the least, but I did not like to tell him to go away. He was a young fellow, certainly more than ten years younger than myself; I had not been﻿—I won’t say in that place, but within sixty miles of it, ever since the year ’67; yet his guileless physiognomy of the open peasant type seemed strangely familiar. It was quite possible that he might have been a descendant, a son or even a grandson, of the servants whose friendly faces had been familiar to me in my early childhood. As a matter of fact he had no such claim on my consideration. He was the product of some village near by and was there on his promotion, having learned the service in one or two houses as pantry-boy. I know this because I asked the worthy V﻿—﻿— next day. I might well have spared the question. I discovered before long that all the faces about the house and all the faces in the village: the grave faces with long moustaches of the heads of families, the downy faces of the young men, the faces of the little fair-haired children, the handsome, tanned, wide-browed faces of the mothers seen at the doors of the huts, were as familiar to me as though I had known them all from childhood, and my childhood were a matter of the day before yesterday.

===Chinese===
我的开箱整理自始至终有仆人在旁照看，他本是进来帮我收拾的，大半时间立在房门口，殷勤有余，却属多余。我全然用不着他，可又不忍叫他走开。他是个小伙子，肯定比我年轻十岁不止；且不说那个地方，就连方圆六十英里之内，自 1867 年起我也再没有到过；然而他那张农家型开朗而无狡黠的面相，却出奇地眼熟。他完全有可能是那些仆人的后代，儿子甚或孙子——那些人的友善面容，在我幼年时是再熟悉不过的。事实是，他并无这般让我另眼相待的来历。他是附近哪个村子出来的人，是升了一级才到这儿来的，先前在一两户人家当过配膳房的听差，学会了伺候人的规矩。这一点我是第二天问过那位可敬的 V—— 才知道的。这个问题其实大可省去。不久我便发现，宅子里上下所有的面孔，村里所有的面孔——一家之主们蓄着长髭的庄重面孔，年轻人茸毛未褪的面孔，浅发孩童的面孔，在农舍门口望见的母亲们那张晒得黝黑、额头宽阔的俊美面孔——对我全都熟稔如故，仿佛我自幼便认识他们，仿佛我的童年不过是前天的事。

===Original===
The tinkle of the traveller’s bells, after growing louder, had faded away quickly, and the tumult of barking dogs in the village had calmed down at last. My uncle, lounging in the corner of a small couch, smoked his long Turkish chibouk in silence.

“This is an extremely nice writing-table you have got for my room,” I remarked.

===Chinese===
旅人的铃声先是渐响，随即迅速消逝，村里犬吠的骚动也终于平息下来。舅舅斜倚在小沙发的一角，默然抽着他那杆长长的土耳其式烟斗。

「你们为我这间屋子置办的这张写字台，真是雅致极了。」我说道。

===Original===
“It is really your property,” he said, keeping his eyes on me, with an interested and wistful expression, as he had done ever since I had entered the house. “Forty years ago your mother used to write at this very table. In our house in Oratow it stood in the little sitting-room which, by a tacit arrangement, was given up to the girls﻿—I mean to your mother and her sister who died so young. It was a present to them jointly from our uncle Nicholas B. when your mother was seventeen and your aunt two years younger. She was a very dear, delightful girl, that aunt of yours, of whom I suppose you know nothing more than the name. She did not shine so much by personal beauty and a cultivated mind, in which your mother was far superior. It was her good sense, the admirable sweetness of her nature, her exceptional facility and ease in daily relations that endeared her to everybody. Her death was a terrible grief and a serious moral loss for us all. Had she lived she would have brought the greatest blessings to the house it would have been her lot to enter, as wife, mother and mistress of a household. She would have created round herself an atmosphere of peace and content which only those who can love unselfishly are able to evoke. Your mother﻿—of far greater beauty, exceptionally distinguished in person, manner and intellect﻿—had a less easy disposition. Being more brilliantly gifted, she also expected more from life. At that trying time especially, we were greatly concerned about her state. Suffering in her health from the shock of her father’s death (she was alone in the house with him when he died suddenly), she was torn by the inward struggle between her love for the man whom she was to marry in the end and her knowledge of her dead father’s declared objection to that match. Unable to bring herself to disregard that cherished memory and that judgment she had always respected and trusted, and, on the other hand, feeling the impossibility to resist a sentiment so deep and so true, she could not have been expected to preserve her mental and moral balance. At war with herself, she could not give to others that feeling of peace which was not her own. It was only later, when united at last with the man of her choice, that she developed those uncommon gifts of mind and heart which compelled the respect and admiration even of our foes. Meeting with calm fortitude the cruel trials of a life reflecting all the national and social misfortunes of the community, she realised the highest conceptions of duty as a wife, a mother and a patriot, sharing the exile of her husband and representing nobly the ideal of Polish womanhood. Our Uncle Nicholas was not a man very accessible to feelings of affection. Apart from his worship for Napoleon the Great, he loved really, I believe, only three people in the world: his mother﻿—your great-grandmother, whom you have seen but cannot possibly remember; his brother, our father, in whose house he lived for so many years; and of all of us, his nephews and nieces grown up around him, your mother alone. The modest, lovable qualities of the youngest sister he did not seem able to see. It was I who felt most profoundly this unexpected stroke of death falling upon the family less than a year after I had become its head. It was terribly unexpected. Driving home one wintry afternoon to keep me company in our empty house, where I had to remain permanently administering the estate and attending to the complicated affairs﻿—the girls took it in turn week and week about﻿—driving, as I said, from the house of the Countess Tekla Potocka, where our invalid mother was staying then to be near a doctor, they lost the road and got stuck in a snowdrift. She was alone with the coachman and old Valery, the personal servant of our late father. Impatient of delay while they were trying to dig themselves out, she jumped out of the sledge and went to look for the road herself. All this happened in ’51, not ten miles from the house in which we are sitting now. The road was soon found, but snow had begun to fall thickly again, and they were four more hours getting home. Both the men took off their sheepskin-lined greatcoats and used all their own rugs to wrap her up against the cold, notwithstanding her protests, positive orders and even struggles, as Valery afterwards related to me. ‘How could I,’ he remonstrated with her, ‘go to meet the blessed soul of my late master if I let any harm come to you while there’s a spark of life left in my body?’ When they reached home at last the poor old man was stiff and speechless from exposure, and the coachman was in not much better plight, though he had the strength to drive round to the stables himself. To my reproaches for venturing out at all in such weather, she answered characteristically that she could not bear the thought of abandoning me to my cheerless solitude. It is incomprehensible how it was that she was allowed to start. I suppose it had to be! She made light of the cough which came on next day, but shortly afterwards inflammation of the lungs set in, and in three weeks she was no more! She was the first to be taken away of the young generation under my care. Behold the vanity of all hopes and fears! I was the most frail at birth of all the children. For years I remained so delicate that my parents had but little hope of bringing me up; and yet I have survived five brothers and two sisters, and many of my contemporaries; I have outlived my wife and daughter, too﻿—and from all those who have had some knowledge at least of these old times you alone are left. It has been my lot to lay in an early grave many honest hearts, many brilliant promises, many hopes full of life.”

===Chinese===
「这实在是你自己的东西，」他说，目光始终留在我身上，带着一种关切而怅惘的神情，自我进这宅子以来他一直是如此。「四十年前，你母亲就是在这张桌子上写字的。在奥拉图夫（Oratow）我们家的宅子里，它摆在那间小起居室里——那屋子经一种心照不宣的安排，是让给姑娘们的——我是说，让给你母亲和她那位早逝的妹妹。那是咱们的舅公尼古拉斯·B（Nicholas B.）送给她们姊妹俩的一份共同礼物，那时你母亲十七岁，你姨母小她两岁。你那位姨母，是个非常可爱、非常讨人喜欢的姑娘，我想你对她的了解，恐怕也只剩一个名字了。她的光彩并不怎么在容貌之美与教养之厚上——在这两方面，你母亲都要出色得多。是她的明达，是她天性中可钦的温婉，是她在日常相处中少有的随和从容，让人人都疼爱她。她的死，对我们大家是一场可怕的悲恸，也是一次严重的道义损失。她若活着，本会给她命定要进的那户人家带去最大的福祉——为人妻，为人母，为一家主妇。她本会在自己四周营造出一片安宁与满足的气氛，这样的气氛，唯有能无私去爱的人才唤得出来。你母亲——美貌远胜，人品、仪态与才智又格外出众——性情却没有那么随和。她天分焕发，对生活的期求也就更多。尤其在那个煎熬人的时期，我们都为她的状况深深忧虑。父亲猝逝的震动损害了她的健康（他死得突然，当时屋中只有她一人在侧），她又被内心的争斗撕裂着：一边是对那个她终究要嫁的男人的爱，一边是她深知亡父生前曾明白表示反对这桩婚事。她既无法迫使自己漠视那份珍视的回忆、漠视她一向敬重信托的那一判断，另一面又觉得不可能抗拒一份如此深沉、如此真挚的情感，我们本就不能指望她守住心智与道义的平衡。她与自己交战，便无法把那种她自己并不拥有的安宁之感给予别人。直到后来，她终于与自己选定的人结合了，她才焕发出那些心智与心灵的不凡禀赋，连我们的仇敌也不得不肃然起敬。她以沉静的坚毅，迎接了那映照着整个民族与社会种种不幸的残酷人生考验，把为人妻、为人母、为爱国者的责任尽到了最高境地，与丈夫同尝流放，高贵地体现了波兰女性的理想。咱们的舅公尼古拉斯，不是个轻易敞开温情的人。除了对拿破仑大帝的崇拜，我相信他在这世上真心爱的只有三个人：他的母亲——你的外曾祖母，你见过她，但不可能记得了；他的兄弟，也就是我们的父亲，他在父亲家中住了那么多年；还有我们这一辈围绕着他长大的侄男甥女中间，他只爱你母亲一个。那位小妹妹身上谦逊可爱的品性，他似乎视而不见。死神这一记出其不意的打击落在家庭头上时，距我接掌这个家还不到一年，而感受最深的正是我。那真是意想不到的可怕。一个冬日的午后，她们驱车回家，来陪陪我，给我的空宅添个人气——我不得不常年守在那里，管理田庄，料理种种繁难的事务，姑娘们便一人一周轮着来——那天她们是从来处动身的，也就是泰克拉·波托茨卡伯爵夫人（Countess Tekla Potocka）的宅邸，我们那位多病的母亲当时住在那里，为的是就近延医——路上，她们迷失了道，陷进了雪堆里。车上只有她、车夫和瓦莱里（Valery），瓦莱里是我们亡父的贴身仆人。他们正设法把雪橇刨出来，她不堪耽搁，跳下雪橇，亲自去寻路。这一切都发生在 1851 年，离我们现在坐着的这所宅子不到十英里。路很快就找到了，可是雪又下得紧了，他们又走了四个钟头才到家。两个男人都脱下衬羊皮的大衣，又把自己随身的毯子尽数裹在她身上为她御寒，任凭她抗议、严令、甚至挣扎——这是瓦莱里后来讲给我听的。『我怎么能，』他当时这样劝她，『在我这身子还剩一口气的时候，让您受半点损伤，回头又怎么有脸去见先主人的在天之灵？』他们终于到家时，可怜的老人已经冻得僵直，说不出话，车夫的情形也好不了多少，尽管他还有力气自己把车赶去马厩。我怪她这样的天气竟敢贸然出门，她给我的回答很能见出她的为人：她说，一想到把我一个人撂在凄清冷寂里，她就受不了。她怎么会获准动身，实在叫人难以理解。我想，这怕是命中注定的吧！第二天她咳嗽起来，满不当回事，可不久肺炎便发作了，三个星期后，她就不在人世了！她是我照管下的年轻一代里，第一个被带走的人。看吧，一切希望与忧惧，何其虚空！生下来的时候，我是所有孩子里最孱弱的一个。好多年里我一直是那样孱弱，父母对我能否养大几乎不抱指望；然而我竟送走了五个兄弟、两个姊妹，送走了许多同龄人；我也活得比自己的妻子和女儿都长——而所有那些至少还知道些当年旧事的人里，如今只剩下你一个了。把许多颗诚实的心、许多灿烂的前程、许多生气勃勃的希望，一一亲手送进早坟，正是我的命分。」

===Original===
He got up brusquely, sighed, and left me, saying: “We will dine in half an hour.” Without moving, I listened to his quick steps resounding on the waxed floor of the next room, traversing the anteroom lined with bookshelves, where he paused to put his chibouk in the pipe-stand before passing into the drawing-room (these were all en suite), where he became inaudible on the thick carpet. But I heard the door of his study-bedroom close. He was then sixty-two years old and had been for a quarter of a century the wisest, the firmest, the most indulgent of guardians, extending over me a paternal care and affection, a moral support which I seemed to feel always near me in the most distant parts of the earth.

As to Mr. Nicholas B., sublieutenant of 1808, lieutenant of 1813 in the French Army, and for a short time Officier d’Ordonnance of Marshal Marmont; afterwards Captain in the 2nd Regiment of Mounted Rifles in the Polish Army﻿—such as it existed up to 1830 in the reduced kingdom established by the Congress of Vienna﻿—I must say that from all that more distant past, known to me traditionally and a little *de visu*, and called out by the words of the man just gone away, he remains the most incomplete figure. It is obvious that I must have seen him in ’64, for it is certain that he would not have missed the opportunity of seeing my mother for what he must have known would be the last time. From my early boyhood to this day, if I try to call up his image, a sort of mist rises before my eyes, mist in which I perceive vaguely only a neatly brushed head of white hair (which is exceptional in the case of the B. family, where it is the rule for men to go bald in a becoming manner, before thirty) and a thin, curved, dignified nose, a feature in strict accordance with the physical tradition of the B. family. But it is not by these fragmentary remains of perishable mortality that he lives in my memory. I knew, at a very early age, that my granduncle Nicholas B. was a Knight of the Legion of Honour and that he had also the Polish Cross for valour, Virtuti Militari. The knowledge of these glorious facts inspired in me an admiring veneration; yet it is not that sentiment, strong as it was, which resumes for me the force and the significance of his personality. It is overborne by another and complex impression of awe, compassion and horror. Mr. Nicholas B. remains for me the unfortunate and miserable (but heroic) being who once upon a time had eaten a dog.

===Chinese===
他霍然起身，叹了口气，撂下一句「半小时后开饭」，便离我而去。我一动不动，听着他那急促的脚步声在隔壁打了蜡的地板上咚咚作响，穿过两旁排满书架的前厅——他在那儿停了停，把烟斗插进烟斗架里，才走进客厅（这几间屋子是成套相连的）——一踏上厚地毯，脚步声便沉寂了。但我听见他那间书房兼卧室的门关上了。他那年六十二岁，而在此前四分之一个世纪里，他一直是最明智、最坚定、也最宽厚的监护人，把父亲般的关怀与慈爱延展到我身上；那是一份道义上的支撑，无论我漂泊到地球上多么遥远的地方，仿佛总能感到它近在身旁。

至于尼古拉斯·B 舅公——1808 年的少尉，1813 年的法军中尉，一度做过马尔蒙元帅（Marshal Marmont）的 *Officier d'Ordonnance*（传令军官），后来又任波兰军骑马步枪兵第二团的上尉——这支军队在 1830 年以前，一直以维也纳会议划定的那个缩域王国的规模存在着——我必须说，在那段更遥远的过去里（我对它的了解，一半得自口耳相传，一半得自少许 *de visu*（亲见）），在那位刚刚离去的男人的话语所唤起的种种形象中间，他始终是最不完整的一个。显而易见，1864 年我必定见过他，因为他肯定不会错过同我母亲见面的机会——他必定知道，那将是最后一次。从少年时代直到今天，每当我试图唤起他的形象，眼前总会浮起一片雾；雾中我只能隐约辨出一头梳得整整齐齐的白发（这在 B 家是个异数——B 家的男人照例都在三十岁以前，便以一种体面的姿态谢了顶），和一只瘦削、微弯、气度庄严的鼻子——这一特征，与 B 家的体貌传统严丝合缝。但他在我记忆中活着的凭据，并不是这些速朽肉身的残片。我很小的时候就知道，我的舅公尼古拉斯·B 是荣誉军团骑士，还得过波兰的英勇十字勋章——Virtuti Militari（波兰军事英勇勋章）。知晓这些光荣事迹，曾在我心中激起一片景仰的崇敬；然而，能在意念中为我凝聚起他这个人的分量与意义的，却不是那份情感，纵然它那样强烈。压倒它的，是另一种复杂的印象——敬畏、怜悯，与骇异。尼古拉斯·B 舅公在我心中，始终是那个不幸而可怜（却又英勇）的生灵——很久很久以前，他吃过一条狗。

===Original===
It is a good forty years since I heard the tale, and the effect has not worn off yet. I believe this is the very first, say, realistic, story I heard in my life; but all the same I don’t know why I should have been so frightfully impressed. Of course I know what our village dogs look like﻿—but still﻿ ﻿… No! At this very day, recalling the horror and compassion of my childhood, I ask myself whether I am right in disclosing to a cold and fastidious world that awful episode in the family history. I ask myself﻿—is it right?﻿—especially as the B. family had always been honourably known in a wide countryside for the delicacy of their tastes in the matter of eating and drinking. But upon the whole, and considering that this gastronomical degradation overtaking a gallant young officer lies really at the door of the Great Napoleon, I think that to cover it up by silence would be an exaggeration of literary restraint. Let the truth stand here. The responsibility rests with the Man of St. Helena in view of his deplorable levity in the conduct of the Russian campaign. It was during the memorable retreat from Moscow that Mr. Nicholas B., in company of two brother officers﻿—as to whose morality and natural refinement I know nothing﻿—bagged a dog on the outskirts of a village and subsequently devoured him. As far as I can remember the weapon used was a cavalry sabre, and the issue of the sporting episode was rather more of a matter of life and death than if it had been an encounter with a tiger. A picket of Cossacks was sleeping in that village lost in the depths of the great Lithuanian forest. The three sportsmen had observed them from a hiding-place making themselves very much at home amongst the huts just before the early winter darkness set in at four o’clock. They had observed them with disgust and perhaps with despair. Late in the night the rash counsels of hunger overcame the dictates of prudence. Crawling through the snow they crept up to the fence of dry branches which generally encloses a village in that part of Lithuania. What they expected to get and in what manner, and whether this expectation was worth the risk, goodness only knows. However, these Cossack parties, in most cases wandering without an officer, were known to guard themselves badly and often not at all. In addition, the village lying at a great distance from the line of French retreat, they could not suspect the presence of stragglers from the Grand Army. The three officers had strayed away in a blizzard from the main column and had been lost for days in the woods, which explains sufficiently the terrible straits to which they were reduced. Their plan was to try and attract the attention of the peasants in that one of the huts which was nearest to the enclosure; but as they were preparing to venture into the very jaws of the lion, so to speak, a dog (it is mighty strange that there was but one), a creature quite as formidable under the circumstances as a lion, began to bark on the other side of the fence.﻿ ﻿…

At this stage of the narrative, which I heard many times (by request) from the lips of Captain Nicholas B.’s sister-in-law, my grandmother, I used to tremble with excitement.

===Chinese===
这故事我听见至今，足足有四十年了，那效力却还没有消散。我相信，这该是我平生听到的第一篇——姑且这么说吧——写实主义故事；可我还是不明白，自己为什么竟被吓得那样厉害。我当然知道我们村里的狗长什么模样——可还是……不！直到今天，一回想起童年时代的那份恐怖与怜悯，我仍不免自问：把家史中那段可怕的插曲，袒露给一个冷淡而挑剔的世界，究竟对不对。我自问——这样妥当吗？——尤其 B 家在这一方广漠的乡野之间，向来是以饮食趣味的精细而清誉著称的。但总的说来，考虑到降临在一位英武青年军官头上的这场口腹上的堕落，实在应该记在拿破仑大帝的账上，我想，若用沉默将它掩盖起来，未免是文学节制的一种过度发挥。就让真相立在此处吧。责任在圣赫勒拿岛上的那个人——谁让他在俄国战事的经营上，有那样可叹的轻率。正是在那次从莫斯科撤退的难忘岁月里，尼古拉斯·B 舅公伙同两名军官同僚——这两人的道德水准与天生雅致如何，我无从知晓——在村口猎获了一条狗，随后把它吃掉了。据我记忆所及，使用的兵器是一把骑兵马刀，而这场游猎插曲的生死攸关程度，比起同一只老虎遭遇，倒更有过之。一小队哥萨克哨兵，正睡在那个村子里——村子迷失在大立陶宛森林的深处。下午四点，早临的冬日暮色四合之前，三位猎人从一处藏身之地望见了他们：哥萨克们正在茅舍之间大模大样地安营扎寨，俨然把那里当成了家。他们望着，心里是厌恶，或许还有绝望。夜深了，饥饿那些轻率的进言，终于压倒了审慎的训诫。他们在雪里匍匐爬行，一点点挪近那道干树枝扎成的栅栏——在立陶宛那一带，村子外围照例都圈着这样一道栅栏。他们指望弄到什么、打算怎么弄、这份指望值不值得冒这个险，只有天知道。不过，这种哥萨克小队，大多是无军官统带地四处游荡，出了名地疏于防范，甚至全不设防。再者，村子离法军撤退的路线极远，他们怎么也料想不到，会有大军团掉队的散兵摸到这里。三位军官是在一场暴风雪里脱离了主力纵队，在林子里迷失了好几天，这就足以说明他们被逼到了怎样可怕的绝境。他们的计划，是设法引起离栅栏最近的那座茅舍里乡民的注意；可是，正当他们准备——不妨这么说吧——往狮子口里送的时候，一条狗（只有一条，这真是怪事一桩），一个在此情形下与狮子同样可畏的生灵，在栅栏那一面吠了起来。……

每到故事的这个关节，我总要兴奋得发抖——这故事我听过许多遍（多半是应我之请），出自我的外祖母、也就是尼古拉斯·B 舅公兄弟的妻子之口。

===Original===
The dog barked. And if he had done no more than bark, three officers of the Great Napoleon’s army would have perished honourably on the points of Cossacks’ lances, or perchance escaping the chase would have died decently of starvation. But before they had time to think of running away, that fatal and revolting dog, being carried away by the excess of his zeal, dashed out through a gap in the fence. He dashed out and died. His head, I understand, was severed at one blow from his body. I understand also that later on, within the gloomy solitudes of the snow-laden woods, when, in a sheltering hollow, a fire had been lit by the party, the condition of the quarry was discovered to be distinctly unsatisfactory. It was not thin﻿—on the contrary, it seemed unhealthily obese; its skin showed bare patches of an unpleasant character. However, they had not killed that dog for the sake of the pelt. He was large.﻿ ﻿… He was eaten.﻿ ﻿… The rest is silence.﻿ ﻿… A silence in which a small boy shudders and says firmly:

===Chinese===
狗吠了。倘若它仅仅止于吠，那么拿破仑大军的三个军官，本可以光荣地殒命在哥萨克长矛的锋尖上，或者侥幸逃过追捕，体面地饿死了事。可是不等他们来得及想到逃跑，那条致命而可憎的狗，已被自己过量的热忱冲昏了头，从栅栏的一道缺口蹿了出来。它蹿出来，死了。据我所知，它的脑袋被一刀从躯体上斩了下来。我还知道，随后，在积雪林木那阴郁的幽深处，当一行人在一处背风的洼地点起火堆时，人们发现猎物的状况实在难以令人满意。它并不瘦——恰恰相反，它肥胖得病态；皮上还露出一块块令人不快的秃斑。不过，他们杀那条狗，本来就不是贪图那张皮。它个头大。……它被吃了。……余下的便是沉默。……一片沉默，其中一个小男孩打着寒噤，斩钉截铁地说：

===Original===
“I could not have eaten that dog.”

And his grandmother remarks with a smile:

“Perhaps you don’t know what it is to be hungry.”

===Chinese===
「我可吃不下那条狗。」

而他的外祖母微笑着说：

「也许你还不知道，挨饿是什么滋味。」

===Original===
I have learned something of it since. Not that I have been reduced to eat dog. I have fed on the emblematical animal, which, in the language of the volatile Gauls, is called *la vache enragée*; I have lived on ancient salt junk, I know the taste of shark, of trepang, of snake, of nondescript dishes containing things without a name﻿—but of the Lithuanian village dog never! I wish it to be distinctly understood that it is not I, but my granduncle Nicholas, of the Polish landed gentry, *Chevalier de la Légion d’Honneur*, etc. etc., who, in his young days, had eaten the Lithuanian dog.

I wish he had not. The childish horror of the deed clings absurdly to the grizzled man. I am perfectly helpless against it. Still, if he really had to, let us charitably remember that he had eaten him on active service, while bearing up bravely against the greatest military disaster of modern history, and, in a manner, for the sake of his country. He had eaten him to appease his hunger, no doubt, but also for the sake of an unappeasable and patriotic desire, in the glow of a great faith that lives still, and in the pursuit of a great illusion kindled like a false beacon by a great man to lead astray the effort of a brave nation.

*Pro patria!*

===Chinese===
这滋味，我后来领教过一些。倒不是说我沦落到吃过狗肉。我吃过那种象征性的动物——在性情轻浮躁动的高卢人的语言里，它叫作 *la vache enragée*（发狂的母牛）；我靠陈年的咸腌牛肉过过活，我尝过鲨鱼肉、海参、蛇肉的味道，尝过种种说不出名目的杂烩，里头的东西连名字也没有——可立陶宛村庄的狗，从来没有！我要人们确切地明白：年轻时吃过那条立陶宛狗的，不是我，而是我的舅公尼古拉斯——波兰乡绅、*Chevalier de la Légion d'Honneur*（荣誉军团骑士），等等，等等。

我倒希望他没有吃过。那桩事变的童年恐怖，荒唐地粘在这个头发斑白的男人身上，我对它毫无办法。不过，倘若他当真非吃不可，那就让我们心怀慈悲地记住：他是在服役期间吃的，一边还在勇敢地承受着现代史上最惨重的军事灾难，而且，在某种意义上，是为了他的祖国。毫无疑问，他吃它是为了平息饥饿，但也是为了那一份无法平息的爱国热望，是在一团至今犹存的大信念的光焰里，是在追逐一个大幻觉的途中——那幻觉像一座假灯塔，由一个伟人点燃，专为把一个勇敢民族的力气引入歧途。

*Pro patria!*（为了祖国！）

===Original===
Looked at in that light it appears a sweet and decorous meal.

And looked at in the same light, my own diet of *la vache enragée* appears a fatuous and extravagant form of self-indulgence; for why should I, the son of a land which such men as these have turned up with their ploughshares and bedewed with their blood, undertake the pursuit of fantastic meals of salt junk and hard tack upon the wide seas? On the kindest view it seems an unanswerable question. Alas! I have the conviction that there are men of unstained rectitude who are ready to murmur scornfully the word desertion. Thus the taste of innocent adventure may be made bitter to the palate. The part of the inexplicable should be allowed for in appraising the conduct of men in a world where no explanation is final. No charge of faithlessness ought to be lightly uttered. The appearances of this perishable life are deceptive like everything that falls under the judgment of our imperfect senses. The inner voice may remain true enough in its secret counsel. The fidelity to a special tradition may last through the events of an unrelated existence, following faithfully, too, the traced way of an inexplicable impulse.

===Chinese===
从这个光里看去，那便是一顿甜美而得体的饭食了。

同样从这个光里看去，我自己那一份 *la vache enragée* 的口粮，便显得是一种蠢愚而铺张的放纵了；因为，我既是这样一个国度的儿子——这样的男人们，曾用犁铧翻起她的泥土，用他们的血润泽过她——我又何必跑到茫茫大海上去，追逐咸肉硬饼的奇幻饭食呢？往最宽厚处想，这也像是个无法回答的问题。唉！我确信，世上有些品行无瑕的正人君子，随时准备轻蔑地吐出「逃亡」二字。于是，无辜冒险的滋味，也可能在舌根上变苦。在一个没有任何解释是终极解释的世界上，评判人们的行止时，总该给「无可解释」留出一份分量。背信的指控，绝不该轻易出口。这速朽生命的种种表象，样样都是骗人的，一如落入我们不完美感官评判之下的一切事物。内心的声音，在它隐秘的参赞里，或许始终是忠实的。对一种独特传统的忠贞，也可能贯穿一段与之毫不相干的人生的种种事变而长存——并且照样忠实，追随着一道莫名冲动画出的路径。

===Original===
It would take too long to explain the intimate alliance of contradictions in human nature which makes love itself wear at times the desperate shape of betrayal. And perhaps there is no possible explanation. Indulgence﻿—as somebody said﻿—is the most intelligent of all the virtues. I venture to think that it is one of the least common, if not the most uncommon of all. I would not imply by this that men are foolish﻿—or even most men. Far from it. The barber and the priest, backed by the whole opinion of the village, condemned justly the conduct of the ingenious hidalgo, who, sallying forth from his native place, broke the head of the muleteer, put to death a flock of inoffensive sheep, and went through very doleful experiences in a certain stable. God forbid that an unworthy churl should escape merited censure by hanging on to the stirrup-leather of the sublime caballero. His was a very noble, a very unselfish fantasy, fit for nothing except to raise the envy of baser mortals. But there is more than one aspect to the charm of that exalted and dangerous figure. He, too, had his frailties. After reading so many romances he desired naively to escape with his very body from the intolerable reality of things. He wished to meet eye to eye the valorous giant Brandabarbaran, Lord of Arabia, whose armour is made of the skin of a dragon, and whose shield, strapped to his arm, is the gate of a fortified city. O amiable and natural weakness! O blessed simplicity of a gentle heart without guile! Who would not succumb to such a consoling temptation? Nevertheless it was a form of self-indulgence, and the ingenious hidalgo of La Mancha was not a good citizen. The priest and the barber were not unreasonable in their strictures. Without going so far as the old King Louis-Philippe, who used to say in his exile, “The people are never in fault”﻿—one may admit that there must be some righteousness in the assent of a whole village. Mad! Mad! He who kept in pious meditation the ritual vigil-of-arms by the well of an inn and knelt reverently to be knighted at daybreak by the fat, sly rogue of a landlord, has come very near perfection. He rides forth, his head encircled by a halo﻿—the patron saint of all lives spoiled or saved by the irresistible grace of imagination. But he was not a good citizen.

===Chinese===
人性中诸般矛盾的亲密联盟，何以会使爱本身有时竟戴上背叛那副孤注一掷的面目——解释起来，未免太费笔墨。而且，或许也根本无从解释。宽恕——正如有人说过——是一切美德中最明智的一种。我斗胆以为，它即便算不上最稀有，也是一切美德里最少见的一种。我这样说，并不是暗指人是愚蠢的——甚至连大多数人都不是。远非如此。理发师和神父，有全村舆论撑腰，义正词严地谴责了那位奇情异想的乡绅的行径：他从家乡贸然出击，打破了骡夫的头，宰了一群无辜的羊，还在某个马厩里经历了极凄惨的苦楚。上帝不容哪个不配的村夫，攀住那位崇高骑士的马镫皮带，便逃脱应得的訾议。他的幻想极高贵、极无私，除了惹起凡庸之辈的嫉羡之外，别无用处。可是，那个崇高而危险的形象，其魅力不止一面。他也有他的软弱。读了那么多骑士传奇之后，他天真地想凭自己这副血肉之躯，逃脱事物那无可忍受的现实。他想同英勇的巨人、阿拉伯的君主布兰达巴尔巴兰（Brandabarbaran）当面相对——那巨人的铠甲是龙皮做的，绑在臂上的盾牌，是一座设防城池的大门。啊，可爱而天然的人性弱点！啊，温柔而无机心的心灵那有福的单纯！谁能不拜倒在这样一个慰藉性的诱惑面前呢？然而，这终究是一种自我放纵，而拉曼却（La Mancha）那位奇情异想的乡绅，并不是一个好公民。神父和理发师的讥评，并非全无道理。我们不必像老王路易-菲利普（Louis-Philippe）走得那样远——他流亡时常说，「人民是永远不会错的」——但也不妨承认，一村人的首肯里，总该有几分公道。疯了！疯了！那位在客店的井边虔敬地行了授封前守夜祷告的仪式、又在破晓时恭恭敬敬跪下、受那个肥头大耳而奸猾的店主册封为骑士的人，实在已近乎完美。他驰骋而出，头顶罩着一轮光环——他是所有被想象那不可抗拒的恩宠毁掉或拯救的人生的主保圣人。但他不是个好公民。

===Original===
Perhaps that and nothing else was meant by the well-remembered exclamation of my tutor.

It was in the jolly year 1873, the very last year in which I have had a jolly holiday. There have been idle years afterwards, jolly enough in a way and not altogether without their lesson, but this year of which I speak was the year of my last schoolboy holiday. There are other reasons why I should remember that year, but they are too long to state formally in this place. Moreover, they have nothing to do with that holiday. What has to do with the holiday is that before the day on which the remark was made we had seen Vienna, the Upper Danube, Munich, the Falls of the Rhine, the Lake of Constance﻿—in fact it was a memorable holiday of travel. Of late we had been tramping slowly up the Valley of the Reuss. It was a delightful time. It was much more like a stroll than a tramp. Landing from a Lake of Lucerne steamer in Fluellen, we found ourselves at the end of the second day, with the dusk overtaking our leisurely footsteps, a little way beyond Hospenthal. This is not the day on which the remark was made: in the shadows of the deep valley and with the habitations of men left some way behind, our thoughts ran not upon the ethics of conduct but upon the simpler human problem of shelter and food. There did not seem anything of the kind in sight, and we were thinking of turning back when suddenly at a bend of the road we came upon a building, ghostly in the twilight.

===Chinese===
我的家庭教师那句记忆犹新的感叹，或许正是指着这个，而不是别的什么。

那是快活的 1873 年，我过上一个快活假期的最后一年。此后也有过闲散的年头，从某种意义上说也够快活，并且并非全无教益，但我所说的这一年，是我学生时代的最后一个假期。我该记住那一年，还有别的缘故，只是说来话长，不宜在此正式铺陈。况且，那些事与那个假期毫不相干。与假期相干的是：在那句议论出口之前，我们已经看过了维也纳（Vienna）、多瑙河上游、慕尼黑（Munich）、莱茵瀑布、博登湖（the Lake of Constance）——事实上，那是一次难忘的旅行假期。近来，我们正沿着罗伊斯河谷（the Valley of the Reuss）缓缓徒步上行。那是一段惬意的时光。与其说是跋涉，不如说是闲庭信步。从卢塞恩湖（the Lake of Lucerne）的轮船在弗吕伦（Fluellen）登岸之后，到第二天日暮时分，暮色追上了我们从容的脚步，我们已走到霍斯彭塔尔（Hospenthal）过去不多远的地方。说出那句议论的，并不是这一天：在深谷的阴影里，人身居所已被抛下一程，我们心里盘算的不是行止的伦理，而是投宿与果腹这个更简单的人生问题。视野之内，似乎并无此类去处，我们正打算折返，忽然，在道路一个转弯处，撞见了一座建筑，在暮色里幽灵似的立着。

===Original===
At that time the work on the St. Gothard Tunnel was going on, and that magnificent enterprise of burrowing was directly responsible for the unexpected building, standing all alone upon the very roots of the mountains. It was long, though not big at all; it was low; it was built of boards, without ornamentation, in barrack hut style, with the white window-frames quite flush with the yellow face of its plain front. And yet it was an hotel; it had even a name which I have forgotten. But there was no gold-laced doorkeeper at its humble door. A plain but vigorous servant-girl answered our inquiries, then a man and woman who owned the place appeared. It was clear that no travellers were expected, or perhaps even desired, in this strange hostelry, which in its severe style resembled the house which surmounts the unseaworthy-looking hulls of the toy Noah’s Arks, the universal possession of European childhood. However, its roof was not hinged and it was not full to the brim of slab-sided and painted animals of wood. Even the live tourist animal was nowhere in evidence. We had something to eat in a long, narrow room at one end of a long, narrow table, which, to my tired perception and to my sleepy eyes, seemed as if it would tilt up like a seesaw plank, since there was no one at the other end to balance it against our two dusty and travel-stained figures. Then we hastened upstairs to bed in a room smelling of pine planks, and I was fast asleep before my head touched the pillow.

===Chinese===
那时，圣哥达隧道（the St. Gothard Tunnel）的工程正在进行，正是那项宏伟的掘进事业，直接造出了这幢出人意料的建筑——它孤零零地立在群山的根脚上。它很长，却一点也说不上大；它低矮；它是木板造的，不加装饰，兵棚式的风格，白色的窗框与素朴正面的黄墙面齐平。然而，它是一家旅店；它甚至还有个名字，只是我已经忘了。只是它那扇寒酸的门前，并没有饰金绦的门房应客。回答我们问询的，是一个相貌平常却精力充沛的使女，随后，这地方的一男一女两位主人也露了面。很明显，这家古怪的客舍并不指望有旅客来，甚至未必欢迎——论它那副严峻的风格，倒像玩具诺亚方舟顶上驮着的那座小屋——那方舟是全欧童年人手一份的玩意儿，船壳看上去一副经不起风浪的样子。不过，这里的屋顶不是用铰链装合的，也没有塞满一只只扁身板、上了彩漆的木制走兽。就连活的「旅客动物」，也一只不见踪影。我们在一间狭长的屋子里、一张狭长桌子的一端吃了点东西，以我疲惫的知觉和困倦的眼睛看来，那桌子活像一块跷跷板，随时会翘起来——因为另一端空无一人，没有谁为我们两个风尘仆仆、灰头土脸的身影配重。随后我们赶紧上楼睡觉，进了一间散发着松木板气味的房间，头还没沾枕头，我已沉沉睡去。

===Original===
In the morning my tutor (he was a student of the Krakow University) woke me up early, and as we were dressing remarked: “There seems to be a lot of people staying in this hotel. I have heard a noise of talking up till eleven o’clock.” This statement surprised me; I had heard no noise whatever, having slept like a top.

We went downstairs into the long and narrow dining-room with its long and narrow table. There were two rows of plates on it. At one of the many uncurtained windows stood a tall, bony man with a bald head set off by a bunch of black hair above each ear and with a long black beard. He glanced up from the paper he was reading and seemed genuinely astonished at our intrusion. By-and-by more men came in. Not one of them looked like a tourist. Not a single woman appeared. These men seemed to know each other with some intimacy, but I cannot say they were a very talkative lot. The bald-headed man sat down gravely at the head of the table. It all had the air of a family party. By-and-by, from one of the vigorous servant-girls in national costume, we discovered that the place was really a boardinghouse for some English engineers engaged at the works of the St. Gothard Tunnel; and I could listen my fill to the sounds of the English language, as far as it is used at a breakfast-table by men who do not believe in wasting many words on the mere amenities of life.

This was my first contact with British mankind apart from the tourist kind seen in the hotels of Zurich and Lucerne﻿—the kind which has no real existence in a workaday world. I know now that the bald-headed man spoke with a strong Scotch accent. I have met many of his kind since, both ashore and afloat. The second engineer of the steamer *Mavis*, for instance, ought to have been his twin brother. I cannot help thinking that he really was, though for some reasons of his own he assured me that he never had a twin brother. Anyway, the deliberate, bald-headed Scot with the coal-black beard appeared to my boyish eyes a very romantic and mysterious person.

===Chinese===
早晨，我的家庭教师（他是克拉科夫大学的学生）一早把我叫醒，我们穿衣的时候，他说道：「这家旅店住的人好像不少。我一直听见说话声，闹到十一点钟。」这话让我吃了一惊；我什么动静也没有听见，睡得极沉。

我们下楼，进了那间狭长的餐室，里面摆着那张狭长的桌子。桌上排着两行盘子。在一扇扇没挂窗帘的窗子中的一扇跟前，站着一个瘦高多骨的男人，秃头顶，两只耳朵上方各留着一撮黑发作为点缀，还蓄着一把长长的黑胡须。他从正读着的报纸上抬起眼来，看见我们闯入，似乎当真心吃了一惊。不多会儿，又进来一些男人。没有一个人像游客。一个女人也没有出现。这些人彼此似乎相当熟稔，但我不能说他们是一伙多话的人。秃头男人庄严地在桌子上首坐了下来。整个场面，自有一家子聚会的气象。又过了一会儿，从其中一位身穿民族服装的精壮使女嘴里，我们弄明白了：这地方其实是一所供膳宿舍，住着几位在圣哥达隧道工程上做事的英国工程师；于是我得以把英语听了个够——就早餐桌上所用的那点英语而言，这些人可不肯在人生的寻常客套上多费唇舌。

这是我同不列颠人类的第一次接触——不同于苏黎世（Zurich）和卢塞恩的旅馆里见到的那类游客——那一类人，在一个实干的世界里，其实并不真实存在。如今我知道，那秃头男人说话带一口浓重的苏格兰口音。此后我在岸上、在海上，都遇到过许多他这一类人。比如「梅维斯号」（*Mavis*）轮船的二管轮，本该是他的孪生兄弟。我不由得不认为他确实是，尽管出于他自己的一些缘由，他向我保证过，他从来没有过孪生兄弟。反正，这位留着炭黑胡须、慢条斯理的秃头苏格兰人，在我少年的眼睛里，是个极富传奇色彩、极神秘的人物。

===Original===
We slipped out unnoticed. Our mapped-out route led over the Furca Pass towards the Rhône Glacier, with the further intention of following down the trend of the Häsli Valley. The sun was already declining when we found ourselves on the top of the pass, and the remark alluded to was presently uttered.

We sat down by the side of the road to continue the argument begun half a mile or so before. I am certain it was an argument because I remember perfectly how my tutor argued and how without the power of reply I listened with my eyes fixed obstinately on the ground. A stir on the road made me look up﻿—and then I saw my unforgettable Englishman. There are acquaintances of later years, familiars, shipmates, whom I remember less clearly. He marched rapidly towards the east (attended by a hangdog Swiss guide) with the mien of an ardent and fearless traveller. He was clad in a knickerbocker suit, but as at the same time he wore short socks under his laced boots, for reasons which, whether hygienic or conscientious, were surely imaginative, his calves exposed to the public gaze and to the tonic air of high altitudes, dazzled the beholder by the splendour of their marble-like condition and their rich tone of young ivory. He was the leader of a small caravan. The light of a headlong, exalted satisfaction with the world of men and the scenery of mountains illumined his clean-cut, very red face, his short, silver-white whiskers, his innocently eager and triumphant eyes. In passing he cast a glance of kindly curiosity and a friendly gleam of big, sound, shiny teeth towards the man and the boy sitting like dusty tramps by the roadside, with a modest knapsack lying at their feet. His white calves twinkled sturdily, the uncouth Swiss guide with a surly mouth stalked like an unwilling bear at his elbow; a small train of three mules followed in single file the lead of this inspiring enthusiast. Two ladies rode past one behind the other, but from the way they sat I saw only their calm, uniform backs, and the long ends of blue veils hanging behind far down over their identical hat-brims. His two daughters, surely. An industrious luggage-mule, with unstarched ears and guarded by a slouching, sallow driver, brought up the rear. My tutor, after pausing for a look and a faint smile, resumed his earnest argument.

===Chinese===
我们悄悄溜了出去，谁也没有留意。我们图定好的路线，是翻越富尔卡山口（the Furca Pass）往罗讷冰川（the Rhône Glacier）去，还打算顺道再沿着黑斯利河谷（the Häsli Valley）的走向一路下行。待我们站上山口顶巅时，日头已经偏西，前面提到的那句议论，不久便出口了。

我们在路旁坐下，继续大约半英里之前开始的那场辩论。我可以肯定那是一场辩论，因为我清清楚楚记得，我的家庭教师怎样滔滔陈词，而我怎样毫无还嘴之力，两眼执拗地盯着地面听着。路上一阵动静使我抬起头来——于是我看见了我那个难以忘怀的英国人。晚年的相识、密友、船上的同僚，有多少人，我记得还没有他清楚。他迈着热忱无畏的旅行家的气派，快步向东行进（身后跟着个垂头丧气的瑞士向导）。他穿一身灯笼裤套装，可同时又在校带靴子里配了短袜，其缘故不管是出于卫生还是出于良心，肯定都是出于想象；他那两条小腿袒露在众目睽睽和高地爽利的空气里，以它们大理石般的光洁品相和新象牙般的润泽色泽，晃得观者眼花。他是一支小小商队的首领。一种对人间世与山峦景色的、一往无前的昂扬得意，照亮了他那轮廓分明、红得发紫的脸膛、他那银白的短颊须、他那双天真热切而得意扬扬的眼睛。经过时，他朝路旁那两个满身尘土、流浪汉似地坐着的一老一少，投来善意好奇的一瞥，还友善地一闪一口又大又结实、亮光闪闪的牙；那一老一少，脚边搁着一只不大的背囊。他的两条白小腿稳健地闪着微光；那个嘴撅得老高、粗野的瑞士向导，像一头老大不情愿的熊，闷声跟在他肘边；一小列三头骡子，鱼贯追随在这位振奋人心的热心家身后。两位女士一前一后骑马经过，但从她们的坐姿，我只看见两副同样沉静的背影，和蓝色面纱长长的尾端，从一模一样的帽檐后直垂下去老远。想必是他的两个女儿。一头勤劳的行李骡子断后——两只耳朵软塌塌的，像没浆过似的——由一个没精打采、面色萎黄的赶骡人看守着。我的家庭教师停了停，望了一眼，淡淡一笑，又接着进行他那恳切的辩论。

===Original===
I tell you it was a memorable year! One does not meet such an Englishman twice in a lifetime. Was he in the mystic ordering of common events the ambassador of my future, sent out to turn the scale at a critical moment on the top of an Alpine pass, with the peaks of the Bernese Oberland for mute and solemn witnesses? His glance, his smile, the unextinguishable and comic ardour of his striving-forward appearance helped me to pull myself together. It must be stated that on that day and in the exhilarating atmosphere of that elevated spot I had been feeling utterly crushed. It was the year in which I had first spoken aloud of my desire to go to sea. At first, like those sounds that, ranging outside the scale to which men’s ears are attuned, remain inaudible to our sense of hearing, this declaration passed unperceived. It was as if it had not been. Later on, by trying various tones, I managed to arouse here and there a surprised momentary attention﻿—the “What was that funny noise?” sort of inquiry. Later on it was﻿—“Did you hear what that boy said? What an extraordinary outbreak!” Presently a wave of scandalised astonishment (it could not have been greater if I had announced the intention of entering a Carthusian monastery) ebbing out of the educational and academical town of Krakow spread itself over several provinces. It spread itself shallow but far-reaching. It stirred up a mass of remonstrance, indignation, pitying wonder, bitter irony and downright chaff. I could hardly breathe under its weight, and certainly had no words for an answer. People wondered what Mr. T. B. would do now with his worrying nephew and, I dare say, hoped kindly that he would make short work of my nonsense.

===Chinese===
我跟你说，那真是个难忘的年头！这样的英国人，一辈子遇不上第二个。在寻常事变那种神秘的编排里，他会不会正是我未来的使者，被派来在阿尔卑斯山口的顶上、在一个紧要关头扭转乾坤，让伯尔尼高地（the Bernese Oberland）的群峰做沉默而庄严的见证？他的一瞥，他的一笑，他那种一往无前、又滑稽又扑不灭的热忱，帮我重新振作了起来。必须说明，那一天，在那处高旷之地令人振奋的空气里，我一直觉得自己被彻底压垮了。正是在那一年，我头一回大声说出了我要去海上的愿望。起初，这宣言就像那些逸出人耳惯常音域之外的声音，我们的听觉捕捉不到，它过去了，无人察觉。仿佛根本没有说过。后来，我换着各种调门再试，才零零星星唤起片刻的惊愕注意——「刚才那声怪响是什么？」式的追问。再后来，就成了——「听见那孩子说的话了吗？真是语出惊人！」不久，一股骇怪愤慨的浪潮（哪怕我宣布要进加尔都西会修道院，也未必更耸动）涌出克拉科夫那座教育与学术之城，漫过了好几省。它漫得不深，却漫得极远。它搅起一大堆的谏劝、义愤、怜惜的惊怪、刻毒的讥诮和赤裸裸的取笑。我在它的重压下几乎喘不过气来，当然也拿不出半句回答。人们纳闷，T. B. 先生如今要拿他这个惹是生非的外甥怎么办；我敢说，他们还一片好心地盼着他快刀斩乱麻，了结我的胡闹。

===Original===
What he did was to come down all the way from Ukraine to have it out with me and to judge by himself, unprejudiced, impartial and just, taking his stand on the ground of wisdom and affection. As far as is possible for a boy whose power of expression is still unformed, I opened the secret of my thoughts to him and he in return allowed me a glimpse into his mind and heart; the first glimpse of an inexhaustible and noble treasure of clear thought and warm feeling, which through life was to be mine to draw upon with a never-deceived love and confidence. Practically, after several exhaustive conversations, he concluded that he would not have me later on reproach him for having spoiled my life by an unconditional opposition. But I must take time for serious reflection. And I must not only think of myself but of others; weigh the claims of affection and conscience against my own sincerity of purpose. “Think well what it all means in the larger issues, my boy,” he exhorted me finally with special friendliness. “And meantime try to get the best place you can at the yearly examinations.”

The scholastic year came to an end. I took a fairly good place at the exams, which for me (for certain reasons) happened to be a more difficult task than for other boys. In that respect I could enter with a good conscience upon that holiday which was like a long visit *pour prendre congé* of the mainland of old Europe I was to see so little of for the next four-and-twenty years. Such, however, was not the avowed purpose of that tour. It was rather, I suspect, planned in order to distract and occupy my thoughts in other directions. Nothing had been said for months of my going to sea. But my attachment to my young tutor and his influence over me were so well known that he must have received a confidential mission to talk me out of my romantic folly. It was an excellently appropriate arrangement, as neither he nor I had ever had a single glimpse of the sea in our lives. That was to come by-and-by for both of us in Venice, from the outer shore of Lido. Meantime he had taken his mission to heart so well that I began to feel crushed before we reached Zurich. He argued in railway trains, in lake steamboats, he had argued away for me the obligatory sunrise on the Righi, by Jove! Of his devotion to his unworthy pupil there can be no doubt. He had proved it already by two years of unremitting and arduous care. I could not hate him. But he had been crushing me slowly, and when he started to argue on the top of the Furca Pass he was perhaps nearer a success than either he or I imagined. I listened to him in despairing silence, feeling that ghostly, unrealised and desired sea of my dreams escape from the unnerved grip of my will.

===Chinese===
他的做法，是不辞路远，从乌克兰专程下来，同我当面把话讲清，亲自判断——不带成见，不偏不倚，公正无私，立足于智慧与慈爱的地面上。我尽一个措辞之力尚未成形的男孩所能，向他敞开了我心里的秘密；作为回报，他也让我窥见了他的心智与心肠——那是第一瞥，瞥见一座清明思想与温热情感的不竭而高贵的宝库；此后一生，我从中支取，怀着一份从未被辜负过的爱与信赖。实际的结果是，经过几次穷根究底的谈话，他断定：他不愿我日后责备他，说一场不留余地的反对毁了我的一生。但我必须拿出时间，认真反省。我不但要想自己，也要想别人；把慈爱与良心的吁求，放到我自己志向的真诚对面去掂量。「孩子，好好想想，这一切在更大的利害上意味着什么，」他最后格外亲切地勉励我，「与此同时，年度考试上，能考多好就考多好。」

学年结束了。考试，我考取了一个相当不错的名次——对我而言（出于某些缘故），这碰巧是件比别的男孩更难的事。在这一点上，我总算能够问心无愧地开始那个假期了；那假期像是一次长长的拜别——*pour prendre congé*（道别）——向我此后二十四年里难得再见的旧欧罗巴大陆拜别。不过，那并不是那次出游公开声明的目的。我疑心，它册宁是为了把我的心思引开、占住，才筹划出来的。一连几个月，去海上这件事，再没有人提起。但我对我的年轻家庭教师的依恋、他对我的影响，早已人尽皆知，于是他必定是领了一桩不便明言的使命，来劝我打消那浪漫的痴念。这安排再妥帖不过，因为我们俩这辈子，都还没有望见过一眼海。那要等日后到了威尼斯（Venice），从利多（Lido）岛外侧的岸边，再一并兑现。而当时，他把使命往心里放得那样实，还没到苏黎世，我就开始觉得被压垮了。他在火车上辩论，在湖上轮船里辩论，天哪，他连里吉山（Righi）那场不容分说的日出，都替我辩没了！他对这个不配的学生尽职尽责，这一点毫无疑问：他用整整两年毫不懈怠、备极辛劳的照拂，早已证明了这一点。我恨不起他来。但他一直在慢慢地压碎我，等他在富尔卡山口顶上重新开讲的时候，他离成功，或许比我们俩谁想象的都近。我听着，绝望地沉默着，只觉得我梦里那片幽灵般的、未曾实现而心向往之的海，正从我意志松了劲的掌握里，一寸寸溜走。

===Original===
The enthusiastic old Englishman had passed﻿—and the argument went on. What reward could I expect from such a life at the end of my years, either in ambition, honour or conscience? An unanswerable question. But I felt no longer crushed. Then our eyes met and a genuine emotion was visible in his as well as in mine. The end came all at once. He picked up the knapsack suddenly and got on to his feet.

“You are an incorrigible, hopeless Don Quixote. That’s what you are.”

===Chinese===
那位热忱的老英国人走过去了——辩论还在继续。这样的一生，到头来我能指望什么报偿，无论在抱负上、名誉上还是良心上？一个无法回答的问题。但我不再觉得被压垮了。随后，我们的目光相遇了，他的眼里和我的一样，都看得见一种真实的激动。结局来得突如其来。他猛地抓起背囊，站起身来。

「你是个不可救药、无药可改的堂吉诃德（Don Quixote）。你就是这么个人。」

===Original===
I was surprised. I was only fifteen and did not know what he meant exactly. But I felt vaguely flattered at the name of the immortal knight turning up in connection with my own folly, as some people would call it to my face. Alas! I don’t think there was anything to be proud of. Mine was not the stuff the protectors of forlorn damsels, the redressers of this world’s wrongs are made of; and my tutor was the man to know that best. Therein, in his indignation, he was superior to the barber and the priest when he flung at me an honoured name like a reproach.

I walked behind him for full five minutes; then without looking back he stopped. The shadows of distant peaks were lengthening over the Furca Pass. When I came up to him he turned to me and in full view of the Finster-Aarhorn, with his band of giant brothers rearing their monstrous heads against a brilliant sky, put his hand on my shoulder affectionately.

===Chinese===
我吃了一惊。我那年只有十五岁，并不确切明白他的意思。但不朽骑士的名号，居然同我自己的痴念（有些人当着我的面就这么叫它）扯上了关系，我隐约觉得与有荣焉。唉！我想，这里头实在没有什么可骄傲的。我这块料，不是做落难女子的保护人、人世间不平事的昭雪者的那号材料；而我的家庭教师，恰是最清楚这一点的人。在这一点上，在他那义愤里，他比理发师和神父高明——他把一个受人敬仰的名字，像一句申斥似的，劈面掷给了我。

我跟在他身后，走了足足五分钟；然后他没有回头，站住了。远处群峰的影子，正在富尔卡山口上越拉越长。等我赶上他，他朝我转过身来，当着芬斯特拉阿尔峰（the Finster-Aarhorn）——它带着一班巨人兄弟，把狰狞的头颅昂在明澈的天空下——把一只手亲昵地搭在我的肩上。

===Original===
“Well! That’s enough. We will have no more of it.”

And indeed there was no more question of my mysterious vocation between us. There was to be no more question of it at all, nowhere or with anyone. We began the descent of the Furca Pass conversing merrily. Eleven years later, month for month, I stood on Tower Hill on the steps of the St. Katherine’s Dockhouse, a master in the British Merchant Service. But the man who put his hand on my shoulder at the top of the Furca Pass was no longer living.

That very year of our travels he took his degree of the Philosophical Faculty﻿—and only then his true vocation declared itself. Obedient to the call, he entered at once upon the four-year course of the Medical Schools. A day came when, on the deck of a ship moored in Calcutta, I opened a letter telling me of the end of an enviable existence. He had made for himself a practice in some obscure little town of Austrian Galicia. And the letter went on to tell me how all the bereaved poor of the district, Christians and Jews alike, had mobbed the good doctor’s coffin with sobs and lamentations at the very gate of the cemetery.

How short his years and how clear his vision! What greater reward in ambition, honour and conscience could he have hoped to win for himself when, on the top of the Furca Pass, he bade me look well to the end of my opening life.

===Chinese===
「好了！够了。这事我们再也不提了。」

而的确，我们之间，再也没有提起过我那神秘的使命。此后根本不会再有人提起它了，无论在哪里，无论对谁。我们说说笑笑，开始下富尔卡山口。十一年后，月份分毫不差，我站在塔丘（Tower Hill）圣凯瑟琳船坞事务所（the St. Katherine's Dockhouse）的台阶上，成了英国商船队的一名船长。而在富尔卡山口顶上，把手搭在我肩上的那个人，已经不在人世了。

就在我们旅行的那一年，他考取了哲学系的学位——直到那时，他真正的天职才显露出来。他一应召唤，立刻投入了医学院四年制的课程。后来有一天，在泊于加尔各答（Calcutta）的一艘船的甲板上，我拆开一封信，得知一个令人艳羡的人生走到了尽头。他在奥地利加利西亚（Austrian Galicia）某座无名小镇上，自行开业行医。信里接着告诉我：全区失去了亲人的穷苦人，基督徒和犹太人一样，怎样在墓园的大门口，围住好医生的棺木，泣不成声，哀恸欲绝。

他的年华何其短促，他的目光何其澄澈！在富尔卡山口的顶上，他嘱咐我看顾好我这场初启人生的结局——彼时彼地，无论在抱负上、名誉上还是良心上，他还能为自己指望什么更大的报偿呢？

## III / III

===Original===
The devouring in a dismal forest of a luckless Lithuanian dog by my granduncle Nicholas B. in company of two other military and famished scarecrows, symbolised, to my childish imagination, the whole horror of the retreat from Moscow and the immorality of a conqueror’s ambition. An extreme distaste for that objectionable episode has tinged the views I hold as to the character and achievements of Napoleon the Great. I need not say that these are unfavourable. It was morally reprehensible for that great captain to induce a simple-minded Polish gentleman to eat dog by raising in his breast a false hope of national independence. It has been the fate of that credulous nation to starve for upwards of a hundred years on a diet of false hopes and﻿—well﻿—dog. It is, when one thinks of it, a singularly poisonous regimen. Some pride in the national constitution which has survived a long course of such dishes is really excusable. But enough of generalising. Returning to particulars, Mr. Nicholas B. confided to his sister-in-law (my grandmother) in his misanthropically laconic manner that this supper in the woods had been nearly “the death of him.” This is not surprising. What surprises me is that the story was ever heard of; for granduncle Nicholas differed in this from the generality of military men of Napoleon’s time (and perhaps of all time) that he did not like to talk of his campaigns, which began at Friedland and ended somewhere in the neighbourhood of Bar-le-Duc. His admiration of the great Emperor was unreserved in everything but expression. Like the religion of earnest men, it was too profound a sentiment to be displayed before a world of little faith. Apart from that he seemed as completely devoid of military anecdotes as though he had hardly ever seen a soldier in his life. Proud of his decorations earned before he was twenty-five, he refused to wear the ribbons at the buttonhole in the manner practised to this day in Europe and even was unwilling to display the insignia on festive occasions, as though he wished to conceal them in the fear of appearing boastful. “It is enough that I have them,” he used to mutter. In the course of thirty years they were seen on his breast only twice﻿—at an auspicious marriage in the family and at the funeral of an old friend. That the wedding which was thus honoured was not the wedding of my mother I learned only late in life, too late to bear a grudge against Mr. Nicholas B., who made amends at my birth by a long letter of congratulation containing the following prophecy: “He will see better times.” Even in his embittered heart there lived a hope. But he was not a true prophet.

===Chinese===
在那片阴郁的森林里，我的舅公尼古拉斯·B 伙同另外两名饿得形同稻草人的军人，吞吃了一条倒霉的立陶宛狗——在我童年的想象里，这一幕象征着莫斯科大撤退的全部恐怖，象征着一位征服者野心的全部不义。对那段可憎插曲的极端厌恶，给我对拿破仑大帝其人其业的看法染上了一层颜色。不必说，这颜色是不利的。那样一位伟大的统帅，竟在一个朴直的波兰绅士胸中唤起民族独立的虚假希望，诱得他吃起狗来，这在道义上是应受谴责的。这个轻信的民族，百余年来一直靠虚假的希望——还有，嗯，狗——充饥度日，这正是它的命。细想起来，那是一剂格外歹毒的养生食谱。这个民族的体格，经受了这么长一串这样的菜色居然熬了过来，为它感到几分自豪，实在情有可原。但泛论到此为止。回到具体：尼古拉斯·B 舅公曾以他厌世者式的三言两语，向他的弟媳（我的外祖母）吐露，林中那顿晚饭险些「要了他的老命」。这并不奇怪。令我奇怪的是，这故事居然还能传世；因为舅公尼古拉斯与拿破仑时代（或许还有一切时代）的军人大众的不同之处，正在于他不爱谈自己的战役——他的戎马生涯始于弗里德兰（Friedland），终于巴尔勒迪克（Bar-le-Duc）左近的某处。他对那位伟大皇帝的景仰毫无保留，唯独不肯形之于辞。正如诚挚者的宗教，那是一种太深的情感，不便陈列在一个信心寡薄的世界面前。除此之外，他仿佛全然没有军旅轶闻，好像这辈子简直没见过一个士兵似的。他为自己二十五岁以前挣下的勋章自豪，却拒绝按欧洲至今通行的做法把勋带佩在扣眼上，连节庆场合也不愿炫示勋章，仿佛唯恐显得自夸，而有意把它们藏起来。「我有它们，这就够了。」他常这样咕哝。三十年间，勋章在他胸前只露过两次面——一次是家中一门吉庆的婚事，一次是一位老友的葬礼。那场受此殊荣的婚礼并不是我母亲的婚礼——这一点我知道得太晚，晚得来不及对尼古拉斯·B 舅公怀恨了；而他在我出生时早已补过——一封长长的贺信，内含如下预言：「他会看到更好的时代。」可见在他那颗怨愤的心里，也还住着一个希望。但他不是一位真正的先知。

===Original===
He was a man of strange contradictions. Living for many years in his brother’s house, the home of many children, a house full of life, of animation, noisy with a constant coming and going of many guests, he kept his habits of solitude and silence. Considered as obstinately secretive in all his purposes, he was in reality the victim of a most painful irresolution in all matters of civil life. Under his taciturn, phlegmatic behaviour was hidden a faculty of short-lived, passionate anger. I suspect he had no talent for narrative; but it seemed to afford him sombre satisfaction to declare that he was the last man to ride over the bridge of the river Elster after the battle of Leipzig. Lest some construction favourable to his valour should be put on the fact he condescended to explain how it came to pass. It seems that shortly after the retreat began he was sent back to the town where some divisions of the French Army (and amongst them the Polish corps of Prince Joseph Poniatowski), jammed hopelessly in the streets, were being simply exterminated by the troops of the Allied Powers. When asked what it was like in there, Mr. Nicholas B. muttered only the word “Shambles.” Having delivered his message to the Prince he hastened away at once to render an account of his mission to the superior who had sent him. By that time the advance of the enemy had enveloped the town, and he was shot at from houses and chased all the way to the river bank by a disorderly mob of Austrian Dragoons and Prussian Hussars. The bridge had been mined early in the morning and his opinion was that the sight of the horsemen converging from many sides in the pursuit of his person alarmed the officer in command of the sappers and caused the premature firing of the charges. He had not gone more than two hundred yards on the other side when he heard the sound of the fatal explosions. Mr. Nicholas B. concluded his bald narrative with the word “Imbecile,” uttered with the utmost deliberation. It testified to his indignation at the loss of so many thousands of lives. But his phlegmatic physiognomy lighted up when he spoke of his only wound, with something resembling satisfaction. You will see that there was some reason for it when you learn that he was wounded in the heel. “Like his Majesty the Emperor Napoleon himself,” he reminded his hearers with assumed indifference. There can be no doubt that the indifference was assumed, if one thinks what a very distinguished sort of wound it was. In all the history of warfare there are, I believe, only three warriors publicly known to have been wounded in the heel﻿—Achilles and Napoleon﻿—demigods indeed﻿—to whom the familial piety of an unworthy descendant adds the name of the simple mortal, Nicholas B.

===Chinese===
他是个矛盾得奇怪的人。多年住在兄弟家中——一幢儿女成群的宅子，一幢生气充盈、宾客川流不息、人声不断的宅子——他却守着他孤独与沉默的习惯。人人以为他万事讳莫如深，其实，在一切俗世事务上，他倒是个最痛苦的优柔寡断的受害者。在那副沉默寡言、冷静迟钝的做派底下，藏着一种为期短促、火气暴烈的怒。我疑心他毫无叙事的才能；但宣称自己是莱比锡（Leipzig）战役之后最后一个驰过埃尔斯特河（the river Elster）大桥的人，似乎给了他一种阴沉的满足。唯恐别人把这件事往他英勇的方向去解释，他才屈尊说明了事情的来龙去脉。似乎是在撤退开始后不久，他被派回城里去——法军的几个师（其中有约瑟夫·波尼亚托夫斯基亲王（Prince Joseph Poniatowski）的波兰军团）死死堵在街上，正被联军部队干脆利落地屠戮。有人问城里是什么光景，尼古拉斯·B 舅公只咕哝出一个词：「屠宰场。」向亲王传达完命令，他立刻赶回去，向派他来的长官复命。那时敌军的推进已经合围了那座城，他从一幢幢房子里遭到射击，被一群乱糟糟的奥地利龙骑兵和普鲁士骠骑兵一路追到河岸。桥上一清早就埋好了炸药，照他的看法，是各路骑兵从四面八方追迫他这个人的景象，吓着了指挥工兵的那位军官，导致炸药过早起爆。他到对岸还走不到两百码，就听见了那几声致命的爆炸。尼古拉斯·B 舅公用「蠢材」一词结束了这段光秃秃的叙述，说得字斟句酌。那是对成千上万条性命之丧失的义愤的明证。可是说起他唯一的伤，他那张冷静的面孔却亮了起来，带着一点近乎得意的神气。等你知道他伤在脚跟上，你就会明白这得意不无理由。「同拿破仑皇帝陛下本人一样，」他提醒听众，装出一副淡然的样子。这淡然肯定是装出来的——只要想想那是何等卓越的一种伤，就明白了。我相信，整部战争史上，脚跟受伤而名闻于世的武士，拢共只有三位——阿喀琉斯（Achilles）与拿破仑——二位诚然是半神——第三位，是一个不肖子孙出于一瓣家族孝心，往这份名单上添的名字：区区凡人，尼古拉斯·B。

===Original===
The Hundred Days found Mr. Nicholas B. staying with a distant relative of ours, owner of a small estate in Galicia. How he got there across the breadth of an armed Europe and after what adventures I am afraid will never be known now. All his papers were destroyed shortly before his death; but if there was amongst them, as he affirmed, a concise record of his life, then I am pretty sure it did not take up more than a half-sheet of foolscap or so. This relative of ours happened to be an Austrian officer, who had left the service after the battle of Austerlitz. Unlike Mr. Nicholas B., who concealed his decorations, he liked to display his honourable discharge in which he was mentioned as *unschreckbar* (fearless) before the enemy. No conjunction could seem more unpromising, yet it stands in the family tradition that these two got on very well together in their rural solitude.

===Chinese===
百日王朝降临的时候，尼古拉斯·B 舅公正住在我们的一位远亲家里——那人在加利西亚（Galicia）拥有一处小小的田产。他是怎样横穿一个武装到牙齿的欧罗巴到那里去的，途中又经历了什么奇遇，恐怕如今永远无从知晓了。他的全部文件，在他去世前不久都已销毁；不过，倘若其中真有他一口咬定的那份生平简记，那我敢肯定，它满打满算也超不过半张对开大页纸。我们这位亲戚恰好是个奥地利军官，奥斯特里茨（Austerlitz）一役之后就退了役。尼古拉斯·B 舅公把勋章藏起来，他却喜欢炫耀自己的荣誉退役证书——上面写着他在敌人面前 *unschreckbar*（无畏）。再没有比这更不般配的结合了，然而家传的说法是：这两个人在乡野的清寂里相处得非常融洽。

===Original===
When asked whether he had not been sorely tempted during the Hundred Days to make his way again to France and join the service of his beloved Emperor, Mr. Nicholas B. used to mutter: “No money. No horse. Too far to walk.”

===Chinese===
有人问他，百日王朝期间，他难道没有狠狠动过心，再设法潜回法国，去投奔他敬爱的皇帝麾下？尼古拉斯·B 舅公总这样咕哝：「没钱。没马。走着去又太远。」

===Original===
The fall of Napoleon and the ruin of national hopes affected adversely the character of Mr. Nicholas B. He shrank from returning to his province. But for that there was also another reason. Mr. Nicholas B. and his brother﻿—my maternal grandfather﻿—had lost their father early, while they were quite children. Their mother, young still and left very well off, married again a man of great charm and of an amiable disposition but without a penny. He turned out an affectionate and careful stepfather; it was unfortunate though that while directing the boys’ education and forming their character by wise counsel he did his best to get hold of the fortune by buying and selling land in his own name and investing capital in such a manner as to cover up the traces of the real ownership. It seems that such practices can be successful if one is charming enough to dazzle one’s own wife permanently and brave enough to defy the vain terrors of public opinion. The critical time came when the elder of the boys on attaining his majority in the year 1811 asked for the accounts and some part at least of the inheritance to begin life upon. It was then that the stepfather declared with calm finality that there were no accounts to render and no property to inherit. The whole fortune was his very own. He was very good-natured about the young man’s misapprehension of the true state of affairs, but of course felt obliged to maintain his position firmly. Old friends came and went busily, voluntary mediators appeared travelling on most horrible roads from the most distant corners of the three provinces; and the Marshal of the Nobility (ex-officio guardian of all wellborn orphans) called a meeting of landowners to “ascertain in a friendly way how the misunderstanding between X and his stepsons had arisen and devise proper measures to remove the same.” A deputation to that effect visited X, who treated them to excellent wines, but absolutely refused his ear to their remonstrances. As to the proposals for arbitration he simply laughed at them; yet the whole province must have been aware that fourteen years before, when he married the widow, all his visible fortune consisted (apart from his social qualities) in a smart four-horse turnout with two servants, with whom he went about visiting from house to house; and as to any funds he might have possessed at that time their existence could only be inferred from the fact that he was very punctual in settling his modest losses at cards. But by the magic power of stubborn and constant assertion, there were found presently, here and there, people who mumbled that surely “there must be something in it.” However, on his next name-day (which he used to celebrate by a great three-days’ shooting-party), of all the invited crowd only two guests turned up, distant neighbours of no importance; one notoriously a fool, and the other a very pious and honest person but such a passionate lover of the gun that on his own confession he could not have refused an invitation to a shooting-party from the devil himself. X met this manifestation of public opinion with the serenity of an unstained conscience. He refused to be crushed. Yet he must have been a man of deep feeling, because, when his wife took openly the part of her children, he lost his beautiful tranquillity, proclaimed himself heartbroken and drove her out of the house, neglecting in his grief to give her enough time to pack her trunks.

===Chinese===
拿破仑的垮台与民族希望的破灭，使尼古拉斯·B 舅公的性格大受打击。他不愿回自己的省份去。但这里头还有另一层缘故。尼古拉斯·B 舅公和他的兄弟——我的外祖父——自幼丧父，那时他们都还是孩童。他们的母亲，年纪尚轻，又继承了一笔很可观的家产，改嫁给一个魅力十足、性情和善却身无分文的男人。这继父倒是个慈爱而细心的继父；不幸的是，他一面指点两个男孩的学业，用明智的劝导塑造他们的品性，一面却处心积虑要把家财弄到自己手里——用自己的名义买地卖地，又把资本投放在足以掩盖真正产权的地方。看来这一套是能成的，只要一个人魅力大到能把自己妻子永久地晃得眼花，胆子大到能蔑视舆论那些虚张声势的恐吓。紧要关头到了：1811 年，兄弟中的长者到了法定成年之龄，要求交出账目，并至少分给他一部分遗产，好开始人生。继父于是以冷静的决断宣布：无账可交，无产可承。全部家财是他自己的。对这位青年人对实情的误解，他非常和气，但自然觉得有义务把立场坚守到底。老朋友们奔走忙碌，自愿出面调停的人从三省最遥远的角落，循着最骇人的道路纷纷赶来；贵族长（凡出身名门的孤儿，照例都归他监护）召集地主开会，「以友好的方式查明 X 与其继子之间的误会因何而起，并筹拟妥善办法加以消解」。为此目的派出的代表团拜访了 X，他以美酒款待，却对他们的一切谏劝一概充耳不闻。至于仲裁的提议，他干脆一笑置之；然而全省必定都记得，十四年前他娶那位寡妇时，他全部看得见的家财（撇开社交才干不谈），就是一套神气的四驾马车带两名仆从，他就靠这个走家串户地做客；至于他当时或许拥有的款项，其存在只能由一个事实推知：他结算牌桌上适度的输账，向来分毫不爽。可是，凭着固执而不断的申说的魔力，不多时，各处就有人咕哝，说这里头「总归有点名堂」吧。然而，等到下一个命名日（他照例要连开三天盛大围猎会来庆贺），应邀的济济宾客之中，只到了两位——都是无足轻重的远邻；一个是出了名的傻子，另一个倒是极虔诚极诚实的人，只是嗜猎成癖，照他自己的说法，魔鬼本人请他打猎，他都推辞不得。X 以无愧于心的安详，迎接了这番民意的表态。他没有被压垮。但他想必是个感情深沉的人，因为当妻子公然站到自己孩子一边时，他失去了那份漂亮的平静，宣布自己伤心欲绝，把她赶出了家门——悲恸之中，疏忽得没给她留出足够打点箱笼的时间。

===Original===
This was the beginning of a lawsuit, an abominable marvel of chicane, which by the use of every legal subterfuge was made to last for many years. It was also the occasion for a display of much kindness and sympathy. All the neighbouring houses flew open for the reception of the homeless. Neither legal aid nor material assistance in the prosecution of the suit was ever wanting. X, on his side, went about shedding tears publicly over his stepchildren’s ingratitude and his wife’s blind infatuation; but as at the same time he displayed great cleverness in the art of concealing material documents (he was even suspected of having burnt a lot of historically interesting family papers), this scandalous litigation had to be ended by a compromise lest worse should befall. It was settled finally by a surrender, out of the disputed estate, in full satisfaction of all claims, of two villages with the names of which I do not intend to trouble my readers. After this lame and impotent conclusion neither the wife nor the stepsons had anything to say to the man who had presented the world with such a successful example of self-help based on character, determination and industry; and my great-grandmother, her health completely broken down, died a couple of years later in Carlsbad. Legally secured by a decree in the possession of his plunder, X regained his wonted serenity and went on living in the neighbourhood in a comfortable style and in apparent peace of mind. His big shoots were fairly well attended again.
