He was never tired of assuring people that he bore no grudge for what was past; he protested loudly of his constant affection for his wife and stepchildren. It was true, he said, that they had tried their best to strip him as naked as a Turkish saint in the decline of his days; and because he had defended himself from spoliation, as anybody else in his place would have done, they had abandoned him now to the horrors of a solitary old age. Nevertheless, his love for them survived these cruel blows. And there might have been some truth in his protestations. Very soon he began to make overtures of friendship to his eldest stepson, my maternal grandfather; and when these were peremptorily rejected he went on renewing them again and again with characteristic obstinacy. For years he persisted in his efforts at reconciliation, promising my grandfather to execute a will in his favour if he only would be friends again to the extent of calling now and then (it was fairly close neighbourhood for these parts, forty miles or so), or even of putting in an appearance for the great shoot on the name-day. My grandfather was an ardent lover of every sport. His temperament was as free from hardness and animosity as can be imagined. Pupil of the liberal-minded Benedictines who directed the only public school of some standing then in the south, he had also read deeply the authors of the eighteenth century. In him Christian charity was joined to a philosophical indulgence for the failings of human nature. But the memory of these miserably anxious early years, his young man’s years robbed of all generous illusions by the cynicism of the sordid lawsuit, stood in the way of forgiveness. He never succumbed to the fascination of the great shoot; and X, his heart set to the last on reconciliation with the draft of the will ready for signature kept by his bedside, died intestate. The fortune thus acquired and augmented by a wise and careful management passed to some distant relatives whom he had never seen and who even did not bear his name.

===Chinese===
于是有了一场官司，一件刁讼中的可憎奇观，靠尽种种法律的规避伎俩，被拖成了旷日持久。它也成了一次大显仁爱与同情的机会。左邻右舍的人家，家家大门洞开，接待无家可归的人。诉讼所需的法学援手与钱财资助，从无匮乏。X 那一边，则四处公开挥泪，哭继子女的忘恩，哭妻子的执迷；但与此同时，他在藏匿实质文书这门艺术上又显出高明的手段（人们甚至疑心他烧掉了许多饶有史料价值的家藏文书），于是这桩臭名昭著的诉讼不得不以妥协收场，以免更糟的祸事。最终的和解方案是：从有争议的田产中出让两座村庄，算是对一切权利要求的完全满足——那两个村名，我不打算拿来烦扰读者。经此跛脚而无力的了局，妻子与继子们对这位向世界奉献了如此成功的一例「以品格、决心与勤勉为本的自助」的先生，再无话可说；而我的外曾祖母，健康彻底垮了，几年之后死在了卡尔斯巴德（Carlsbad）。凭一纸判决把掠夺物合法落袋的 X，恢复了惯常的安详，继续在左近住得舒舒服服，表面上心境平和。他的盛大围猎会，赴会的又相当踊跃了。他不知疲倦地向人保证，往事不记仇；他高声申言，他对妻子和继子女的爱始终不渝。他说，他们确实处心积虑，要在他风烛残年把他剥得像一位土耳其圣徒一样赤条精光；而他不过像任何人处在他的位置都会做的那样，保卫自己免遭掠夺，他们如今便把他抛下，让他独自面对孤苦晚景的种种恐怖。话虽如此，他对他们的爱挺过了这些残酷的打击。而他的这些表白，或许也有几分真。不久，他就开始向长子——我的外祖父——递出友谊的表示；表示被斩钉截铁地回绝之后，他又以他特有的执拗，一而再、再而三地重新递过去。一连多年，他坚持和解的努力，向外祖父许诺立一份有利于他的遗嘱——条件仅仅是重修旧好，好到偶尔来走动走动的地步（在这一带，这算得上紧邻了，四十英里上下），或者命名日的大围猎会上赏个脸，露上一面。我的外祖父是各项运动的狂热爱好者。他的性情，想象得到地不含半分刻薄与怨毒。他受业于思想开明的本笃会士——当时南方唯一略有声望的公立学校便由他们主持——又遍读过十八世纪的诸家著述。在他身上，基督徒的博爱，联着一种对人性弱点的哲人式的宽宥。可是，那些凄惶焦虑的早年——他风华正茂的岁月，被这场卑污官司的犬儒主义剥尽了所有慷慨的幻想——的记忆，横在宽恕的路上。他始终没有臣服于大围猎会的魅力；而 X 呢，至死一心求和，那份只待签字的遗嘱草稿就放在床头，终究未立遗嘱而卒。他这样到手、又经明智而精心的经营加以扩大的家财，最后落到几个远亲手里——他从未见过他们，他们甚至连他的姓都不姓。

===Original===
Meantime the blessing of general peace descended upon Europe. Mr. Nicholas B., bidding goodbye to his hospitable relative, the “fearless” Austrian officer, departed from Galicia, and without going near his native place, where the odious lawsuit was still going on, proceeded straight to Warsaw and entered the army of the newly constituted Polish kingdom under the sceptre of Alexander I, Autocrat of all the Russias.

===Chinese===
其间，普遍和平的福泽降临了欧罗巴。尼古拉斯·B 舅公向那位好客的亲戚、「无畏」的奥地利军官道了别，离开加利西亚，也不回老家——那里那桩可憎的官司还没打完——径直去了华沙（Warsaw），加入了全俄专制君主亚历山大一世（Alexander I）权杖之下那个新立的波兰王国的军队。

===Original===
This kingdom, created by the Vienna Congress as an acknowledgment to a nation of its former independent existence, included only the central provinces of the old Polish patrimony. A brother of the Emperor, the Grand Duke Constantine (Pavlovitch), its Viceroy and Commander-in-Chief, married morganatically to a Polish lady to whom he was fiercely attached, extended this affection to what he called “My Poles” in a capricious and savage manner. Sallow in complexion, with a Tartar physiognomy and fierce little eyes, he walked with his fists clenched, his body bent forward, darting suspicious glances from under an enormous cocked hat. His intelligence was limited and his sanity itself was doubtful. The hereditary taint expressed itself, in his case, not by mystic leanings as in his two brothers, Alexander and Nicholas (in their various ways, for one was mystically liberal and the other mystically autocratic), but by the fury of an uncontrollable temper which generally broke out in disgusting abuse on the parade ground. He was a passionate militarist and an amazing drill-master. He treated his Polish Army as a spoiled child treats a favourite toy, except that he did not take it to bed with him at night. It was not small enough for that. But he played with it all day and every day, delighting in the variety of pretty uniforms and in the fun of incessant drilling. This childish passion, not for war but for mere militarism, achieved a desirable result. The Polish Army, in its equipment, in its armament and in its battlefield efficiency, as then understood, became, by the end of the year 1830, a first-rate tactical instrument. Polish peasantry (not serfs) served in the ranks by enlistment, and the officers belonged mainly to the smaller nobility. Mr. Nicholas B., with his Napoleonic record, had no difficulty in obtaining a lieutenancy, but the promotion in the Polish Army was slow, because, being a separate organisation, it took no part in the wars of the Russian Empire either against Persia or Turkey. Its first campaign, against Russia itself, was to be its last. In 1831, on the outbreak of the Revolution, Mr. Nicholas B. was the senior captain of his regiment. Some time before he had been made head of the remount establishment quartered outside the kingdom in our southern provinces, whence almost all the horses for the Polish cavalry were drawn. For the first time since he went away from home at the age of eighteen to begin his military life by the battle of Friedland, Mr. Nicholas B. breathed the air of the “Border,” his native air. Unkind fate was lying in wait for him amongst the scenes of his youth. At the first news of the rising in Warsaw all the remount establishment, officers, vets., and the very troopers, were put promptly under arrest and hurried off in a body beyond the Dnieper to the nearest town in Russia proper. From there they were dispersed to the distant parts of the Empire. On this occasion poor Mr. Nicholas B. penetrated into Russia much farther than he ever did in the times of Napoleonic invasion, if much less willingly. Astrakhan was his destination. He remained there three years, allowed to live at large in the town, but having to report himself every day at noon to the military commandant, who used to detain him frequently for a pipe and a chat. It is difficult to form a just idea of what a chat with Mr. Nicholas B. could have been like. There must have been much compressed rage under his taciturnity, for the commandant communicated to him the news from the theatre of war, and this news was such as it could be, that is, very bad for the Poles. Mr. Nicholas B. received these communications with outward phlegm, but the Russian showed a warm sympathy for his prisoner. “As a soldier myself I understand your feelings. You, of course, would like to be in the thick of it. By heavens! I am fond of you. If it were not for the terms of the military oath I would let you go on my own responsibility. What difference could it make to us, one more or less of you?”

===Chinese===
这个由维也纳会议创设、以承认一个民族昔日曾有独立存在的王国，只包括古老波兰版图的中央诸省。皇帝的兄弟康斯坦丁·帕夫洛维奇大公（Grand Duke Constantine Pavlovitch）以副王兼总司令之尊统治它；他贵贱通婚，娶了一位他眷恋至深的波兰贵妇，又把这份感情，以一种任性和暴戾的方式，延及他口中的「我的波兰人」。他面色灰黄，长着一张鞑靼人的脸和一双凶悍的小眼睛，走路双拳紧握，身子前倾，从一顶硕大无朋的三角帽底下射出疑忌的目光。他智力有限，神智本身也可疑。遗传的疯癫在他身上，不像在他两位兄弟亚历山大和尼古拉身上那样表现为神秘主义的倾向（两人各有各的路数，一个神秘地自由，一个神秘地专制），而是表现为一种控制不住的暴怒，通常在阅兵场上发作成不堪入耳的咒骂。他是个狂热的军国主义者，又是个惊人的操练大师。他对待他的波兰军队，就像被宠坏的孩子对待心爱的玩具，只有一点不同：夜里他没有把它带上床。它不够小，带不上床。但他整日整天地玩它，欣赏漂亮军服的花样，享受不停歇操练的乐趣。这一股孩子气的热狂——不为战争，只为军国主义本身——竟收到了可观的成果。到 1830 年年底，波兰军队以其装备、军械和当时所谓战场效率而论，已成为一件第一流的战术工具。波兰农民（不是农奴）以募兵制入伍列伍行间，军官则主要出身小贵族。尼古拉斯·B 舅公凭他的拿破仑资历，谋一个中尉军职毫不费力；但波兰军中升迁很慢，因为它是一个独立的组织，俄罗斯帝国对波斯或对土耳其的战争，它一概无份。它的第一场战役，对俄国本国的战役，也是它的最后一场。1831 年，革命爆发时，尼古拉斯·B 舅公是他团里的资深上尉。在那之前不久，他被任命为马匹采办站的站长，驻扎在王国境外我们的南方诸省——波兰骑兵的马匹几乎全部取给于那里。自从十八岁离家、以弗里德兰一战开始他的军旅生涯以来，尼古拉斯·B 舅公头一回重新呼吸到了「边区」的空气，他故乡的空气。不友善的命运，正在他青春的旧地等着他。华沙起义的消息一传来，整个马匹采办站——军官、兽医，乃至士兵——立刻被一体逮捕，成群解往第聂伯河（the Dnieper）之外、俄国本土最近的城镇，再从那里分散到帝国的边远地方。这一回，可怜的尼古拉斯·B 舅公深入俄国的程度，远过了拿破仑入侵时代的任何一次——只是心不甘情不愿得多。他的目的地是阿斯特拉罕（Astrakhan）。他在那里待了三年，获准在城里自由居住，但每天正午须到卫戍司令官那里报到，司令官还常常留他抽一斗烟、聊聊天。同尼古拉斯·B 舅公聊天能聊成什么样，很难形成一个公允的概念。他的沉默底下，想必压着极大的怒火，因为司令官要向他传达战事的消息，而这消息能是什么样就是什么样——对波兰人来说，坏极了。尼古拉斯·B 舅公收到这些通报时外表冷静，那俄国人倒对他的囚徒表现出热切的同情。「我自己也是军人，明白你的心情。你当然想在那边大干一场。天哪！我喜欢你。要不是军誓在身，我就自己做主放了你。多你一个少你一个，对我们能有什么两样？」

===Original===
At other times he wondered with simplicity.

“Tell me, Nicholas Stepanovitch”﻿—(my great-grandfather’s name was Stephen and the commandant used the Russian form of polite address)﻿—“tell me why is it that you Poles are always looking for trouble? What else could you expect from running up against Russia?”

===Chinese===
另一些时候，他会天真地纳闷。

「你给我说说，尼古拉·斯捷潘诺维奇（Nicholas Stepanovitch）」——（我的外曾祖父名叫斯蒂芬〔Stephen〕，司令官用的是俄式的敬称）——「你说说，你们波兰人怎么老是自找麻烦？往俄国身上撞，还能指望有什么好下场？」

===Original===
He was capable, too, of philosophical reflections.

“Look at your Napoleon now. A great man. There is no denying it that he was a great man as long as he was content to thrash those Germans and Austrians and all those nations. But no! He must go to Russia looking for trouble, and what’s the consequence? Such as you see me, I have rattled this sabre of mine on the pavements of Paris.”

===Chinese===
他还有本领发一点哲学的感想。

「瞧瞧你们那位拿破仑。一个伟人。只要他满足于痛打那些德国人、奥地利人和所有那些民族，他就是伟人，这没什么可否认的。可是不！他非要跑到俄国去找麻烦，结果怎样？就像你眼前看到的这副样子——我这条军刀，在巴黎的人行道上哐啷哐啷地拖过。」

===Original===
After his return to Poland Mr. Nicholas B. described him as a “worthy man but stupid,” whenever he could be induced to speak of the conditions of his exile. Declining the option offered him to enter the Russian Army, he was retired with only half the pension of his rank. His nephew (my uncle and guardian) told me that the first lasting impression on his memory as a child of four was the glad excitement reigning in his parents’ house on the day when Mr. Nicholas B. arrived home from his detention in Russia.

===Chinese===
回到波兰以后，每逢有人哄得他谈起流放时期的境况，尼古拉斯·B 舅公总把那人形容为「好人一个，就是蠢」。他谢绝了让他转入俄军服役的选项，以本衔半薪退役。他的侄儿（我的舅舅兼监护人）告诉我，他记忆中最早的、历久不磨的印象，是四岁那年，尼古拉斯·B 舅公从俄国拘禁地回到家那天，父母宅中一片欢腾的兴奋。

===Original===
Every generation has its memories. The first memories of Mr. Nicholas B. might have been shaped by the events of the last partition of Poland, and he lived long enough to suffer from the last armed rising in 1863, an event which affected the future of all my generation and has coloured my earliest impressions.

===Chinese===
每一代人都有自己的记忆。尼古拉斯·B 舅公最早的记忆，或许是由波兰最后一次被瓜分的事件塑成的；而他又活得很长，长到饱尝了 1863 年最后一次武装起义的苦果——那场事变影响了我这一代人全体的命运，也给我的最初印象染上了颜色。

===Original===
His brother, in whose house he had sheltered for some seventeen years his misanthropical timidity before the commonest problems of life, having died in the early fifties, Mr. Nicholas B. had to screw his courage up to the sticking-point and come to some decision as to the future. After a long and agonising hesitation he was persuaded at last to become the tenant of some fifteen hundred acres out of the estate of a friend in the neighbourhood. The terms of the lease were very advantageous, but the retired situation of the village and a plain, comfortable house in good repair were, I fancy, the greatest inducements. He lived there quietly for about ten years, seeing very few people and taking no part in the public life of the province, such as it could be under an arbitrary bureaucratic tyranny. His character and his patriotism were above suspicion; but the organisers of the rising in their frequent journeys up and down the province scrupulously avoided coming near his house. It was generally felt that the repose of the old man’s last years ought not to be disturbed. Even such intimates as my paternal grandfather, a comrade-in-arms during Napoleon’s Moscow campaign and later on a fellow officer in the Polish Army, refrained from visiting his crony as the date of the outbreak approached. My paternal grandfather’s two sons and his only daughter were all deeply involved in the revolutionary work; he himself was of that type of Polish squire whose only ideal of patriotic action was to “get into the saddle and drive them out.” But even he agreed that “dear Nicholas must not be worried.” All this considerate caution on the part of friends, both conspirators and others, did not prevent Mr. Nicholas B. being made to feel the misfortunes of that ill-omened year.

===Chinese===
他的兄弟——在那幢宅子里，他把自己面对生活最寻常难题时的那种厌世的怯懦，庇藏了大约十七年——于五十年代初去世了，尼古拉斯·B 舅公只好把勇气拧到铆钉处，对将来作个决断。经过长久而痛苦的踌躇，他终于被说服，承租了邻近一位朋友田产中的大约一千五百英亩。租约的条件非常优厚，但我想，那村子的僻静位置和一幢素朴、舒适、修缮良好的房子，才是最大的诱因。他在那里安安静静住了十年上下，少见人，也绝不参与省里的公共生活——在专横的官僚暴政之下，那种生活也谈不上是什么。他的品格与爱国心无可置疑；但起义的组织者们在这一省频繁地往返奔波，都刻意避开他的住宅。大家普遍觉得，老人晚年的安宁不该搅扰。就连我祖父这样的密友——他是拿破仑莫斯科远征时的袍泽，后来又在波兰军中做过他的同僚——随着起事之期临近，也不再去拜访他的老伙伴了。我祖父的两个儿子和独生女儿都深深卷入了革命工作；他本人则是那一类波兰乡绅，爱国行动的理想只有一条：「翻身上马，把他们赶出去。」可就连他也同意：「亲爱的尼古拉斯千万惊动不得。」朋友们——密谋的与不密谋的——如此体贴的谨慎，却没能让尼古拉斯·B 舅公免于尝到那个凶年的种种不幸。

===Original===
Less than forty-eight hours after the beginning of the rebellion in that part of the country, a squadron of scouting Cossacks passed through the village and invaded the homestead. Most of them remained formed between the house and the stables, while several, dismounting, ransacked the various outbuildings. The officer in command, accompanied by two men, walked up to the front door. All the blinds on that side were down. The officer told the servant who received him that he wanted to see his master. He was answered that the master was away from home, which was perfectly true.

I follow here the tale as told afterwards by the servant to my granduncle’s friends and relatives, and as I have heard it repeated.

===Chinese===
那一带叛乱开始后不出四十八小时，一队哥萨克侦察骑兵穿村而过，闯进了庄院。他们大部分列队在宅子和马厩之间，另有几个下了马，把各处附属房舍翻了个底朝天。指挥官带着两个人，径直走到正门前。那一面所有的百叶帘都放着。他对接待他的仆人说，他要见主人。得到的回答是主人外出不在家——这千真万确。

我这里照录的，是那仆人后来讲给我舅公的亲友们听的版本，也是我听人复述过的版本。

===Original===
On receiving this answer the Cossack officer, who had been standing in the porch, stepped into the house.

“Where is the master gone, then?”

“Our master went to J﻿—﻿—” (the government town some fifty miles off), “the day before yesterday.”

“There are only two horses in the stables. Where are the others?”

“Our master always travels with his own horses” (meaning: not by post). “He will be away a week or more. He was pleased to mention to me that he had to attend to some business in the Civil Court.”

While the servant was speaking the officer looked about the hall. There was a door facing him, a door to the right and a door to the left. The officer chose to enter the room on the left and ordered the blinds to be pulled up. It was Mr. Nicholas B.’s study with a couple of tall bookcases, some pictures on the walls, and so on. Besides the big centre table, with books and papers, there was a quite small writing-table with several drawers, standing between the door and the window in a good light; and at this table my granduncle usually sat either to read or write.

On pulling up the blind the servant was startled by the discovery that the whole male population of the village was massed in front, trampling down the flowerbeds. There were also a few women amongst them. He was glad to observe the village priest (of the Orthodox Church) coming up the drive. The good man in his haste had tucked up his cassock as high as the top of his boots.

===Chinese===
听到这个回答，原本立在门廊里的哥萨克军官，一脚跨进了宅门。

「那么，主人上哪儿去了？」

「我们家老爷前天动身到 J—— 城去了」（即约五十英里外的省城）。

「马厩里只有两匹马。其余的呢？」

「我们家老爷出门，向来用自备的马」（意思是：不乘驿站的车）。「他这一去要一个星期或者更久。他吩咐过小人，说他要到民事法庭去办点事。」

仆人说话的当口，军官环顾着门厅。迎面一扇门，右手一扇门，左手一扇门。军官择了左手那间走进去，吩咐把百叶帘拉起来。那是尼古拉斯·B 舅公的书房，有几座高高的书柜，墙上挂些画，如此等等。除了摆着书报文件的大写字台，还有一张带几个抽屉的小写字台，安在门与窗之间光线很好的地方；舅公平日读书写字，就坐在那张桌子跟前。

百叶帘一拉起，仆人吓了一跳：全村的男丁都聚在宅子前面，把花坛踩得稀烂。其中还夹杂着几个女人。他高兴地看到，本村的神父（东正教的）正沿着宅前车道走上来。好人赶路赶得急，把教士长袍一直撩到靴筒口上头。

===Original===
The officer had been looking at the backs of the books in the bookcases. Then he perched himself on the edge of the centre table and remarked easily:

“Your master did not take you to town with him, then.”

“I am the head servant and he leaves me in charge of the house. It’s a strong, young chap that travels with our master. If﻿—God forbid﻿—there was some accident on the road he would be of much more use than I.”

Glancing through the window he saw the priest arguing vehemently in the thick of the crowd, which seemed subdued by his interference. Three or four men, however, were talking with the Cossacks at the door.

===Chinese===
军官一直在看书柜里那些书的书脊。随后他往大写字台的边上一坐，随口说道：

「这么说，你主人进城，没带上你。」

「小人是管家，老爷把宅子交给小人照看。跟我们老爷出门的，是个结实的小伙子。万一——上天不容——路上出点什么意外，他可比小人顶用多了。」

他朝窗外瞥了一眼，看见神父正在人堆当中激烈地争辩，人群似乎被他的干预压住了。不过有三四个汉子，正在门口同哥萨克们说话。

===Original===
“And you don’t think your master has gone to join the rebels maybe﻿—eh?” asked the officer.

“Our master would be too old for that, surely. He’s well over seventy and he’s getting feeble too. It’s some years now since he’s been on horseback and he can’t walk much, either, now.”

The officer sat there swinging his leg, very quiet and indifferent. By that time the peasants who had been talking with the Cossack troopers at the door had been permitted to get into the hall. One or two more left the crowd and followed them in. They were seven in all and amongst them the blacksmith, an ex-soldier. The servant appealed deferentially to the officer.

“Won’t your honour be pleased to tell the people to go back to their homes? What do they want to push themselves into the house like this for? It’s not proper for them to behave like this while our master’s away and I am responsible for everything here.”

The officer only laughed a little, and after a while inquired:

“Have you any arms in the house?”

“Yes. We have. Some old things.”

“Bring them all here, on to this table.”

The servant made another attempt to obtain protection.

“Won’t your honour tell these chaps﻿ ﻿… ?”

===Chinese===
「你们不觉得，你们老爷兴许是投奔叛党去了——呃？」军官问道。

「我们家老爷干那个，年纪肯定太大了。他都七十好几了，身子骨也一天天衰了。他不上马都有好几年了，如今也走不动多少路了。」

军官坐在那里荡着腿，非常安静，非常漠然。这时候，先前在门口同哥萨克兵说话的那几个庄稼人已获准进了门厅。又有一两个离开人群，跟着他们进来了。总共七个人，其中有铁匠——一个退伍兵。仆人恭恭敬敬地向军官求告。

「老爷您行行好，叫乡亲们回自己家去，成不成？他们硬往宅子里挤，这是要干什么？我们家老爷不在家，这里里外外都归小人担着责任，他们这样胡来，实在不成体统。」

军官只是淡淡一笑，过了一会儿才问：

「宅子里有武器吗？」

「有。有几件。都是些老物件。」

「全都拿来，放到这张桌子上。」

仆人又作了一次求保护的尝试。

「老爷您就跟这些汉子们说一声……？」

===Original===
But the officer looked at him in silence in such a way that he gave it up at once and hurried off to call the pantry-boy to help him collect the arms. Meantime the officer walked slowly through all the rooms in the house, examining them attentively but touching nothing. The peasants in the hall fell back and took off their caps when he passed through. He said nothing whatever to them. When he came back to the study all the arms to be found in the house were lying on the table. There was a pair of big flintlock holster pistols from Napoleonic times, two cavalry swords, one of the French the other of the Polish Army pattern, with a fowling-piece or two.

===Chinese===
可是军官一声不响地看了他一眼，那眼神让他当即死了心，赶紧跑去叫配膳房的听差来帮着收拢武器。这期间，军官在宅子里所有房间慢慢走了一遍，看得仔细，却什么也不碰。他从门厅经过时，庄稼人们退到一边，摘下了帽子。他对他们一句话也没说。等他回到书房，宅子里找得出的武器都已摆在桌上：一对拿破仑时代的大号燧发鞍囊手枪，两把骑兵马刀——一把是法军制式，另一把是波兰军制式——外加一两支猎枪。

===Original===
The officer, opening the window, flung out pistols, swords and guns, one after another, and his troopers ran to pick them up. The peasants in the hall, encouraged by his manner, had stolen after him into the study. He gave not the slightest sign of being conscious of their existence and, his business being apparently concluded, strode out of the house without a word. Directly he left, the peasants in the study put on their caps and began to smile at each other.

The Cossacks rode away, passing through the yards of the home farm straight into the fields. The priest, still arguing with the peasants, moved gradually down the drive and his earnest eloquence was drawing the silent mob after him, away from the house. This justice must be rendered to the parish priests of the Greek Church that, strangers to the country as they were (being all drawn from the interior of Russia), the majority of them used such influence as they had over their flocks in the cause of peace and humanity. True to the spirit of their calling, they tried to soothe the passions of the excited peasantry and opposed rapine and violence whenever they could, with all their might. And this conduct they pursued against the express wishes of the authorities. Later on some of them were made to suffer for this disobedience by being removed abruptly to the far north or sent away to Siberian parishes.

===Chinese===
军官推开窗，把手枪、马刀、猎枪一件接一件扔了出去，他的部下跑过去捡。门厅里的庄稼人受了这做派的鼓励，蹑手蹑脚跟进了书房。他丝毫没有显出察觉他们存在的迹象，事情显然已经办完，便一言不发，大步走出了宅子。他前脚一走，书房里的庄稼人便戴上帽子，彼此微笑起来。

哥萨克们策马走了，穿过庄园的场院，径直进了田野。神父还在同庄稼人争辩，渐渐移下车道，他恳切的雄辩把那群沉默的人渐渐引离了宅子。对希腊教会的教区神父们，有句公道话必须说：他们虽是这片乡土的异乡人（全从俄国内地抽调而来），其中多数人，却把他们对教民的那点影响，用在了和平与人道的名下。他们忠于自身圣职的精神，努力抚慰激愤起来的庄稼人的情绪，凡力所能及，无不竭尽全力抵制劫掠与暴行。而他们这样做，是违拗当局的明令的。后来，其中一些人就为这份不服从付出了代价——被猝然调往极北之地，或被发配到西伯利亚的教区。

===Original===
The servant was anxious to get rid of the few peasants who had got into the house. What sort of conduct was that, he asked them, towards a man who was only a tenant, had been invariably good and considerate to the villagers for years, and only the other day had agreed to give up two meadows for the use of the village herd? He reminded them, too, of Mr. Nicholas B.’s devotion to the sick in the time of cholera. Every word of this was true and so far effective that the fellows began to scratch their heads and look irresolute. The speaker then pointed at the window, exclaiming: “Look! there’s all your crowd going away quietly and you silly chaps had better go after them and pray God to forgive you your evil thoughts.”

This appeal was an unlucky inspiration. In crowding clumsily to the window to see whether he was speaking the truth, the fellows overturned the little writing-table. As it fell over a chink of loose coin was heard. “There’s money in that thing,” cried the blacksmith. In a moment the top of the delicate piece of furniture was smashed and there lay exposed in a drawer eighty half-imperials. Gold coin was a rare sight in Russia even at that time; it put the peasants beside themselves. “There must be more of that in the house and we shall have it,” yelled the ex-soldier blacksmith. “This is war time.” The others were already shouting out of the window urging the crowd to come back and help. The priest, abandoned suddenly at the gate, flung his arms up and hurried away so as not to see what was going to happen.

In their search for money that bucolic mob smashed everything in the house, ripping with knives, splitting with hatchets, so that, as the servant said, there were no two pieces of wood holding together left in the whole house. They broke some very fine mirrors, all the windows, and every piece of glass and china. They threw the books and papers out on the lawn and set fire to the heap for the mere fun of the thing apparently. Absolutely the only one solitary thing which they left whole was a small ivory crucifix, which remained hanging on the wall in the wrecked bedroom above a wild heap of rags, broken mahogany and splintered boards which had been Mr. Nicholas B.’s bedstead. Detecting the servant in the act of stealing away with a japanned tin box, they tore it from him, and because he resisted they threw him out of the dining-room window. The house was on one floor but raised well above the ground, and the fall was so serious that the man remained lying stunned till the cook and a stable-boy ventured forth at dusk from their hiding-places and picked him up. By that time the mob had departed, carrying off the tin box, which they supposed to be full of paper money. Some distance from the house in the middle of a field they broke it open. They found inside documents engrossed on parchment and the two crosses of the Legion of Honour and For Valour. At the sight of these objects, which, the blacksmith explained, were marks of honour given only by the Tsar, they became extremely frightened at what they had done. They threw the whole lot away into a ditch and dispersed hastily.

On learning of this particular loss Mr. Nicholas B. broke down completely. The mere sacking of his house did not seem to affect him much. While he was still in bed from the shock the two crosses were found and returned to him. It helped somewhat his slow convalescence, but the tin box and the parchments, though searched for in all the ditches around, never turned up again. He could not get over the loss of his Legion of Honour Patent, whose preamble, setting forth his services, he knew by heart to the very letter, and after this blow volunteered sometimes to recite, tears standing in his eyes the while. Its terms haunted him apparently during the last two years of his life to such an extent that he used to repeat them to himself. This is confirmed by the remark made more than once by his old servant to the more intimate friends. “What makes my heart heavy is to hear our master in his room at night walking up and down and praying aloud in the French language.”

===Chinese===
仆人急着打发走那几个进了宅子的庄稼人。他质问他们：对这样一位老爷——不过是个租户，多年来待村里人向来和善体恤，前两天还刚答应让出两块草场给村里的牲畜共用——这样行事，算什么样的事理？他又提起尼古拉斯·B 舅公在霍乱流行时怎样看护病人。这些话句句属实，也颇见效，那几个汉子开始挠起头来，现出犹豫。说话的人便朝窗外一指，高声道：「瞧！你们那一大伙人都安安静静走了，你们这帮蠢货，最好跟上去，求上帝饶恕你们的恶念。」

这一呼吁是个倒霉的灵机。那几个汉子笨手笨脚地拥到窗前，想看看他说的是真是假，一下子掀翻了那张小写字台。台子翻倒时，响起一串散钱的叮当。「那里头有钱。」铁匠喊道。转眼间，这件精巧家具的桌面被砸得稀烂，抽屉里赫然躺着八十枚半帝国金币。金币在那时的俄国也是稀罕物；庄稼人们登时疯了。「宅子里一定还有，咱们非把它抠出来不可。」当过兵的铁匠吼道。「这是战时。」其余的人已经趴在窗口叫喊，招呼人群回来帮忙。神父被突然撂在大门口，双臂一扬，匆匆走开了，免得看见接下来要发生的事。

那一群乡野暴徒搜钱搜遍了整幢宅子，见什么砸什么：用刀撬，用斧劈，以至于照那仆人的说法，整幢宅子里再找不出两块还连在一起的木头。他们砸了几面极精美的穿衣镜，砸了所有的窗子，砸了每一件玻璃器皿和瓷器。他们把书报文件抛到草坪上，点着了那堆东西——看那样子，纯粹是为了取乐。他们完完整整留下的，绝对只有一件孤零零的东西：一只小小的象牙十字架，它仍旧挂在被砸烂的卧室墙上，底下是一大堆乱七八糟的破布、断裂的红木和碎木板——那本是尼古拉斯·B 舅公的床。他们撞见仆人抱着一只漆铁皮箱想溜，便从他手里夺了下来，又因为他反抗，把他从餐室的窗户扔了出去。宅子只有一层，却高出地面不少，这一摔摔得不轻，那人躺在地上人事不省，直到黄昏，厨娘和一个马夫才壮着胆子从藏身的地方出来，把他抬进屋。那时暴民们已经散了，掳走了那只铁皮箱，满以为里面装满了纸币。在离宅子不远的一块田中央，他们把箱子砸开了。里面是几卷誊写在羊皮纸上的文书，和荣誉军团与英勇两枚十字勋章。一看见这些东西——铁匠解释说，这是只有沙皇才颁赐的荣誉标记——他们对自己干下的事怕到了极点。他们把这一整堆东西扔进沟里，仓皇四散。

获悉这一项特别的损失时，尼古拉斯·B 舅公整个人垮了。宅子被洗劫这件事本身，似乎倒没怎么击中他。他还因这场打击卧床不起的时候，那两枚十字勋章找了回来，还给了他。这对他缓慢的康复不无小补；可是那只铁皮箱和那些羊皮纸文书，四下的沟渠都找遍了，再也没有现身。荣誉军团勋章证书的丧失，他始终不能释怀——证书的导言历数他的战功，他能一字不差地背下来；挨了这一击之后，他有时还主动背诵给人听，眼里站着泪。看得出来，那证书的字句在他生命最后两年里纠缠着他，缠得他时常向自己复述。他的老仆人向较亲密的朋友们不止一次说过的一句话，可以作证：「叫人心里发沉的，是夜里听见老爷在他屋里走来走去，用法语大声祷告。」

===Original===
It must have been somewhat over a year afterwards that I saw Mr. Nicholas B.﻿—or, more correctly, that he saw me, for the last time. It was, as I have already said, at the time when my mother had a three-months’ leave from exile, which she was spending in the house of her brother, and friends and relations were coming from far and near to do her honour. It is inconceivable that Mr. Nicholas B. should not have been of the number. The little child a few months old he had taken up in his arms on the day of his homecoming after years of war and exile was confessing her faith in national salvation by suffering exile in her turn. I do not know whether he was present on the very day of our departure. I have already admitted that for me he is more especially the man who in his youth had eaten roast dog in the depths of a gloomy forest of snow-loaded pines. My memory cannot place him in any remembered scene. A hooked nose, some sleek white hair, an unrelated evanescent impression of a meagre, slight, rigid figure militarily buttoned up to the throat, is all that now exists on earth of Mr. Nicholas B., only this vague shadow pursued by the memory of his grandnephew, the last surviving human being, I suppose, of all those he had seen in the course of his taciturn life.

===Chinese===
大约一年多之后，我见了尼古拉斯·B 舅公一面——或者更准确地说，是他见了我，那是最后一面。如我前面说过的，那时母亲获准三个月的流放假，正在她兄弟家中度过，亲朋故旧从四面八方赶来向她致敬。尼古拉斯·B 舅公不在其列，是不可想象的。当年他从多年战争与流放中归来那天，曾抱在臂弯里的那个几个月大的小女孩，如今正轮到她自己以身流放，宣认她对民族得救的信仰。他是否在我们启程的那一天在场，我不知道。我已经承认过，在我心中，他更是那个青年时代在大雪压松的阴郁森林深处吃过烤狗肉的人。我的记忆，没法把他安放进任何一个记得起来的场景。一只鹰钩鼻，几绺油亮的白发，一段不相联贯、转瞬即逝的印象——一个瘦削、单薄、僵直的身影，衣扣军装似的一直扣到喉咙口——这便是尼古拉斯·B 舅公如今在人间残存的一切了：只剩这一道模糊的影子，还被他的侄孙的记忆追索着；而这位侄孙，我想，是他沉默的一生中所见过的那些人里，最后一个还活在世上的。

===Original===
But I remember well the day of our departure back to exile. The elongated, *bizarre*, shabby travelling-carriage with four post-horses, standing before the long front of the house with its eight columns, four on each side of the broad flight of stairs. On the steps, groups of servants, a few relations, one or two friends from the nearest neighbourhood, a perfect silence, on all the faces an air of sober concentration; my grandmother all in black gazing stoically, my uncle giving his arm to my mother down to the carriage in which I had been placed already; at the top of the flight my little cousin in a short skirt of a tartan pattern with a deal of red in it, and like a small princess attended by the women of her own household: the head gouvernante, our dear, corpulent Francesca (who had been for thirty years in the service of the B. family), the former nurse, now outdoor attendant, a handsome peasant face wearing a compassionate expression, and the good, ugly Mlle. Durand, the governess, with her black eyebrows meeting over a short thick nose, and a complexion like pale brown paper. Of all the eyes turned towards the carriage, her good-natured eyes only were dropping tears, and it was her sobbing voice alone that broke the silence with an appeal to me: “*N’oublie pas ton français, mon chéri.*” In three months, simply by playing with us, she had taught me not only to speak French but to read it as well. She was indeed an excellent playmate. In the distance, halfway down to the great gates, a light, open trap, harnessed with three horses in Russian fashion, stood drawn up on one side with the police-captain of the district sitting in it, the visor of his flat cap with a red band pulled down over his eyes.

It seems strange that he should have been there to watch our going so carefully. Without wishing to treat with levity the just timidities of Imperialists all the world over, I may allow myself the reflection that a woman, practically condemned by the doctors, and a small boy not quite six years old could not be regarded as seriously dangerous even for the largest of conceivable empires saddled with the most sacred of responsibilities. And this good man I believe did not think so either.

===Chinese===
但我们重回流放地去的那一天，我记得清清楚楚。那辆车身修长、*bizarre*（怪模怪样）、寒酸的四马驿车，停在宅子长长的正立面跟前——正面立着八根圆柱，宽宽的石阶两侧各四根。台阶上，一群群仆人，几位亲戚，一两个近邻的朋友，一片绝对的寂静，人人脸上都是一种凝重专注的神情；外祖母一身黑衣，斯多噶式地凝望着；舅舅挽着母亲，一步步走下台阶，走向我早已被安放进去的那辆马车；石阶顶上，立着我的小表妹，穿着一条格纹短裙，红颜色占了多半，像个小公主，由她自己家里的女眷们伺候着：女教师总管、我们亲爱的胖胖的弗兰切斯卡（Francesca）——她在 B 家已经服务了三十年；昔日的乳母、如今的门外女仆，一张俊俏的农家面孔，带着悲悯的表情；还有善良的丑姑娘迪朗小姐（Mlle. Durand），那位女教师，两道黑眉连成一线，压在又短又粗的鼻梁上，面色像淡棕色的纸。在所有转向马车的眼睛里，只有她那双好性子的眼睛在落泪，也只有她呜咽的声音打破了寂静，冲我喊了一句：「*N'oublie pas ton français, mon chéri.*（别忘了你的法语，我亲爱的。）」短短三个月，光靠陪我们玩，她就教会了我不仅讲法语，还能读法语。她真是个出色的玩伴。远处，通向大门的路走到一半的地方，一辆轻便敞篷马车按俄式套着三匹马，停在道旁，车里坐着本县的警察局长，他那顶缀红帽箍的制帽，帽檐压到了眼睛上。

他把我们的离去盯得这样仔细，似乎很奇怪。我无意轻慢全世界帝国主义者们那点正常的胆怯，但仍不妨容自己想上一想：一个实际上已被医生判了死刑的女人，加一个不满六岁的小男孩，就算对那个可以想象的最大、又负着最神圣责任的帝国来说，恐怕也算不上真正危险。而我相信，这位好人自己也不认为危险。

===Original===
I learned afterwards why he was present on that day. I don’t remember any outward signs, but it seems that, about a month before, my mother became so unwell that there was a doubt whether she could be made fit to travel in the time. In this uncertainty the Governor-General in Kiev was petitioned to grant her a fortnight’s extension of stay in her brother’s house. No answer whatever was returned to this prayer, but one day at dusk the police-captain of the district drove up to the house and told my uncle’s valet, who ran out to meet him, that he wanted to speak with the master in private, at once. Very much impressed (he thought it was going to be an arrest) the servant, “more dead than alive with fright,” as he related afterwards, smuggled him through the big drawing-room, which was dark (that room was not lighted every evening), on tiptoe, so as not to attract the attention of the ladies in the house, and led him by way of the orangery to my uncle’s private apartments.

The policeman, without any preliminaries, thrust a paper into my uncle’s hands.

“There. Pray read this. I have no business to show this paper to you. It is wrong of me. But I can’t either eat or sleep with such a job hanging over me.”

That police-captain, a native of Great Russia, had been for many years serving in the district.

===Chinese===
他那天为什么在场，我后来才知道。我不记得有什么外部迹象，但似乎约莫一个月前，母亲病得那样重，一时都拿不准她能不能按期经得起旅途。在这悬而未决之中，家人向基辅的总督递了呈文，请求准许她在兄弟家中多住两个星期。这份呈请没有得到任何回音；可是有一天黄昏，本县的警察局长驱车来到宅前，对我舅舅那跑出来迎接的贴身男仆说，他有要事，要立刻单独见老爷。那仆人大为震动（他以为要来抓人了），据他后来讲，当时「吓得死去活来」，领着局长踮着脚尖穿过黑着灯的大客厅（那间屋子并非每晚都点灯），生怕惊动宅里的太太小姐们，又领他打花房那边绕过去，到了舅舅的私人住处。

那警察一句开场白也没有，把一纸公文塞进我舅舅手里。

「喏。请过目。这公文我本不该拿给您看。这是我的错处。可这么一桩差事悬在头上，我饭也吃不下，觉也睡不着。」

那位警察局长是大俄罗斯（Great Russia）本地人，在本县已任职多年。

===Original===
My uncle unfolded and read the document. It was a service order issued from the Governor-General’s secretariat, dealing with the matter of the petition and directing the police-captain to disregard all remonstrances and explanations in regard to that illness either from medical men or others, “and if she has not left her brother’s house”﻿—it went on to say﻿—“on the morning of the day specified on her permit, you are to despatch her at once under escort, direct” (underlined) “to the prison-hospital in Kiev, where she will be treated as her case demands.”

“For God’s sake, Mr. B., see that your sister goes away punctually on that day. Don’t give me this work to do with a woman﻿—and with one of your family, too. I simply cannot bear to think of it.”

===Chinese===
我舅舅展开读了那份公文。那是总督公署秘书处下发的一道勤务令，内容正涉及那件呈请，指令警察局长对一切有关那场疾病的谏劝与说明——无论来自医生还是旁人——一概置之不理，「倘若她在许可证载明之日的早晨尚未离开其兄弟的住宅」——公文接着说——「你须即刻派警押解，径直〔原文加着重点〕送往基辅的监狱医院，在那里按其病情予以收治。」

「看在上帝份上，B 先生，务必让您妹妹那一天准时动身。别把这差事派到我头上——办在一个女人身上，还是办在您家里人身上。我简直想都不敢想。」

===Original===
He was absolutely wringing his hands. My uncle looked at him in silence.

“Thank you for this warning. I assure you that even if she were dying she would be carried out to the carriage.”

“Yes﻿—indeed﻿—and what difference would it make﻿—travel to Kiev or back to her husband. For she would have to go﻿—death or no death. And mind, Mr. B., I will be here on the day, not that I doubt your promise but because I must. I have got to. Duty. All the same, my trade is not fit for a dog since some of you Poles will persist in rebelling, and all of you have got to suffer for it.”

===Chinese===
他简直是在绞自己的双手。我舅舅默默看着他。

「多谢您这一番预警。我向您保证，哪怕她咽气在即，也会被人抬上马车。」

「是啊——可不是——反正也没什么两样——去基辅，还是回她丈夫那儿去。因为她横竖得走——死也罢，不死也罢。还有，B 先生，那一天我会到场，不是我不信您的保证，是我必须到场。我非到不可。职责所在。话又说回来，自打你们波兰人里头有人非要造反不可，我这行当就落得狗都不配干了，你们大伙儿也就都得跟着遭殃。」

===Original===
This is the reason why he was there in an open three-horse trap pulled up between the house and the great gates. I regret not being able to give up his name to the scorn of all believers in the rights of conquest, as a reprehensibly sensitive guardian of Imperial greatness. On the other hand, I am in a position to state the name of the Governor-General who signed the order with the marginal note “to be carried out to the letter” in his own handwriting. The gentleman’s name was Bezak. A high dignitary, an energetic official, the idol for a time of the Russian Patriotic Press.

Each generation has its memories.

===Chinese===
这便是那天他把一辆三驾敞篷轻便马车停在宅子与大门之间的原因。我很遗憾，不能把他的名字交给一切信奉征服权利的人们去鄙夷，斥之为帝国伟业的一名心肠软得该受申斥的看守人。另一方面，那位在命令上签字、并亲笔加上「须不折不扣执行」眉批的总督，我倒有本事说出他的名字。这位先生叫贝扎克（Bezak）。一位显贵，一位干练的官员，一度是俄国爱国报刊的宠儿。

每一代人，都有自己的记忆。

## IV / 四

===Original===
It must not be supposed that in setting forth the memories of this half-hour between the moment my uncle left my room till we met again at dinner, I am losing sight of *Almayer’s Folly*. Having confessed that my first novel was begun in idleness﻿—a holiday task﻿—I think I have also given the impression that it was a much-delayed book. It was never dismissed from my mind, even when the hope of ever finishing it was very faint. Many things came in its way: daily duties, new impressions, old memories. It was not the outcome of a need﻿—the famous need of self-expression which artists find in their search for motives. The necessity which impelled me was a hidden, obscure necessity, a completely masked and unaccountable phenomenon. Or perhaps some idle and frivolous magician (there must be magicians in London) had cast a spell over me through his parlour window as I explored the maze of streets east and west in solitary leisurely walks without chart and compass. Till I began to write that novel I had written nothing but letters, and not very many of these. I never made a note of a fact, of an impression or of an anecdote in my life. The conception of a planned book was entirely outside my mental range when I sat down to write; the ambition of being an author had never turned up amongst these gracious imaginary existences one creates fondly for oneself at times in the stillness and immobility of a daydream: yet it stands clear as the sun at noonday that from the moment I had done blackening over the first manuscript page of *Almayer’s Folly* (it contained about two hundred words and this proportion of words to a page has remained with me through the fifteen years of my writing life), from the moment I had, in the simplicity of my heart and the amazing ignorance of my mind, written that page the die was cast. Never had Rubicon been more blindly forded, without invocation to the gods, without fear of men.

===Chinese===
切莫以为，我在追述这半个钟头里的种种记忆——从舅舅走出我房间的那一刻起，到我们晚餐桌上重逢为止——就把《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》置诸脑后了。我既已招认我的第一部小说是在无所事事中开笔的——一件假日里的营生——我想我也给人留下了这样的印象：它是一本迟迟难产的书。可它从未被我从心头撂开，连写完它的希望极渺茫的时候也未曾。多少事情横插进来：日常的职务，新的观感，旧的记忆。它并非出于某种需要的产物——即艺术家们在探寻动机时念叨的那有名的自我表现的需要。驱策我的那种必然，是一种隐蔽而暧昧的必然，一种完全戴着面具、无从索解的现象。要不然，就是哪位闲得发慌、轻浮成性的魔法师（伦敦想必也有魔法师），趁我不带海图与罗盘、独自懒洋洋地漫步于东西纵横的街道迷宫之中时，从他家客厅的窗口对我施了咒。在动笔写那部小说之前，我平生除了信什么也没写过，而且信也为数不多。我一生从不曾把一件事实、一点观感或一段轶事记在本子上。坐下提笔的时候，一部规划周密的书这个概念，全然在我心智的范围之外；当作家的抱负，也从未在那些可意的虚构生涯中间露面——人在白日梦的静滞之中，有时会满心欢喜地替自己编造出那样一些生涯来。然而明明白白如同正午的太阳：从我把《阿尔迈耶的愚蠢》第一页手稿涂黑的那一刻起（那一页上大约二百来个词，而这个每页的词数比例，此后十五年写作生涯一直跟着我），从我在内心的单纯与心智的惊人无知之中写完那一页的那一刻起，骰子已经掷下。卢比孔河从没有人渡得比这更盲目，既不向诸神祷告，也不惧怕世人。

===Original===
That morning I got up from my breakfast, pushing the chair back, and rang the bell violently, or perhaps I should say resolutely, or perhaps I should say eagerly, I do not know. But manifestly it must have been a special ring of the bell, a common sound made impressive, like the ringing of a bell for the raising of the curtain upon a new scene. It was an unusual thing for me to do. Generally, I dawdled over my breakfast and I seldom took the trouble to ring the bell for the table to be cleared away; but on that morning for some reason hidden in the general mysteriousness of the event I did not dawdle. And yet I was not in a hurry. I pulled the cord casually, and while the faint tinkling somewhere down in the basement went on, I charged my pipe in the usual way and I looked for the matchbox with glances distraught indeed but exhibiting, I am ready to swear, no signs of a fine frenzy. I was composed enough to perceive after some considerable time the matchbox lying there on the mantelpiece right under my nose. And all this was beautifully and safely usual. Before I had thrown down the match my landlady’s daughter appeared with her calm, pale face and an inquisitive look, in the doorway. Of late it was the landlady’s daughter who answered my bell. I mention this little fact with pride, because it proves that during the thirty or forty days of my tenancy I had produced a favourable impression. For a fortnight past I had been spared the unattractive sight of the domestic slave. The girls in that Bessborough Gardens house were often changed, but whether short or long, fair or dark, they were always untidy and particularly bedraggled, as if in a sordid version of the fairy tale the ashbin cat had been changed into a maid. I was infinitely sensible of the privilege of being waited on by my landlady’s daughter. She was neat if anemic.

===Chinese===
那天早晨，我推开椅子从早餐桌前站起，狠狠摇了一下铃——或者该说断然地，或者该说急切地，我说不上来。但显而易见，那必定是一次特别的摇铃，一种寻常的声响却摇出了不寻常的分量，像催人肃静的铃声，宣告新的一幕即将启幕。这在我是不寻常的举动。平日我吃早餐总是磨磨蹭蹭，很少费神摇铃叫人收拾桌子；可那天早晨，出于隐没在这件事整体的神秘性之中的某种缘由，我没有拖沓。然而我也并不匆忙。我漫不经心地一拉铃绳，地下室深处隐隐传来的叮铃声还没停歇，我便照常往烟斗里装烟丝，四下里找火柴盒，目光固然慌乱，却绝无一丝狂热的迷乱，这一点我敢起誓。我还算镇定，过了好半晌才发觉火柴盒就在壁炉台上，就在我鼻子底下。而这一切都美妙地、安稳地一如往常。火柴还没扔下，房东太太的女儿就出现了，一张沉静苍白的脸带着探询的神气，立在门口。近来应我铃声的总是房东太太的女儿。我提这件小事是出于自豪，因为它证明，在我赁居的三四十天里，我已经给人留下了好感。已有两个星期，我不必再看那女仆不顺眼的模样了。贝斯伯勒花园那座房子里的女仆换得很勤，但无论矮个高个、金发黑发，她们总是邋遢不堪、尤其一副湿淋淋的狼狈相，仿佛在童话的龌龊版本里，垃圾箱里的猫变成了女佣。由房东太太的女儿来侍候，这份特权让我受宠若惊。她人还算齐整，只是贫血。

===Original===
“Will you please clear away all this at once?” I addressed her in convulsive accents, being at the same time engaged in getting my pipe to draw. This, I admit, was an unusual request. Generally on getting up from breakfast I would sit down in the window with a book and let them clear the table when they liked; but if you think that on that morning I was in the least impatient, you are mistaken. I remember that I was perfectly calm. As a matter of fact I was not at all certain that I wanted to write, or that I meant to write, or that I had anything to write about. No, I was not impatient. I lounged between the mantelpiece and the window, not even consciously waiting for the table to be cleared. It was ten to one that before my landlady’s daughter was done I would pick up a book and sit down with it all the morning in a spirit of enjoyable indolence. I affirm it with assurance, and I don’t even know now what were the books then lying about the room. Whatever they were they were not the works of great masters, where the secret of clear thought and exact expression can be found. Since the age of five I have been a great reader, as is not perhaps wonderful in a child who was never aware of learning to read. At ten years of age I had read much of Victor Hugo and other romantics. I had read in Polish and in French, history, voyages, novels; I knew *Gil Blas* and *Don Quixote* in abridged editions; I had read in early boyhood Polish poets and some French poets, but I cannot say what I read on the evening before I began to write myself. I believe it was a novel, and it is quite possible that it was one of Anthony Trollope’s novels. It is very likely. My acquaintance with him was then very recent. He is one of the English novelists whose works I read for the first time in English. With men of European reputation, with Dickens and Walter Scott and Thackeray, it was otherwise. My first introduction to English imaginative literature was *Nicholas Nickleby*. It is extraordinary how well Mrs. Nickleby could chatter disconnectedly in Polish and the sinister Ralph rage in that language. As to the Crummles family and the family of the learned Squeers, it seemed as natural to them as their native speech. It was, I have no doubt, an excellent translation. This must have been in the year ’70. But I really believe that I am wrong. That book was not my first introduction to English literature. My first acquaintance was (or were) the *Two Gentlemen of Verona*, and that in the very MS. of my father’s translation. It was during our exile in Russia, and it must have been less than a year after my mother’s death, because I remember myself in the black blouse with a white border of my heavy mourning. We were living together, quite alone, in a small house on the outskirts of the town of T﻿—﻿—. That afternoon, instead of going out to play in the large yard which we shared with our landlord, I had lingered in the room in which my father generally wrote. What emboldened me to clamber into his chair I am sure I don’t know, but a couple of hours afterwards he discovered me kneeling in it with my elbows on the table and my head held in both hands over the MS. of loose pages. I was greatly confused, expecting to get into trouble. He stood in the doorway looking at me with some surprise, but the only thing he said after a moment of silence was:

===Chinese===
「劳驾，请马上把这些收拾掉好吗？」我用近乎痉挛的声调对她说，一边还忙着把烟斗吸出烟来。我承认，这是个不寻常的请求。平日里早餐起身，我总会拿本书在窗前坐下，随他们高兴什么时候来收拾桌子；但你若以为那天早晨我有半点不耐烦，那就错了。我记得自己十分平静。事实上，我根本不能确定我想写，或者我打算写，或者我有什么可写的。不，我不急。我在壁炉台与窗户之间懒懒地踱着，甚至并没有自觉地在等桌子收拾干净。十有八九，不等房东太太的女儿收拾完，我就会拿起一本书，怀着惬意的懒散，捧着它坐上整个上午。这一点我敢断言，而且直到现在我也不知道当时散放在房间里的是些什么书。无论它们是什么，总归不是大师的名著——清晰的思想与精确的措辞之秘诀，得到那里去找。打五岁起我就是个大读书人，这对一个从不曾察觉自己何时学会阅读的孩子来说，或许不算稀奇。十岁上我已经读了不少维克多·雨果和其他浪漫派作家的作品。我用波兰文和法文读过历史、游记、小说；《吉尔·布拉斯》和《堂吉诃德》读的是节本；童年早期我读过波兰诗人和一些法国诗人的作品，可我说不出自己动笔写作的前一晚读的是什么。我记得那是本小说，而且很可能是安东尼·特罗洛普的小说中的一部。非常可能。我与他相识未久。他是我头一回用英文阅读其作品的英国小说家之一。至于那些名满欧洲的人物，狄更斯、司各特和萨克雷，情形就不同了。我与英国想象文学的初次结缘是《尼古拉斯·尼克贝》。尼克贝太太竟能用波兰语东拉西扯、絮絮不休，阴险的拉尔夫竟能用那种语言大发雷霆，真是了不起。至于克拉姆尔斯一家和博学的斯奎尔斯一家，波兰语在他们嘴里竟像乡音一样自然。毫无疑问，那是个出色的译本。这该是一八七〇年的事了。但我实在相信我记错了。那本书并不是我与英国文学的初次结缘。最早的相识是《维洛那二绅士》，而且恰恰是从我父亲翻译的手稿中读的。那时我们流放在俄国，时间必在我母亲去世之后不到一年，因为我记得自己穿着重孝的镶白边黑罩衫。我们相依为命，孤零零两个人住在T——城郊外的一座小屋里。那天下午，我没有出去到我们与房东合用的大院子里玩，却赖在父亲平素写作的那间屋里。是什么壮了我的胆子爬上他的椅子，我实在不知道；只知道两个钟头之后，他发现我跪在椅子上，两肘撑着桌子，双手捧着头，伏在那摊散页手稿上。我窘极了，只等着挨训。他站在门口，略带诧异地望着我，但沉默片刻之后，他说的唯一一句话是：

===Original===
“Read the page aloud.”

===Chinese===
「把这一页大声念出来。」

===Original===
Luckily the page lying before me was not overblotted with erasures and corrections, and my father’s handwriting was otherwise extremely legible. When I got to the end he nodded and I flew out of doors thinking myself lucky to have escaped reproof for that piece of impulsive audacity. I have tried to discover since the reason of this mildness, and I imagine that all unknown to myself I had earned, in my father’s mind, the right to some latitude in my relations with his writing-table. It was only a month before, or perhaps it was only a week before, that I had read to him aloud from beginning to end, and to his perfect satisfaction, as he lay on his bed, not being very well at the time, the proofs of his translation of Victor Hugo’s *Toilers of the Sea*. Such was my title to consideration, I believe, and also my first introduction to the sea in literature. If I do not remember where, how and when I learned to read, I am not likely to forget the process of being trained in the art of reading aloud. My poor father, an admirable reader himself, was the most exacting of masters. I reflect proudly that I must have read that page of *Two Gentlemen of Verona* tolerably well at the age of eight. The next time I met them was in a five shilling one-volume edition of the dramatic works of William Shakespeare, read in Falmouth, at odd moments of the day, to the noisy accompaniment of caulkers’ mallets driving oakum into the deck-seams of a ship in dry dock. We had run in, in a sinking condition and with the crew refusing duty after a month of weary battling with the gales of the North Atlantic. Books are an integral part of one’s life and my Shakespearean associations are with that first year of our bereavement, the last I spent with my father in exile (he sent me away to Poland to my mother’s brother directly he could brace himself up for the separation), and with the year of hard gales, the year in which I came nearest to death at sea, first by water and then by fire.

===Chinese===
幸而摊在我面前的那一页没有被涂抹修改得墨迹狼藉，父亲的笔迹又素来极其清楚易认。我念到末尾，他点了点头，我便一溜烟跑出门外，心想这回莽撞竟没招来训斥，运气实在好。此后我一直想找出这份温和的缘故，我想，必是我在不知不觉之间，已在父亲心里赢得了对他的写字桌稍微放肆一点的权利。就在一个月之前，或许不过一个星期之前，我曾在他卧病在床、身子不大舒坦的时候，从头到尾念给他听，念的是他那部维克多·雨果《海上劳工》译稿的校样，念得他完全满意。我相信，这便是我受此体谅的凭据，也是我与文学中大海的初次相识。倘若我不记得自己是何时何地、怎样学会阅读的，那么受训于朗读艺术的那番经历，我却忘不了。我那可怜的父亲，本人是一位可佩的朗读家，又是天下最苛求的师傅。我颇为得意地想，八岁那年，我把《维洛那二绅士》那一页念得想必还算过得去。下一次与这两位绅士相遇，是在一册五先令的单卷本威廉·莎士比亚戏剧集里，在法尔茅斯读的，趁白天零星的空当，伴着捻缝工的木槌往干船坞里一艘船的甲板缝里捶麻絮的嘈杂声响。我们是漏得快沉了才进坞的，同北大西洋的风暴苦斗一个月之后，船员已经拒绝执勤。书是一个人生命不可分割的部分，而我的莎士比亚记忆，正连着我们丧亲的头一年——那是我与流放中的父亲共度的最后一年（他一能硬起心肠承受别离，便把我打发回波兰我舅舅那里）——也连着那个狂风不断的年头，那一年我在海上两度濒死，先是水，后是火。

===Original===
Those things I remember, but what I was reading the day before my writing life began I have forgotten. I have only a vague notion that it might have been one of Trollope’s political novels. And I remember, too, the character of the day. It was an autumn day with an opaline atmosphere, a veiled, semi-opaque, lustrous day, with fiery points and flashes of red sunlight on the roofs and windows opposite, while the trees of the square with all their leaves gone were like tracings of indian ink on a sheet of tissue paper. It was one of those London days that have the charm of mysterious amenity, of fascinating softness. The effect of opaline mist was often repeated at Bessborough Gardens on account of the nearness to the river.

===Chinese===
这些我都记得，可写作生涯开始的前一天我读的是什么，却忘掉了。只剩下一点模糊的印象：或许是特罗洛普的政治小说中的一部。我还记得那天的天色。那是一个秋日，空气呈蛋白石般的光泽，一个蒙着面纱、半透明而莹润的日子，对面的屋顶和窗玻璃上闪动着一簇簇火红的阳光与光斑，广场上的树落尽了叶，像一张薄纸上用墨描出的轮廓。伦敦有些日子自有一种神秘宜人的魅力、一种醉人的温柔，那便是其中之一。因为离河不远，这种蛋白色雾霭的效果在贝斯伯勒花园时常重现。

===Original===
There is no reason why I should remember that effect more on that day than on any other day, except that I stood for a long time looking out of the window after the landlady’s daughter was gone with her spoil of cups and saucers. I heard her put the tray down in the passage and finally shut the door; and still I remained smoking with my back to the room. It is very clear that I was in no haste to take the plunge into my writing life, if as plunge this first attempt may be described. My whole being was steeped deep in the indolence of a sailor away from the sea, the scene of never-ending labour and of unceasing duty. For utter surrender to indolence you cannot beat a sailor ashore when that mood is on him, the mood of absolute irresponsibility tasted to the full. It seems to me that I thought of nothing whatever, but this is an impression which is hardly to be believed at this distance of years. What I am certain of is, that I was very far from thinking of writing a story, though it is possible and even likely that I was thinking of the man Almayer.

===Chinese===
其实并没有什么理由，让我偏偏记住那一天、而不是别的日子的这种景致，只除了这样一桩：房东太太的女儿端着她那杯盘碟子的斩获走后，我在窗前伫立了很久。我听见她把托盘搁在过道里，末了关上了门；我依然背对着房间抽烟。很清楚，我并不急于一头扎进我的写作生涯——如果这初次尝试也可以称作一头扎进去的话。我整个身心，都浸透了一个离海上岸的水手的懒散——海上是无休无止的劳作与永不间断的职责。要论彻底向怠惰缴械，谁也比不过上了岸、而且那股劲头正上来的水手，那种把绝对的无责一身轻尝到十成的劲头。我觉得自己当时什么都不曾想，但隔了这么多年，这种印象很难令人相信。我能确定的是，我离想要写一篇故事还远得很，尽管我有可能、甚至很可能正在想着阿尔迈耶这个人。

===Original===
I had seen him for the first time some four years before from the bridge of a steamer moored to a rickety little wharf forty miles up, more or less, a Bornean river. It was very early morning, and a slight mist, an opaline mist as in Bessborough Gardens only without the fiery flicks on roof and chimney-pot from the rays of the red London sun, promised to turn presently into a woolly fog. Barring a small dugout canoe on the river there was nothing moving within sight. I had just come up yawning from my cabin. The serang and the Malay crew were overhauling the cargo chains and trying the winches; their voices sounded subdued on the deck below and their movements were languid. That tropical daybreak was chilly. The Malay quartermaster, coming up to get something from the lockers on the bridge, shivered visibly. The forests above and below and on the opposite bank looked black and dank; wet dripped from the rigging upon the tightly stretched deck awnings, and it was in the middle of a shuddering yawn that I caught sight of Almayer. He was moving across a patch of burnt grass, a blurred, shadowy shape with the blurred bulk of a house behind him, a low house of mats, bamboos and palm-leaves with a high-pitched roof of grass.

===Chinese===
我头一回见到他，是在大约四年之前，从一艘轮船的驾驶台上；那船系泊在一条婆罗洲河流上游约莫四十英里处一座摇摇欲坠的小码头旁。时间是大清早，一层薄雾——和贝斯伯勒花园一样的蛋白色薄雾，只是没有红艳艳的伦敦太阳在屋顶和烟囱帽上抹出的火红光点——眼看就要变成毛茸茸的浓雾。河上除了一只小小的独木舟，视野之内别无一物移动。我刚从舱里打着呵欠走上来。水手长正带着马来船员检修吊货链、试转绞车；他们的声音在下面甲板上听来低低的，动作也懒洋洋的。那热带的黎明颇有寒意。上驾驶台来从柜子里取东西的马来舵工，明显地打了个寒噤。上下游以及对岸的森林黑黢黢、湿漉漉的；水珠从索具上滴落在绷得紧紧的甲板遮阳篷上；而我恰在一个呵欠打到寒战的一半时，望见了阿尔迈耶。他正穿过一片烧过的草地走来，一个模糊的影子，身后衬着一座同样模糊的房子——一座低矮的房子，用席子、竹子和棕榈叶搭成，顶着高高的草顶。

===Original===
He stepped upon the jetty. He was clad simply in flapping pyjamas of cretonne pattern (enormous flowers with yellow petals on a disagreeable blue ground) and a thin cotton singlet with short sleeves. His arms, bare to the elbow, were crossed on his chest. His black hair looked as if it had not been cut for a very long time and a curly wisp of it strayed across his forehead. I had heard of him at Singapore; I had heard of him on board; I had heard of him early in the morning and late at night; I had heard of him at tiffin and at dinner; I had heard of him in a place called Pulo Laut from a half-caste gentleman there, who described himself as the manager of a coal-mine; which sounded civilised and progressive till you heard that the mine could not be worked at present because it was haunted by some particularly atrocious ghosts. I had heard of him in a place called Dongola, in the Island of Celebes, when the Rajah of that little-known seaport (you can get no anchorage there in less than fifteen fathom, which is extremely inconvenient) came on board in a friendly way with only two attendants, and drank bottle after bottle of soda-water on the after-skylight with my good friend and commander, Captain C﻿—﻿—. At least I heard his name distinctly pronounced several times in a lot of talk in Malay language. Oh, yes, I heard it quite distinctly﻿—Almayer, Almayer﻿—and saw Captain C﻿—﻿— smile while the fat, dingy Rajah laughed audibly. To hear a Malay Rajah laugh outright is a rare experience, I can assure you. And I overheard more of Almayer’s name amongst our deck passengers (mostly wandering traders of good repute) as they sat all over the ship﻿—each man fenced round with bundles and boxes﻿—on mats, on pillows, on quilts, on billets of wood, conversing of Island affairs. Upon my word, I heard the mutter of Almayer’s name faintly at midnight, while making my way aft from the bridge to look at the patent taffrail-log tinkling its quarter-miles in the great silence of the sea. I don’t mean to say that our passengers dreamed aloud of Almayer, but it is indubitable that two of them at least, who could not sleep apparently and were trying to charm away the trouble of insomnia by a little whispered talk at that ghostly hour, were referring in some way or other to Almayer. It was really impossible on board that ship to get away definitely from Almayer; and a very small pony tied up forward and whisking its tail inside the galley, to the great embarrassment of our Chinaman cook, was destined for Almayer. What he wanted with a pony goodness only knows, since I am perfectly certain he could not ride it; but here you have the man, ambitious, aiming at the grandiose, importing a pony, whereas in the whole settlement at which he used to shake daily his impotent fist there was only one path that was practicable for a pony: a quarter of a mile at most, hedged in by hundreds of square leagues of virgin forest. But who knows? The importation of that Bali pony might have been part of some deep scheme, of some diplomatic plan, of some hopeful intrigue. With Almayer one could never tell. He governed his conduct by considerations removed from the obvious, by incredible assumptions, which rendered his logic impenetrable to any reasonable person. I learned all this later. That morning, seeing the figure in pyjamas moving in the mist, I said to myself: “That’s the man.”

===Chinese===
他踏上栈桥。他穿得简单，只是一条哗啦啦飘动的印花布睡裤（底子是惹眼的不讨喜的蓝色，上面开着大朵大朵黄瓣的花），加一件短袖薄棉汗衫。两条胳膊赤到肘部，交叉在胸前。他的黑发像是很久没有剪过了，一绺鬈发披散在额前。我在新加坡就听说过他；我在船上听说过他；一大早听说过他，深夜也听说过他；午餐时听说过，晚餐时也听说过；我在一个叫普劳劳特的地方听说过他，从当地一位混血绅士口中——那人自称是一座煤矿的经理，听着倒文明而进步，直到你听说那矿眼下开采不得，因为里头闹着几个格外凶恶的鬼。我在一个叫栋戈拉的地方、在西里伯斯岛上听说过他，当时那个鲜为人知的港口的拉者（那地方不到十五英寻寻不着锚地，极是不便）只带两名随从、和和气气地上了船，同我的好友兼船长C——坐在后舱天窗旁，一瓶接一瓶地灌苏打水。至少，在一片马来语的谈话里，我清清楚楚听见他的名字被念了好几遍。哦，是的，我听得真真切切——阿尔迈耶，阿尔迈耶——还看见C——船长微笑，而那位胖乎乎、灰扑扑的拉者朗声大笑。要听一位马来拉者放声大笑，可是难得的经历，我可以向你担保。我还从我们的甲板乘客中间（大都是信誉不错的流动商贩）听到过更多阿尔迈耶的名字，他们满船坐着——每人围着自己的包袱和箱子圈出地盘——坐在席子上、坐垫上、被褥上、木墩上，谈着群岛的事务。说真的，午夜时分，我从驾驶台往船尾走、去看那台专利船尾计程仪在海上巨大的静寂里叮叮当当数着四分之一海里的时候，还隐隐听见阿尔迈耶的名字在喃喃人语中飘过。我并不是说我们的乘客会大声梦见阿尔迈耶，但确凿无疑的是，至少有两个人——他们显然睡不着，想借着那个鬼魅时辰的一点耳语驱走失眠的烦恼——正在这样那样地谈论着阿尔迈耶。在那条船上，要想彻底躲开阿尔迈耶实在不可能；还有，拴在船头、尾巴扫进厨房门里、害得我们中国厨子大为狼狈的一匹小小马，正是要送给阿尔迈耶的。他要一匹小马做什么，只有天知道，因为我完全确定他根本不会骑；可是你瞧这个人，野心勃勃，一心向往宏大气象，居然进口了一匹小马，而在他天天对着挥舞无能拳头的那个居留地里，小马走得通的路只有一条：至多四分之一英里长，四周被几百平方里格的原始森林圈着。可谁知道呢？进口那匹巴厘小马，没准是某个深远图谋的一部分，某个外交方略、某个有指望的密谋的一部分。跟阿尔迈耶打交道，你永远说不准。他行事依据的是远离常情的考量，是匪夷所思的假定，因此他的逻辑对任何讲道理的人都深不可测。这些都是我后来才知道的。那天早晨，看着那个穿睡裤的身影在雾里移动，我对自己说：「就是这个人。」

===Original===
He came quite close to the ship’s side and raised a harassed countenance, round and flat, with that curl of black hair over the forehead and a heavy, pained glance.

===Chinese===
他走到紧挨船舷的地方，抬起一张愁苦的脸，又圆又扁，额前披着那绺鬈曲的黑发，目光滞重而痛苦。

===Original===
“Good morning.”

===Chinese===
「早安。」

===Original===
“Good morning.”

===Chinese===
「早安。」

===Original===
He looked hard at me: I was a new face, having just replaced the chief mate he was accustomed to see; and I think that this novelty inspired him, as things generally did, with deep-seated mistrust.

===Chinese===
他狠狠地打量我：我是张新面孔，刚接替了他看惯了的大副；我想，这一新异照例在他心里激起了一种根深蒂固的猜疑。

===Original===
“Didn’t expect you in till this evening,” he remarked suspiciously.

===Chinese===
「还当你们傍晚才到得了呢。」他疑心地咕哝道。

===Original===
I don’t know why he should have been aggrieved, but he seemed to be. I took pains to explain to him that having picked up the beacon at the mouth of the river just before dark and the tide serving, Captain C﻿—﻿— was enabled to cross the bar and there was nothing to prevent him going up the river at night.

===Chinese===
我不知道他有什么可愤懑的，可他看上去就是愤懑。我费了番口舌向他解释：天擦黑前刚好认出了河口的信标，潮水又得力，C——船长得以越过拦门沙，再没有什么拦着他夜里溯河而上。

===Original===
“Captain C﻿—﻿— knows this river like his own pocket,” I concluded discursively, trying to get on terms.

===Chinese===
「C——船长熟悉这条河，就像熟悉自己的口袋。」我东拉西扯地补上这一句，想套套近乎。

===Original===
“Better,” said Almayer.

===Chinese===
「更熟。」阿尔迈耶说。

===Original===
Leaning over the rail of the bridge I looked at Almayer, who looked down at the wharf in aggrieved thought. He shuffled his feet a little; he wore straw slippers with thick soles. The morning fog had thickened considerably. Everything round us dripped﻿—the derricks, the rails, every single rope in the ship﻿—as if a fit of crying had come upon the universe.

===Chinese===
我凭着驾驶台的栏杆俯视阿尔迈耶，他正满怀委屈地低头望着码头出神。他的脚稍稍挪了挪；脚上是一双厚底草拖鞋。晨雾浓了许多。我们四周的一切都在滴水——吊杆，栏杆，船上每一根绳索——仿佛整个宇宙发作了一场呜咽。

===Original===
Almayer again raised his head and, in the accents of a man accustomed to the buffets of evil fortune, asked hardly audibly: “I suppose you haven’t got such a thing as a pony on board?”

===Chinese===
阿尔迈耶又抬起头来，用一种听惯了厄运摆布之人的声调，几乎听不见地问道：「你们船上没有带着一匹小马之类的活物吧？」

===Original===
I told him almost in a whisper, for he attuned my communications to his minor key, that we had such a thing as a pony, and I hinted, as gently as I could, that he was confoundedly in the way too. I was very anxious to have him landed before I began to handle the cargo. Almayer remained looking up at me for a long while with incredulous and melancholy eyes as though it were not a safe thing to believe my statement. This pathetic mistrust in the favourable issue of any sort of affair touched me deeply, and I added:

===Chinese===
我几乎用耳语告诉他——因为他把我的一切通报都调到了他的小调上——我们船上确实有这么一匹小马，我又尽量委婉地暗示，这家伙也实在碍事得很。我急着要在动手装卸货物之前把它卸上岸。阿尔迈耶仰头望了我好半晌，眼里满是难以置信的忧郁，仿佛相信我的话是件不保险的事。这种对任何事情都难有好结局的可悲的不信任深深触动了我，于是我补上一句：

===Original===
“He doesn’t seem a bit the worse for the passage. He’s a nice pony, too.”

===Chinese===
「这一路颠簸，它好像一点没吃亏。而且是匹漂亮的小马。」

===Original===
Almayer was not to be cheered up; for all answer he cleared his throat and looked down again at his feet. I tried to close with him on another tack.

===Chinese===
阿尔迈耶的情绪鼓舞不起来；他只清了清嗓子作为全部回答，又低头去看自己的脚。我试着换个路子接近他。

===Original===
“By Jove!” I said. “Aren’t you afraid of catching pneumonia or bronchitis or something, walking about in a singlet in such a wet fog?”

===Chinese===
「天哪！」我说，「这么湿的雾天，只穿一件汗衫走来走去，你不怕染上肺炎、支气管炎什么的吗？」

===Original===
He was not to be propitiated by a show of interest in his health. His answer was a sinister “No fear,” as much as to say that even that way of escape from inclement fortune was closed to him.

===Chinese===
对他的健康表示关切也安抚不了他。他的回答是一声阴沉的「不怕」，那意思像是说，就连这条躲开刻薄命运的出路，对他也是封死的。

===Original===
“I just came down﻿ ﻿…” he mumbled after a while.

===Chinese===
「我不过是下来一趟……」过了半晌他咕哝道。

===Original===
“Well, then, now you’re here I will land that pony for you at once and you can lead him home. I really don’t want him on deck. He’s in the way.”

===Chinese===
「那么好吧，既然你来了，我马上替你把小马卸上岸，你可以牵它回家。我真不想要它待在甲板上。碍事得很。」

===Original===
Almayer seemed doubtful. I insisted:

===Chinese===
阿尔迈耶似乎将信将疑。我坚持道：

===Original===
“Why, I will just swing him out and land him on the wharf right in front of you. I’d much rather do it before the hatches are off. The little devil may jump down the hold or do some other deadly thing.”

===Chinese===
「这样吧，我把它吊出去，就在你眼前放到码头上。我宁可趁舱盖还没打开就办妥。这小魔头没准会跳进货舱，或者干出别的要命的事来。」

===Original===
“There’s a halter?” postulated Almayer.

===Chinese===
「有笼头吧？」阿尔迈耶认定似的问。

===Original===
“Yes, of course there’s a halter.” And without waiting any more I leaned over the bridge rail.

===Chinese===
「有，当然有笼头。」我不再多等，探身到驾驶台栏杆外。

===Original===
“Serang, land Tuan Almayer’s pony.”

===Chinese===
「水手长，把图安·阿尔迈耶老爷的小马卸上岸。」

===Original===
The cook hastened to shut the door of the galley and a moment later a great scuffle began on deck. The pony kicked with extreme energy, the kalashes skipped out of the way, the serang issued many orders in a cracked voice. Suddenly the pony leaped upon the fore-hatch. His little hoofs thundered tremendously; he plunged and reared. He had tossed his mane and his forelock into a state of amazing wildness, he dilated his nostrils, bits of foam flecked his broad little chest, his eyes blazed. He was something under eleven hands; he was fierce, terrible, angry, warlike, he said ha! ha! distinctly, he raged and thumped﻿—and sixteen able-bodied kalashes stood round him like disconcerted nurses round a spoilt and passionate child. He whisked his tail incessantly; he arched his pretty neck; he was perfectly delightful; he was charmingly naughty. There was not an atom of vice in that performance; no savage baring of teeth and laying back of ears. On the contrary, he pricked them forward in a comically aggressive manner. He was totally unmoral and lovable; I would have liked to give him bread, sugar, carrots. But life is a stern thing and the sense of duty the only safe guide. So I steeled my heart and from my elevated position on the bridge I ordered the men to fling themselves upon him in a body.

===Chinese===
厨子赶紧关上厨房的门，片刻之后，甲板上开始了一场大混战。小马力气十足地乱踢，土著水手们蹦跳着躲闪，水手长扯着沙哑的嗓子连下号令。忽然小马一跃跳上了前舱盖。小小的蹄子擂出惊天动地的声响；它又掀又立。它把鬃毛和额发甩成一片惊人的狂野，鼻孔张得大大的，宽阔的小胸脯上溅着点点白沫，两眼放光。它身高还不到十一掌（量马高度的单位，一掌合四英寸）；它凶悍，可怕，恼怒，好战，它分明喊着哈！哈！，它又暴跳又擂蹄——十六名精壮的土著水手围着它站着，像束手无策的保姆们围着一个被惯坏的暴脾气孩子。它甩着尾巴甩个不停；它把自己漂亮的脖颈弓成弧形；它可爱极了；它淘气得迷人。那场表演里没有一丝一毫的恶意；没有狰狞的龇牙，没有狠狠向后伏倒的耳朵。恰恰相反，它把耳朵滑稽而挑衅地朝前竖着。它全然无所谓善恶，只管讨人喜欢；我真想喂它面包、糖、胡萝卜。可是生活是严峻的，责任感才是唯一可靠的向导。于是我硬起心肠，从驾驶台的高处下令众人一齐扑上去。

===Original===
The elderly serang, emitting a strange inarticulate cry, gave the example. He was an excellent petty officer﻿—very competent indeed, and a moderate opium smoker. The rest of them in one great rush smothered that pony. They hung on to his ears, to his mane, to his tail; they lay in piles across his back, seventeen in all. The carpenter, seizing the hook of the cargo-chain, flung himself on the top of them. A very satisfactory petty officer, too, but he stuttered. Have you ever heard a light-yellow, lean, sad, earnest Chinaman stutter in pidgin-English? It’s very weird indeed. He made the eighteenth. I could not see the pony at all; but from the swaying and heaving of that heap of men I knew that there was something alive inside.

===Chinese===
年迈的水手长发出一声古怪的、不成言语的喊叫，率先做出了榜样。他是个极出色的小头目——确实十分干练，鸦片也抽得有节制。其余的人一拥而上，把那匹小马闷在底下。他们吊住它的耳朵、鬃毛、尾巴；他们成堆地横趴在它背上，一共十七个。木匠抓起吊货链的钩子，扑在这一堆人的最顶上。他也是个很叫人满意的小头目，只是口吃。你可曾听过一个淡黄脸、精瘦、忧郁、一本正经的中国人用洋泾浜英语结结巴巴地说话？那真是古怪极了。他是第十八个。小马我完全看不见了；但从那一堆人的摇晃与起伏，我知道里面有个活物。

===Original===
From the wharf Almayer hailed in quavering tones:

===Chinese===
码头上，阿尔迈耶用发颤的声音喊道：

===Original===
“Oh, I say!”

===Chinese===
「喂，我说！」

===Original===
Where he stood he could not see what was going on on deck unless perhaps the tops of the men’s heads; he could only hear the scuffle, the mighty thuds, as if the ship were being knocked to pieces. I looked over: “What is it?”

===Chinese===
以他所站的位置，甲板上的动静他什么也看不见，至多看见几个人的头顶；他只能听见那场混战，那一声声重击，仿佛船正在被人拆散。我探出头去：「什么事？」

===Original===
“Don’t let them break his legs,” he entreated me plaintively.

===Chinese===
「别让他们把它的腿弄断了。」他哀声央告我。

===Original===
“Oh, nonsense! He’s all right now. He can’t move.” By that time the cargo-chain had been hooked to the broad canvas belt round the pony’s body, the kalashes sprang off simultaneously in all directions, rolling over each other, and the worthy serang, making a dash behind the winch, turned the steam on.

===Chinese===
「咳，瞎说！它现在老实了。动不了啦。」这时吊货链已经钩住小马身上那条宽阔的帆布吊带，土著水手们同时朝四面八方跳开，滚作一团，可敬的水手长一个箭步蹿到绞车后面，打开了蒸汽。

===Original===
“Steady!” I yelled, in great apprehension of seeing the animal snatched up to the very head of the derrick.

===Chinese===
「稳住！」我大吼一声，生怕看见那牲口被一下子拽到吊杆的顶头。

===Original===
On the wharf Almayer shuffled his straw slippers uneasily. The rattle of the winch stopped, and in a tense, impressive silence that pony began to swing across the deck.

===Chinese===
码头上，阿尔迈耶不安地蹭着他那双草拖鞋。绞车的嗒嗒声停了，在一种紧张而肃穆的寂静中，那匹小马开始荡过甲板的上空。

===Original===
How limp he was! Directly he felt himself in the air he relaxed every muscle in a most wonderful manner. His four hoofs knocked together in a bunch, his head hung down, and his tail remained pendent in a nerveless and absolute immobility. He reminded me vividly of the pathetic little sheep which hangs on the collar of the Order of the Golden Fleece. I had no idea that anything in the shape of a horse could be so limp as that, either living or dead. His wild mane hung down lumpily, a mere mass of inanimate horsehair; his aggressive ears had collapsed, but as he went swaying slowly across the front of the bridge I noticed an astute gleam in his dreamy, half-closed eye. A trustworthy quartermaster, his glance anxious and his mouth on the broad grin, was easing over the derrick watchfully. I superintended, greatly interested.

===Chinese===
它软塌塌的！一悬到半空，它立刻以一种最可观的方式把全身肌肉都松弛下来。四只蹄子并成一束碰在一起，脑袋耷拉着，尾巴垂着，全然无力、纹丝不动。它让我鲜明地想起金羊毛骑士团勋章领口上悬着的那只可怜的小绵羊。我从不知道凡是马形的东西竟能软成那样，活的死的都一样。它那狂野的鬃毛一绺绺耷拉下来，成了一团没有生命的马毛；它那挑衅的耳朵塌了下去；但当它缓缓荡过驾驶台前面时，我注意到它那双迷离的半阖的眼里闪过一丝狡黠。一位靠得住的舵手正小心翼翼地缓着吊杆的吊索，眼里透着焦急，嘴咧得老大。我在一旁监督，兴味盎然。

===Original===
“So! That will do.”

===Chinese===
「好了！行了。」

===Original===
The derrick-head stopped. The kalashes lined the rail. The rope of the halter hung perpendicular and motionless like a bellpull in front of Almayer. Everything was very still. I suggested amicably that he should catch hold of the rope and mind what he was about. He extended a provokingly casual and superior hand.

===Chinese===
吊杆头停住了。土著水手们沿栏杆排成一列。笼头上的绳索垂直不动地悬在阿尔迈耶面前，像一条拉铃绳。万籁俱寂。我好心提点他，请他抓住绳子，留神自己的一举一动。他伸出一只手，那副随随便便、居高临下的懒散劲儿直叫人生气。

===Original===
“Look out, then! Lower away!”

===Chinese===
「那好，留神！慢慢放！」

===Original===
Almayer gathered in the rope intelligently enough, but when the pony’s hoofs touched the wharf he gave way all at once to a most foolish optimism. Without pausing, without thinking, almost without looking, he disengaged the hook suddenly from the sling, and the cargo-chain, after hitting the pony’s quarters, swung back against the ship’s side with a noisy, rattling slap. I suppose I must have blinked. I know I missed something, because the next thing I saw was Almayer lying flat on his back on the jetty. He was alone.

===Chinese===
阿尔迈耶收绳子的动作还算明白，可小马的蹄子一沾码头，他一下子就沉入了一种最愚蠢的乐观。不停顿，不思考，几乎连看也不看，他猛地把钩子从吊带上摘了下来，于是吊货链擦过小马的臀部，哐啷一声甩回去，狠狠拍在船舷上。我估计我准是眨了一下眼。我知道自己漏掉了什么，因为我接着看见的，便是阿尔迈耶仰面朝天躺在栈桥上。只有他一个人。

===Original===
Astonishment deprived me of speech long enough to give Almayer time to pick himself up in a leisurely and painful manner. The kalashes lining the rail had all their mouths open. The mist flew in the light breeze, and it had come over quite thick enough to hide the shore completely.

===Chinese===
惊愕夺去了我的言语，时间刚够阿尔迈耶慢吞吞而痛苦地自己爬起来。沿栏杆排成一列的土著水手个个张着嘴。薄雾在微风中飞动，已经浓得把河岸完全遮没了。

===Original===
“How on earth did you manage to let him get away?” I asked scandalised.

===Chinese===
「你到底怎么搞的，竟让它跑了？」我愤然问道。

===Original===
Almayer looked into the smarting palm of his right hand, but did not answer my inquiry.

===Chinese===
阿尔迈耶看了看自己右掌火辣辣的掌心，却没有回答我的质问。

===Original===
“Where do you think he will get to?” I cried. “Are there any fences anywhere in this fog? Can he bolt into the forest? What’s to be done now?”

===Chinese===
「你说它能跑到哪里去？」我嚷道。「这雾里四处有栅栏吗？它能逃进林子里去吗？现在可怎么办？」

===Original===
Almayer shrugged his shoulders.

===Chinese===
阿尔迈耶耸了耸肩。

===Original===
“Some of my men are sure to be about. They will get hold of him sooner or later.”

===Chinese===
「我的人总有些在外面转悠的。早晚会抓住它的。」

===Original===
“Sooner or later! That’s all very fine, but what about my canvas sling﻿—he’s carried it off. I want it now, at once, to land two Celebes cows.”

===Chinese===
「早晚！说得倒轻巧，可我的帆布吊带怎么办——让它带跑了。我眼下就要用，马上就要，还要卸两头西里伯斯小牛呢。」

===Original===
Since Dongola we had on board a pair of the pretty little island cattle in addition to the pony. Tied up on the other side of the fore deck they had been whisking their tails into the other door of the galley. These cows were not for Almayer, however; they were invoiced to Abdullah bin Selim, his enemy. Almayer’s disregard of my requirements was complete.

===Chinese===
自栋戈拉以来，船上除了那匹小马，还带着一对漂亮的小岛牛。它们拴在前甲板另一侧，一直把尾巴扫进厨房的另一扇门里。不过这两头牛不是给阿尔迈耶的；发货单上写的是他的仇家阿卜杜拉·本·塞利姆。阿尔迈耶对我的需要全然置之不理。

===Original===
“If I were you I would try to find out where he’s gone,” I insisted. “Hadn’t you better call your men together or something? He will throw himself down and cut his knees. He may even break a leg, you know.”

===Chinese===
「我要是你，就会去弄清楚它跑哪儿去了。」我坚持道。「你还是把你的人叫拢来吧，或者想个法子？它会自己摔跟头蹭破膝盖的。弄不好还会断一条腿呢，你知道。」

===Original===
But Almayer, plunged in abstracted thought, did not seem to want that pony any more. Amazed at this sudden indifference, I turned all hands out on shore to hunt for him on my own account, or, at any rate, to hunt for the canvas sling which he had round his body. The whole crew of the steamer, with the exception of firemen and engineers, rushed up the jetty past the thoughtful Almayer and vanished from my sight. The white fog swallowed them up; and again there was a deep silence that seemed to extend for miles up and down the stream. Still taciturn, Almayer started to climb on board, and I went down from the bridge to meet him on the after deck.

===Chinese===
可是阿尔迈耶沉浸在自己的思绪里，似乎再也不想要那匹小马了。我对这突如其来的漠然大为惊讶，便自己召集全船人手上岸替他找马——无论如何也要找回缠在马身上的那条帆布吊带。轮船上全体人员，除却锅炉工和轮机员，一窝蜂冲上栈桥，从若有所思的阿尔迈耶身边跑过去，从我眼前消失了。白茫茫的雾把他们吞没；河上下游数英里之内仿佛又只剩下深深的寂静。阿尔迈耶依旧一言不发，开始攀登上船，我从驾驶台下来，到后甲板去迎他。

===Original===
“Would you mind telling the captain that I want to see him very particularly?” he asked me, in a low tone, letting his eyes stray all over the place.

===Chinese===
「劳驾你告诉船长，我有特别要紧的事要见他。」他低声对我说，眼睛朝四处乱瞟。

===Original===
“Very well. I will go and see.”

===Chinese===
「好吧。我去说。」

===Original===
With the door of his cabin wide open, Captain C﻿—﻿—, just back from the bathroom, big and broad-chested, was brushing his thick, damp, iron-grey hair with two large brushes.

===Chinese===
C——船长舱门大敞着，他刚从浴室回来，高大魁伟，胸膛宽厚，正用两把大刷子刷着他那头浓密、潮湿的铁灰色头发。

===Original===
“Mr. Almayer told me he wanted to see you very particularly, sir.”

===Chinese===
「阿尔迈耶先生告诉我，他有特别要紧的事要见您，船长。」

===Original===
Saying these words I smiled. I don’t know why I smiled except that it seemed absolutely impossible to mention Almayer’s name without a smile of a sort. It had not to be necessarily a mirthful smile. Turning his head towards me Captain C﻿—﻿— smiled too, rather joylessly.

===Chinese===
说这话时我微笑了。我不知道自己为什么微笑，只因为提起阿尔迈耶的名字而不带一丝笑意，似乎断无可能。那笑倒不必是欢快的笑。C——船长朝我转过头来，也笑了，笑得相当无趣。

===Original===
“The pony got away from him﻿—eh?”

===Chinese===
「小马从他手里跑掉了——嗯？」

===Original===
“Yes, sir. He did.”

===Chinese===
「是的，船长。跑掉了。」

===Original===
“Where is he?”

===Chinese===
「它在哪儿？」

===Original===
“Goodness only knows.”

===Chinese===
「只有天知道。」

===Original===
“No. I mean Almayer. Let him come along.”

===Chinese===
「不。我是说阿尔迈耶。让他过来吧。」

===Original===
The captain’s stateroom opening straight on deck under the bridge, I had only to beckon from the doorway to Almayer, who had remained aft, with downcast eyes, on the very spot where I had left him. He strolled up moodily, shook hands, and at once asked permission to shut the cabin door.

===Chinese===
船长的舱室直通驾驶台底下的甲板，我只消站在门口朝阿尔迈耶招招手——他一直待在船尾我离开他的那个地方，垂着眼。他郁郁地踱上前来，握了握手，随即请求允许关上舱门。

===Original===
“I have a pretty story to tell you,” were the last words I heard. The bitterness of tone was remarkable.

===Chinese===
「我有段妙事要讲给您听。」这是我能听见的最后一句话。那声调里的怨愤，非同小可。

===Original===
I went away from the door, of course. For the moment I had no crew on board; only the Chinaman carpenter, with a canvas bag hung round his neck and a hammer in his hand, roamed about the empty decks knocking out the wedges of the hatches and dropping them into the bag conscientiously. Having nothing to do I joined our two engineers at the door of the engine-room. It was near breakfast-time.

===Chinese===
我自然从门边走开了。眼下船上没有我的水手；只有那个中国木匠，脖子上挂着帆布袋，手里拿着锤子，在空荡荡的甲板上走来走去，敲出舱盖的楔子，一丝不苟地把它们丢进袋里。我无事可做，便到机舱门口去和我们的两位轮机员作伴。快到早餐时间了。

===Original===
“He’s turned up early, hasn’t he?” commented the second engineer, and smiled indifferently. He was an abstemious man with a good digestion and a placid, reasonable view of life even when hungry.

===Chinese===
「他来得够早的，是不是？」二管轮搭了句腔，淡然一笑。他是个饮食有节的人，消化良好，即便饿着肚子，对人生也抱一种平和而讲道理的看法。

===Original===
“Yes,” I said. “Shut up with the old man. Some very particular business.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，」我说。「跟老头子关起门来了。有什么特别要紧的事。」

===Original===
“He will spin him a damned endless yarn,” observed the chief engineer.

===Chinese===
「他准要给他讲一个没完没了的混账长篇。」轮机长说。

===Original===
He smiled rather sourly. He was dyspeptic and suffered from gnawing hunger in the morning. The second smiled broadly, a smile that made two vertical folds on his shaven cheeks. And I smiled too, but I was not exactly amused. In that man, whose name apparently could not be uttered anywhere in the Malay Archipelago without a smile, there was nothing amusing whatever. That morning he breakfasted with us silently, looking mostly into his cup. I informed him that my men came upon his pony capering in the fog on the very brink of the eight-foot-deep well in which he kept his store of guttah. The cover was off, with no one near by, and the whole of my crew just missed going heels over head into that beastly hole. Jurumudi Itam, our best quartermaster, deft at fine needlework, he who mended the ship’s flags and sewed buttons on our coats, was disabled by a kick on the shoulder.

===Chinese===
他笑得相当酸涩。他有消化不良的毛病，早晨备受饥饿的啃噬。二管轮咧嘴而笑，笑得剃得精光的脸颊上起了两道竖纹。我也笑了，但并不觉得真有多好笑。在那个名字在整个马来群岛显然无法不带着一丝微笑被提起的人身上，实在没有一丝可笑之处。那天早晨他同我们一道用早餐，默默无言，多半盯着自己的杯子。我告诉他，我的人手在雾里撞见他的小马，正在那口八英尺深的井口边缘腾跃撒欢，井里贮藏着他的古塔胶存货。井盖敞着，四下无人，我全体船员险些一头栽进那个该死的窟窿。我们最好的舵手尤鲁穆迪·伊塔姆，做精细针线活的巧手，替船上缝补船旗、给我们外套钉纽扣的那位，肩上挨了一蹄，干不了活了。

===Original===
Both remorse and gratitude seemed foreign to Almayer’s character. He mumbled:

===Chinese===
悔意与感激，在阿尔迈耶的品性里似乎都是外来物。他咕哝道：

===Original===
“Do you mean that pirate fellow?”

===Chinese===
「你是说那个海盗模样的家伙？」

===Original===
“What pirate fellow? The man has been in the ship eleven years,” I said indignantly.

===Chinese===
「什么海盗模样的家伙？这人在船上干了十一年了。」我愤愤地说。

===Original===
“It’s his looks,” Almayer muttered for all apology.

===Chinese===
「就是他那副长相。」阿尔迈耶咕哝了一句，算是道歉。

===Original===
The sun had eaten up the fog. From where we sat under the after awning we could see in the distance the pony tied up in front of Almayer’s house, to a post of the verandah. We were silent for a long time. All at once Almayer, alluding evidently to the subject of his conversation in the captain’s cabin, exclaimed anxiously across the table:

===Chinese===
太阳把雾吃掉了。从后甲板遮阳篷下我们坐的地方望出去，远远可以看见那匹小马拴在阿尔迈耶的房子前面，系在游廊的一根柱子上。我们沉默了很久。阿尔迈耶突然隔着桌子焦虑地喊出声来，指的显然是他在船长舱里密谈的那件事：

===Original===
“I really don’t know what I can do now!”

===Chinese===
「我真不知道现在我还能怎么办！」

===Original===
Captain C﻿—﻿— only raised his eyebrows at him, and got up from his chair. We dispersed to our duties, but Almayer, half-dressed as he was in his cretonne pyjamas and the thin cotton singlet, remained on board, lingering near the gangway as though he could not make up his mind whether to go home or stay with us for good. Our Chinamen boys gave him side glances as they went to and fro; and Ah Sing, our young chief steward, the handsomest and most sympathetic of Chinamen, catching my eye, nodded knowingly at his burly back. In the course of the morning I approached him for a moment.

===Chinese===
C——船长只朝他扬了扬眉毛，就从椅子里站起身。我们各自散去忙各人的活，阿尔迈耶却仍留在船上，身上还是那身印花布睡裤和薄棉汗衫的半装束，在舷梯旁流连，仿佛拿不定主意是回家去，还是索性跟我们长住下去。我们的中国侍童来来往往，都拿眼角瞟他；我们年轻的大管事阿盛，中国人里最英俊、最和气的一个，接住我的目光，会意地朝他壮实的背影点了点头。那天上午的某个时候，我走近他待了片刻。

===Original===
“Well, Mr. Almayer,” I addressed him easily, “you haven’t started on your letters yet.”

===Chinese===
「怎么样，阿尔迈耶先生，」我随意地同他搭话，「您的信还没开始拆呢。」

===Original===
We had brought him his mail and he had held the bundle in his hand ever since we got up from breakfast. He glanced at it when I spoke and, for a moment, it looked as if he were on the point of opening his fingers and letting the whole lot fall overboard. I believe he was tempted to do so. I shall never forget that man afraid of his letters.

===Chinese===
邮件是我们带给他的，从早餐起身起，他就把那一捆一直攥在手里。我一开口他就瞥了它一眼，有一瞬间，他看上去简直就要张开五指，把整捆东西撒进海里。我相信他确实受到了这样的诱惑。我永远忘不了那个害怕自己信件的人。

===Original===
“Have you been long out from Europe?” he asked me.

===Chinese===
「离开欧洲很久了吗？」他问我。

===Original===
“Not very. Not quite eight months,” I told him. “I left a ship in Samarang with a hurt back and have been in the hospital in Singapore some weeks.”

===Chinese===
「不久。不满八个月，」我告诉他。「我在三宝垄下了船，伤了背，在新加坡的医院里躺了几个星期。」

===Original===
He sighed.

===Chinese===
他叹了口气。

===Original===
“Trade is very bad here.”

===Chinese===
「这地方的买卖很不景气。」

===Original===
“Indeed!”

===Chinese===
「是吗！」

===Original===
“Hopeless!﻿ ﻿… See these geese?”

===Chinese===
「没指望了！……瞧见这些鹅没有？」

===Original===
With the hand holding the letters he pointed out to me what resembled a patch of snow creeping and swaying across the distant part of his compound. It disappeared behind some bushes.

===Chinese===
他用握着信的那只手，指给我看一样东西，像一片白雪，在他宅地远处的那头缓缓爬动、摇曳。它消失在几丛灌木后面。

===Original===
“The only geese on the East Coast,” Almayer informed me in a perfunctory mutter without a spark of faith, hope or pride. Thereupon, with the same absence of any sort of sustaining spirit, he declared his intention to select a fat bird and send him on board for us not later than next day.

===Chinese===
「东海岸仅有的鹅。」阿尔迈耶用敷衍的咕哝告诉我，没有一丝信念、希望或自豪的火星。接着，他怀着同样毫无精神支撑的模样，宣布他打算挑一只肥的，最迟明天给我们送上船来。

===Original===
I had heard of these largesses before. He conferred a goose as if it were a sort of Court decoration given only to the tried friends of the house. I had expected more pomp in the ceremony. The gift had surely its special quality, multiple and rare. From the only flock on the East Coast! He did not make half enough of it. That man did not understand his opportunities. However, I thanked him at some length.

===Chinese===
这种慷慨的馈赠我早有耳闻。他颁授一只鹅，仿佛那是某种宫廷勋章，只授予这家久经考验的朋友。我原以为仪式会更隆重些。这件礼物确有它独特的品质，多重而珍稀。出自东海岸唯一的鹅群！他自己却连一半的排场都没摆。那人不懂得自己的机会。不过，我还是郑重其事地谢了他半天。

===Original===
“You see,” he interrupted abruptly in a very peculiar tone, “the worst of this country is that one is not able to realise﻿ ﻿… it’s impossible to realise﻿ ﻿…” His voice sank into a languid mutter. “And when one has very large interests﻿ ﻿… very important interests﻿ ﻿…” he finished faintly﻿ ﻿… “up the river.”

===Chinese===
「你瞧，」他忽然用一种非常特别的声调打断我，「这个国家最糟的是，人没法意识到……不可能意识到……」他的声音低成了一声倦怠的咕哝。「而当一个人有着极大的利害……极重要的利害……」他有气无力地收了尾……「在上游。」
