===Original===
We looked at each other. He astonished me by giving a start and making a very queer grimace.

===Chinese===
我们对望了一眼。他猛地一惊，做了个非常古怪的鬼脸，叫我吃了一惊。

===Original===
“Well, I must be off,” he burst out hurriedly. “So long!”

===Chinese===
「好了，我得走了。」他仓促地冒出一句。「回见！」

===Original===
At the moment of stepping over the gangway he checked himself, though, to give me a mumbled invitation to dine at his house that evening with my captain, an invitation which I accepted. I don’t think it could have been possible for me to refuse.

===Chinese===
跨上舷梯的那一刻，他却顿了顿，含含糊糊地邀我当晚带着船长去他家吃饭——这个邀请我接受了。我想，要我拒绝怕是不可能的。

===Original===
I like the worthy folk who will talk to you of the exercise of free will “at any rate for practical purposes.” Free, is it? For practical purposes! Bosh! How could I have refused to dine with that man? I did not refuse simply because I could not refuse. Curiosity, a healthy desire for a change of cooking, common civility, the talk and the smiles of the previous twenty days, every condition of my existence at that moment and place made irresistibly for acceptance; and, crowning all that, there was the ignorance, the ignorance, I say, the fatal want of foreknowledge to counterbalance these imperative conditions of the problem. A refusal would have appeared perverse and insane. Nobody unless a surly lunatic would have refused. But if I had not got to know Almayer pretty well it is almost certain there would never have been a line of mine in print.

===Chinese===
我倒喜欢那些可敬的人们，他们会同你大谈自由意志的运用，「至少就实际目的而言」。自由，是吗？就实际目的而言！胡扯！我怎么可能拒绝与那个人共进晚餐呢？我没有拒绝，只因为我无法拒绝。好奇心，换换口味的正常欲望，寻常的礼貌，此前二十天里的言谈与微笑，我彼时彼地生存境况的每一个条件，都不可抗拒地指向接受；而压倒这一切的，还有无知，无知啊，我说，就是那致命的缺乏先见之明，没有它来抵销这道难题里这些强制性的条件。拒绝倒会显得乖张而疯狂。除非是个横眉竖眼的疯子，谁也不会拒绝。可是，倘若我没有相当深入地认识阿尔迈耶，几乎可以断定，世上永远不会有一行我的文字变成铅字。

===Original===
I accepted then﻿—and I am paying yet the price of my sanity. The possessor of the only flock of geese on the East Coast is responsible for the existence of some fourteen volumes, so far. The number of geese he had called into being under adverse climatic conditions was considerably more than fourteen. The tale of volumes will never overtake the counting of heads, I am safe to say; but my ambitions point not exactly that way, and whatever the pangs the toil of writing has cost me I have always thought kindly of Almayer.

===Chinese===
于是我接受了——而直到今天，我还在为我的神志健全支付代价。那位东海岸唯一鹅群的主人，要对迄今大约十四卷书的存在负责。他在不利气候条件下催生出的鹅，数目却远不止十四只。我敢说，书卷的数目永远赶不上鹅头的清点；不过我的抱负所指的并非那个方向，而且无论写作的辛劳让我付出过怎样的痛楚，我一向对阿尔迈耶怀着好感。

===Original===
I wonder, had he known anything of it, what his attitude would have been? This is something not to be discovered in this world. But if we ever meet in the Elysian Fields﻿—where I cannot depict him to myself otherwise than attended in the distance by his flock of geese (birds sacred to Jupiter)﻿—and he addresses me in the stillness of that passionless region, neither light nor darkness, neither sound nor silence, and heaving endlessly with billowy mists from the impalpable multitudes of the swarming dead, I think I know what answer to make.

===Chinese===
我想，倘若他对这一切有所知晓，他会是种什么态度？这是此生此世无从探明的事了。但倘若我们有朝一日在极乐原野（Elysian Fields）相逢——在那里，我给自己描绘的他，只能是这样一幅图景：远处跟着他的那群鹅（那是奉献给朱庇特的神鸟）——倘若他在那个无情无欲的疆域的静寂中对我说起话来，那疆域无光亦无暗，无声亦无寂，因亡者不可触知的大群熙熙攘攘而如雾涛般无尽起伏，我想我知道该怎样回答。

===Original===
I would say, after listening courteously to the unvibrating tone of his measured remonstrances, which should not disturb, of course, the solemn eternity of stillness in the least﻿—I would say something like this:

===Chinese===
我会先客客气气地听完他那平板不惊、字斟句酌的怨责——这怨责自不应惊扰那庄严的永恒静寂分毫——然后，我会说些这样的话：

===Original===
“It is true, Almayer, that in the world below I have converted your name to my own uses. But that is a very small larceny. What’s in a name, O Shade? If so much of your old mortal weakness clings to you yet as to make you feel aggrieved (it was the note of your earthly voice, Almayer), then, I entreat you, seek speech without delay with our sublime fellow-Shade﻿—with him who, in his transient existence as a poet, commented upon the smell of the rose. He will comfort you. You came to me stripped of all prestige by men’s queer smiles and the disrespectful chatter of every vagrant trader in the Islands. Your name was the common property of the winds: it, as it were, floated naked over the waters about the Equator. I wrapped round its unhonoured form the royal mantle of the tropics and have essayed to put into the hollow sound the very anguish of paternity﻿—feats which you did not demand from me﻿—but remember that all the toil and all the pain were mine. In your earthly life you haunted me, Almayer. Consider that this was taking a great liberty. Since you were always complaining of being lost to the world, you should remember that if I had not believed enough in your existence to let you haunt my rooms in Bessborough Gardens, you would have been much more lost. You affirm that had I been capable of looking at you with a more perfect detachment and a greater simplicity, I might have perceived better the inward marvellousness which, you insist, attended your career upon that tiny pinpoint of light, hardly visible far, far below us, where both our graves lie. No doubt! But reflect, O complaining Shade! that this was not so much my fault as your crowning misfortune. I believed in you in the only way it was possible for me to believe. It was not worthy of your merits? So be it. But you were always an unlucky man, Almayer. Nothing was ever quite worthy of you. What made you so real to me was that you held this lofty theory with some force of conviction and with an admirable consistency.”

===Chinese===
「不错，阿尔迈耶，在下界，我把你的名字挪作了我自己的用场。可那不过是桩很小的偷窃。一个名字里有什么呢，哦，阴灵？倘若你身上至今还黏附着那么多旧日的凡人弱点，以至于觉得委屈（那原是你尘世嗓音的调子，阿尔迈耶），那么我恳求你，速速去找我们那位崇高的阴灵同侪说话——找那位在昙花一现的诗人生涯里谈论过玫瑰之香气的先哲。他会安慰你的。你到我这儿来时，早已被世人古怪的微笑和群岛上每个流浪商贩不知敬重的闲嗑牙剥得一丝威望不剩。你的名字是风的公共财产：可以说，它赤条条漂浮在赤道一带的海面上。我给这不受尊敬的形体裹上了热带的王者披风，还试着往那空洞的音响里注入为人父者的切肤之痛——这些功劳你并未曾要求于我——可是请记住，一切的辛劳、一切的苦痛，都是我的。在尘世的一生里，你缠住了我，阿尔迈耶。想想看，这实在是大大地放肆。你既然总抱怨自己已被世人遗忘，那就该记得，倘若不是我对你的存在信得足够，容你在贝斯伯勒花园我的房间里出没，你被遗忘得还要彻底得多。你断言，倘若我能够以更完全的超脱、更大的单纯来看你，我或许会更好地看出那种内在的非凡——你坚称那非凡始终伴随着你在那一星微光上的生涯，那光点在我们下方遥远遥远之处，几乎望不见，我们两人的坟墓都在那里。无疑是这样！可是想想吧，哦，爱抱怨的阴灵！这与其说是我的过错，不如说是你压轴的大不幸。我以我唯一可能的方式信了你。这方式配不上你的功德？那就这样吧。可你从来就是个不走运的人，阿尔迈耶。从来没有什么配得上过你。而使你在我眼里如此真实的，正是你抱着这套崇高的理论，怀着几分执拗的确信，怀着一种可敬的始终如一。」

===Original===
It is with some such words translated into the proper shadowy expressions that I am prepared to placate Almayer in the Elysian Abode of Shades, since it has come to pass that having parted many years ago, we are never to meet again in this world.

===Chinese===
翻译成恰如其分的幽冥之语，怀着这样一些话，我随时准备在亡灵的极乐居所里安抚阿尔迈耶——既然事已至此：我们多年前一别，此生此世再无相见之日。

## V / 五

===Original===
In the career of the most unliterary of writers, in the sense that literary ambition had never entered the world of his imagination, the coming into existence of the first book is quite an inexplicable event. In my own case I cannot trace it back to any mental or psychological cause which one could point out and hold to. The greatest of my gifts being a consummate capacity for doing nothing, I cannot even point to boredom as a rational stimulus for taking up a pen. The pen at any rate was there, and there is nothing wonderful in that. Everybody keeps a pen (the cold steel of our days) in his rooms in this enlightened age of penny stamps and halfpenny postcards. In fact, this was the epoch when by means of postcard and pen Mr. Gladstone had made the reputation of a novel or two. And I too had a pen rolling about somewhere﻿—the seldom-used, the reluctantly-taken-up pen of a sailor ashore, the pen rugged with the dried ink of abandoned attempts, of answers delayed longer than decency permitted, of letters begun with infinite reluctance and put off suddenly till next day﻿—till next week as likely as not! The neglected, uncared-for pen, flung away at the slightest provocation, and under the stress of dire necessity hunted for without enthusiasm, in a perfunctory, grumpy worry, in the “Where the devil *is* the beastly thing gone to?” ungracious spirit. Where indeed! It might have been reposing behind the sofa for a day or so. My landlady’s anemic daughter (as Ollendorff would have expressed it), though commendably neat, had a lordly, careless manner of approaching her domestic duties. Or it might even be resting delicately poised on its point by the side of the table-leg, and when picked up show a gaping, inefficient beak which would have discouraged any man of literary instincts. But not me! “Never mind. This will do.”

===Chinese===
在这样一个最无文人气的作家的生涯里——所谓无文人气，是说文学的抱负从不曾进入过他想象的世界——第一本书的诞生实在是一桩无从解释的事。就我自己而言，我无法把它追溯到任何可以指认、可以坚执的心智或心理成因。我最大的天赋是把无所事事做臻于至境，所以就连无聊，我也指认不成提笔的合理刺激。笔好歹是在的，这也没有什么可奇怪的。在这个有便士邮票和半便士明信片的启蒙时代，人人房间里都备着一支笔（我们这时代冷冰冰的钢笔）。事实上，正是在那个年代，格拉德斯通先生靠明信片和笔，捧红过一两部小说。我也有一支笔在什么地方滚着——一支水手上岸人难得一用、勉为其难才拿起的笔，一支结着干墨痂的笔，结着屡次半途而废的痂、结着拖过了体面期限的回信的痂、结着起了头又无限勉强、忽然搁到明天——多半是搁到下个星期——的信的痂。这支没人理会、没人爱惜的笔，稍一惹它便被丢开，等到情势所迫非用不可，又得在一种敷衍的、悻悻的烦躁里去找，找的时候怀着「这挨千刀的东西到底滚到哪儿去了？」的不客气心情。到底滚到哪儿去了呢！它可能已在沙发背后静卧了一两天了。我房东太太的那位贫血的女儿（正如奥伦多夫会表述的那样），虽然整洁得可嘉，操持家务的派头却是一种贵妇式的漫不经心。它也可能正优雅地尖头朝下，倚在桌腿旁立着，捡起来一看，笔尖张着一张无能为力的豁口，足以叫任何有文学本能的人心灰。可我不！「不碍事。这支能用。」

===Original===
O days without guile! If anybody had told me then that a devoted household, having a generally exaggerated idea of my talents and importance, would be put into a state of tremor and flurry by the fuss I would make because of a suspicion that somebody had touched my sacrosanct pen of authorship, I would have never deigned as much as the contemptuous smile of unbelief. There are imaginings too unlikely for any kind of notice, too wild for indulgence itself, too absurd for a smile. Perhaps, had that seer of the future been a friend, I should have been secretly saddened. “Alas!” I would have thought, looking at him with an unmoved face, “the poor fellow is going mad.”

===Chinese===
哦，那些毫无机心的日子！倘若那时有人告诉我，有一个满怀忠爱的家庭——他们对我才华与重要性的估量向来偏高——竟会因为我疑心有人动了作家的我那支神圣不可侵犯的笔而小题大作，被搅得战战兢兢、手忙脚乱，我连一个鄙夷的不信的微笑也懒得赏给他。有些想象太不可能，不值得任何理会；太荒唐，连纵容它一下都不配；太荒谬，连一笑都不值。倘若那位预见未来的先知恰好是位朋友，我或许会在暗地里难过。「唉！」我会神色不动地望着他想，「这可怜的家伙要疯了。」

===Original===
I would have been, without doubt, saddened; for in this world where the journalists read the signs of the sky, and the wind of heaven itself, blowing where it listeth, does so under the prophetical management of the Meteorological Office, but where the secret of human hearts cannot be captured either by prying or praying, it was infinitely more likely that the sanest of my friends should nurse the germ of incipient madness than that I should turn into a writer of tales.

===Chinese===
我无疑会难过；因为在这个世界上，新闻记者们观天象断风云，连天上的风要往哪里吹——它本随心所欲地吹——也得听气象局的预言调度，而人心的秘密却无论窥探还是祈祷都捕捉不到；在这样一个世界上，我最头脑清醒的朋友们身上潜伏着初发的疯狂胚芽，也远比我自己变成一个写小说的人要可能无限倍。

===Original===
To survey with wonder the changes of one’s own self is a fascinating pursuit for idle hours. The field is so wide, the surprises so varied, the subject so full of unprofitable but curious hints as to the work of unseen forces, that one does not weary easily of it. I am not speaking here of megalomaniacs who rest uneasy under the crown of their unbounded conceit﻿—who really never rest in this world, and when out of it go on fretting and fuming on the straitened circumstances of their last habitation, where all men must lie in obscure equality. Neither am I thinking of those ambitious minds who, always looking forward to some aim of aggrandisement, can spare no time for a detached, impersonal glance upon themselves.

===Chinese===
以惊奇的眼光审视自身的种种变化，是闲暇时刻一桩迷人的营生。这天地如此广阔，意外如此纷繁，题目里又满是关于那些看不见的力量如何运作的无益而有趣的暗示，人不容易对它生厌。我这里说的不是那些自大狂——他们在自己那顶漫无边际的自负王冠下坐卧不宁——这些人在这世上从不曾安歇，出了这世道，还要在末一处居所的局促境况里继续烦恼咆哮，在那居所里，人人躺下时一律默默无闻、彼此平等。我想到的也不是那些野心勃勃的心智，他们永远瞻望着某个自我扩张的目标，抽不出一点时间，对自己投去超然的、非个人的一瞥。

===Original===
And that’s a pity. They are unlucky. These two kinds, together with the much larger band of the totally unimaginative, of those unfortunate beings in whose empty and unseeing gaze (as a great French writer has put it) “the whole universe vanishes into blank nothingness,” miss, perhaps, the true task of us men whose day is short on this earth, the abode of conflicting opinions. The ethical view of the universe involves us at last in so many cruel and absurd contradictions, where the last vestiges of faith, hope, charity, and even of reason itself, seem ready to perish, that I have come to suspect that the aim of creation cannot be ethical at all. I would fondly believe that its object is purely spectacular: a spectacle for awe, love, adoration, or hate, if you like, but in this view﻿—and in this view alone﻿—never for despair! Those visions, delicious or poignant, are a moral end in themselves. The rest is our affair﻿—the laughter, the tears, the tenderness, the indignation, the high tranquillity of a steeled heart, the detached curiosity of a subtle mind﻿—that’s our affair! And the unwearied self-forgetful attention to every phase of the living universe reflected in our consciousness may be our appointed task on this earth. A task in which fate has perhaps engaged nothing of us except our conscience, gifted with a voice in order to bear true testimony to the visible wonder, the haunting terror, the infinite passion and the illimitable serenity; to the supreme law and the abiding mystery of the sublime spectacle.

===Chinese===
而这是一件憾事。他们是不幸的。这两种人，加上人数多得多的一群全无想象力的人——那些不幸的家伙，他们空洞无神的凝视里（正如一位伟大的法国作家所言）「整个宇宙化为一片空无」——错过的，也许正是我们这些在世上时日无多的人的真正使命，在这块众说纷纭的栖居之地上。宇宙的伦理观最终把我们卷入那么多残忍而荒谬的自相矛盾之中，在那里，信仰、希望、仁爱的最后残迹，甚至理性本身，都仿佛随时会熄灭，以至于我渐渐怀疑，创世的目的根本不可能是伦理的。我宁愿相信，它的目的纯粹是观赏的：一场供敬畏、供热爱、供崇拜或供憎恨的奇观，随你怎么说，但在这个观念里——唯有在这个观念里——决没有绝望的份！那些或甘美或惨痛的景象，其本身就是道德的目的。其余是我们的事——笑，泪，温柔，义愤，铁石心肠的高度宁静，敏锐心智的超然好奇——那是我们的事！而对映入我们意识中的活生生宇宙的每一面貌保持一种不倦的、忘我的关注，也许正是我们在世上领受的使命。在这使命里，命运对我们要求的，或许只有良心——它被赋予了声音，以便为那可见的奇迹、那萦绕不去的恐怖、那无限的激情、那无边的宁静作真实的见证；为那至高的法则、那壮丽景观中恒在的奥秘作见证。

===Original===
*Chi lo sà?* It may be true. In this view there is room for every religion except for the inverted creed of impiety, the mask and cloak of arid despair; for every joy and every sorrow, for every fair dream, for every charitable hope. The great aim is to remain true to the emotions called out of the deep encircled by the firmament of stars, whose infinite numbers and awful distances may move us to laughter or tears (was it the Walrus or the Carpenter, in the poem, who “wept to see such quantities of sand”?), or, again, to a properly steeled heart, may matter nothing at all.

===Chinese===
*Chi lo sà?*（谁知道呢？）这话也许不错。在这个观念里，一切宗教都有容身之地，唯独容不下那不信神的倒错教义——那干涸绝望的面具与斗篷；容得下每一份欢乐与每一份哀愁，每一个美梦，每一种悲悯的希望。伟大的目标，是对那被满天星斗环抱的深渊唤起的情感保持忠贞，星辰无穷的数目与可畏的距离，可以令我们或笑或泪（诗里那一位「望着这么多的沙哭泣」的，是海象还是木匠来着？），或者，对一颗恰如其分地硬起来的心而言，也可以全然无足轻重。

===Original===
The casual quotation, which had suggested itself out of a poem full of merit, leads me to remark that in the conception of a purely spectacular universe, where inspiration of every sort has a rational existence, the artist of every kind finds a natural place; and amongst them the poet as the seer *par excellence*. Even the writer of prose, who in his less noble and more toilsome task should be a man with the steeled heart, is worthy of a place, providing he looks on with undimmed eyes and keeps laughter out of his voice, let who will laugh or cry. Yes! Even he, the prose artist of fiction, which after all is but truth often dragged out of a well and clothed in the painted robe of imagined phrases﻿—even he has his place amongst kings, demagogues, priests, charlatans, dukes, giraffes, Cabinet Ministers, Fabians, bricklayers, apostles, ants, scientists, Kaffirs, soldiers, sailors, elephants, lawyers, dandies, microbes and constellations of a universe whose amazing spectacle is a moral end in itself.

===Chinese===
这句顺口引来的诗——它出自一首颇有可取的诗——引得我说：在纯粹观赏性的宇宙这一构想里，那里一切种类的灵感都有其理性的存在，每一种艺术家都找到了天然的位置；而其中诗人便是那超群绝伦的先知。就连散文作家——他那较为不高贵、较为辛苦的行当要求他有一颗铁打的心——也配得一席之地，只要他睁着不昏花的眼睛旁观，只要他不让笑声进入自己的嗓音，管别人去笑去哭。是的！就连他，小说的散文艺术家，也自有其位置——小说归根到底不过是常常从井里拖出来、再裹上想象语句织成的彩衣的真相——就连他，也与国王、煽动家、教士、骗子、公爵、长颈鹿、内阁大臣、费边社社员、砌砖匠、使徒、蚂蚁、科学家、卡菲尔人、士兵、水手、大象、律师、纨绔子、微生物，以及宇宙的种种星座同列；这宇宙的奇观，其本身便是一种道德的目的。

===Original===
Here I perceive (speaking without offence) the reader assuming a subtle expression, as if the cat were out of the bag. I take the novelist’s freedom to observe the reader’s mind formulating the exclamation, “That’s it! The fellow talks *pro domo*.”

===Chinese===
说到这里，我察觉（绝无冒犯之意）读者脸上浮现出一个微妙的表情，仿佛猫已从袋子里钻了出来。我且行使小说家的自由，看着读者心里正在成形的那句感叹：「就是嘛！这家伙是在 *pro domo*（为己辩护）呢。」

===Original===
Indeed it was not the intention! When I shouldered the bag I was not aware of the cat inside. But, after all, why not? The fair courtyards of the House of Art are thronged by many humble retainers. And there is no retainer so devoted as he who is allowed to sit on the doorstep. The fellows who have got inside are apt to think too much of themselves. This last remark, I beg to state, is not malicious within the definition of the law of libel. It’s fair comment on a matter of public interest. But never mind. *Pro domo.* So be it. For his house *tant que vous voudrez*. And yet in truth I was by no means anxious to justify my existence. The attempt would have been not only needless and absurd, but almost inconceivable, in a purely spectacular universe, where no such disagreeable necessity can possibly arise. It is sufficient for me to say (and I am saying it at some length in these pages): *J’ai vécu*. I have existed, obscure amongst the wonders and terrors of my time, as the Abbé Sieyès, the original utterer of the quoted words, had managed to exist through the violences, the crimes, and the enthusiasms of the French Revolution. *J’ai vécu*, as I apprehend most of us manage to exist, missing all along the varied forms of destruction by a hair’s-breadth, saving my body, that’s clear, and perhaps my soul also, but not without some damage here and there to the fine edge of my conscience, that heirloom of the ages, of the race, of the group, of the family, colourable and plastic, fashioned by the words, the looks, the acts, and even by the silences and abstentions surrounding one’s childhood; tinged in a complete scheme of delicate shades and crude colours by the inherited traditions, beliefs, or prejudices﻿—unaccountable, despotic, persuasive, and often, in its texture, romantic.

===Chinese===
其实那绝非本意！我扛起那只袋子时，并不知道里面藏着猫。可是说到底，又有什么不可以呢？艺术之宅的清雅庭院里，本就挤满了众多卑微的仆从。而没有哪个仆从，比获准坐在门槛上的那个更忠心耿耿。进到里面去的那班人，倒容易把自己看得过高。最后这句话，我谨此声明，在诽谤法的定义之内并无恶意。这是对一桩公共事务的正当评论。不过不去管它。*Pro domo*。就是这样吧。为了他的宅子，*tant que vous voudrez*（悉听尊便）。然而实情是，我丝毫无意为自己的存在辩护。在一个纯粹观赏性的宇宙里，这种不愉快的必要根本无从产生，那样的企图不仅多余、荒唐，而且近乎不可思议。对我说来，说一句（而且我正在这几页里相当絮叨地说着）也就够了：*J'ai vécu*（我活过了）。我存在过，在我那个时代的奇观与恐怖之间默默无闻地存在过，正如那句被引的话的最初说出者西哀士神父，在法国大革命的暴力、罪愆与狂热之间设法存在过来一样。*J'ai vécu*，依我想，我们大多数人都是这样设法存在的：一路间不容发地躲过种种形式的毁灭，保全了肉体，这很明显，或许也保全了灵魂，却不无处处磕损着我良心那敏锐的锋刃——那良心是岁月的传家宝，是种族的、族群的、家庭的传家宝，可染可塑，由围着一个童年的言辞、目光、举动、乃至沉默与回避塑造而成；又由承袭来的传统、信仰或偏见，染上一整套细腻层次与粗粝色彩的图样——无从索解，专横霸道，又善于说服，而且就其质地而言，常常是浪漫的。

===Original===
And often romantic!﻿ ﻿… The matter in hand, however, is to keep these reminiscences from turning into confessions, a form of literary activity discredited by Jean Jacques Rousseau on account of the extreme thoroughness he brought to the work of justifying his own existence; for that such was his purpose is palpably, even grossly, visible to an unprejudiced eye. But then, you see, the man was not a writer of fiction. He was an artless moralist, as is clearly demonstrated by his anniversaries being celebrated with marked emphasis by the heirs of the French Revolution, which was not a political movement at all, but a great outburst of morality. He had no imagination, as the most casual perusal of *Émile* will prove. He was no novelist, whose first virtue is the exact understanding of the limits traced by the reality of his time to the play of his invention. Inspiration comes from the earth, which has a past, a history, a future, not from the cold and immutable heaven. A writer of imaginative prose (even more than any other sort of artist) stands confessed in his works. His conscience, his deeper sense of things, lawful and unlawful, gives him his attitude before the world. Indeed, everyone who puts pen to paper for the reading of strangers (unless a moralist, who, generally speaking, has no conscience except the one he is at pains to produce for the use of others) can speak of nothing else. It is M. Anatole France, the most eloquent and just of French prose-writers, who says that we must recognise at last that, “failing the resolution to hold our peace, we can only talk of ourselves.”

===Chinese===
而且常常是浪漫的！……不过，眼下的事，是别让这些回忆变成忏悔录。忏悔录这种文字营生，已经让-雅克·卢梭败坏了名声，因为他给自己生存辩护的工程做得太过彻底；他的目的正是如此，这在不带偏见的眼睛看来明明白白，甚至到了刺眼的地步。可是你要知道，此人并不是写小说的。他是位朴拙的道德家，这一点从他的忌辰受到法国大革命的继承者们隆重的纪念，即可明白证明——那场革命根本不是什么政治运动，而是道德的一场大爆发。他没有想象力，把《爱弥儿》（*Émile*）随便翻上几页即可证明。他不是小说家；小说家的第一美德，是确切懂得他的时代的现实给自己想象力的驰骋划下的界限。灵感来自大地——大地有过去，有历史，有未来——而不是来自那冰冷不变的天穹。想象性散文的作家（比任何别的艺术家都更甚）在自己的作品里袒露无遗。他的良心，他对合法与非法之事的更深层的感觉，决定了他面对世界的姿态。的确，凡是执笔写给陌生人读的人（道德家除外，因为道德家照例没有良心，只有一副他费心为他人使用而制作出来的良心），除了它，别的什么也谈不上。正是阿纳托尔·法朗士先生，法国散文作家中最雄辩、最公正的一位，说过：我们必须最终承认，「除非下决心保持沉默，我们只能谈自己」。

===Original===
This remark, if I remember rightly, was made in the course of a sparring match with the late Ferdinand Brunetière over the principles and rules of literary criticism. As was fitting for a man to whom we owe the memorable saying, “The good critic is he who relates the adventures of his soul amongst masterpieces,” M. Anatole France maintained that there were no rules and no principles. And that may be very true. Rules, principles and standards die and vanish every day. Perhaps they are all dead and vanished by this time. These, if ever, are the brave, free days of destroyed landmarks, while the ingenious minds are busy inventing the forms of the new beacons which, it is consoling to think, will be set up presently in the old places. But what is interesting to a writer is the possession of an inward certitude that literary criticism will never die, for man (so variously defined) is, before everything else, a critical animal. And, as long as distinguished minds are ready to treat it in the spirit of high adventure, literary criticism shall appeal to us with all the charm and wisdom of a well-told tale of personal experience.

===Chinese===
这句话，如果我没记错，是他在与已故的费迪南·布吕内蒂埃就文学批评的原则与规则过招时说的。对一位我们有负于他那句名言——「好的批评家，就是讲述自己的灵魂在杰作之间的历险的人」——的人，阿纳托尔·法朗士先生主张无规则、无原则，再合适不过。这话或许很对。规则、原则与标准天天死去、天天消逝。或许到此刻它们早已死绝、消逝尽了。如果真有那么一天，那如今正是路碑尽毁的勇敢而自由的日子，而机巧的心智们正忙着发明新灯塔的样式——想想也令人宽慰：它们很快就会立在老地方了。但对一个作家来说，有趣的是内心确知文学批评永远不会死，因为人（定义纷纭的那个动物）首先是种爱评断的动物。而且，只要有杰出的心智乐于本着高度历险的精神来从事它，文学批评就将以一段讲述得很好的亲身经历的一切魅力与智慧，向我们发出召唤。

===Original===
For Englishmen especially, of all the races of the earth, a task, any task, undertaken in an adventurous spirit acquires the merit of romance. But the critics as a rule exhibit but little of an adventurous spirit. They take risks, of course﻿—one can hardly live without that. The daily bread is served out to us (however sparingly) with a pinch of salt. Otherwise one would get sick of the diet one prays for, and that would be not only improper, but impious. From impiety of that or any other kind﻿—save us! An ideal of reserved manner, adhered to from a sense of proprieties, from shyness, perhaps, or caution, or simply from weariness, induces, I suspect, some writers of criticism to conceal the adventurous side of their calling, and then the criticism becomes a mere “notice,” as it were the relation of a journey where nothing but the distances and the geology of a new country should be set down; the glimpses of strange beasts, the dangers of flood and field, the hair’s-breadth escapes, and the sufferings (oh, the sufferings too! I have no doubt of the sufferings) of the traveller being carefully kept out; no shady spot, no fruitful plant being ever mentioned either; so that the whole performance looks like a mere feat of agility on the part of a trained pen running in a desert. A cruel spectacle﻿—a most deplorable adventure. “Life,” in the words of an immortal thinker of, I should say, bucolic origin, but whose perishable name is lost to the worship of posterity﻿—“life is not all beer and skittles.” Neither is the writing of novels. It isn’t really. *Je vous donne ma parole d’honneur* that it﻿—is﻿—not. Not *all*. I am thus emphatic because some years ago, I remember, the daughter of a general.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
尤其是对英格兰人——普天之下诸种族中最甚——凡是本着历险精神承担的任务，无论什么任务，都能获得传奇的优点。可惜批评家们照例很少显露历险精神。风险他们当然是冒的——不冒风险几乎无法过活。我们每日的面包发到手里时（无论多么俭省）总附着一撮盐。否则人就会对自己祈求得来的口粮生腻，那不仅有失体统，而且近乎渎神。这样的渎神也罢，别的什么渎神也罢——可别沾我们！一种矜持风度的理想，出于对体统的顾念，或者出于羞怯，出于谨慎，或者干脆出于厌倦，我疑心正是它诱使一些写批评的人把自己行当里历险的一面藏起来，于是批评就成了一纸干巴巴的「报道」，好比一段游记，只该记下里程和新地方的地质；陌生野兽的惊鸿一瞥、水陆的危险、间不容发的逃脱，还有旅行者的种种苦楚（哦，苦楚也是有的！这我不怀疑），都被小心地略去；也不提一处阴凉的地角，一株结果的植物；于是整场表演，看上去不过是训练有素的笔在一篇荒漠里的敏捷把戏。残酷的奇观——一场极可叹的历险。「生活」，用一位不朽思想家的话说——我想他是田园出身的吧，只是那易朽的大名已失落在后人的敬拜之外——「生活并不全是啤酒和九柱戏」。写小说也何尝不是。真的不是。*Je vous donne ma parole d'honneur*（我以名誉担保），它——不——是。不全是。我这样斩钉截铁，是因为我记得，若干年前，有位将军的女儿。……

===Original===
Sudden revelations of the profane world must have come now and then to hermits in their cells, to the cloistered monks of Middle Ages, to lonely sages, men of science, reformers; the revelations of the world’s superficial judgment, shocking to the souls concentrated upon their own bitter labour in the cause of sanctity, or of knowledge, or of temperance, let us say, or of art, if only the art of cracking jokes or playing the flute. And thus this general’s daughter came to me﻿—or I should say one of the general’s daughters did. There were three of these bachelor ladies, of nicely graduated ages, who held a neighbouring farmhouse in a united and more or less military occupation. The eldest warred against the decay of manners in the village children, and executed frontal attacks upon the village mothers for the conquest of curtseys. It sounds futile, but it was really a war for an idea. The second skirmished and scouted all over the country; and it was that one who pushed a reconnaissance right to my very table﻿—I mean the one who wore stand-up collars. She was really calling upon my wife in the soft spirit of afternoon friendliness, but with her usual martial determination. She marched into my room swinging her stick﻿ ﻿… but no﻿—I mustn’t exaggerate. It is not my speciality. I am not a humoristic writer. In all soberness, then, all I am certain of is that she had a stick to swing.

===Chinese===
尘世的种种启示，想必也曾不时降临到修行室里的隐士、中世纪的修道僧、孤独的圣哲、科学家和改革家身上；那是尘世浮面判断的启示，对于把灵魂专注于圣洁、或知识、或节制——姑且这么说吧——或艺术（哪怕只是说笑话或吹长笛的艺术）的苦役上的人，这种启示是骇人的。就这样，那位将军家的女儿向我走来——或者说，将军家的女儿们中的一位向我走来。这样的闺中淑女有三位，年纪匀称地拉开档，以一种联合的、多少带些军旅意味的占据状态守着邻近的一座农庄。大小姐对村里孩子们礼貌的衰颓开战，为了征服屈膝礼，对村里的母亲们发起正面强攻。这听着无谓，其实是一场为理念而成的战争。二小姐在全国各地游弋侦察；正是她，把一次搜索推进到了我的书桌跟前——我说的是戴直立硬领的那位。她其实是抱着午后友情的温软情意来看我妻子的，却带着她一贯的军事决心。她挥着手杖开进我的房间……不——我不能夸张。那不是我的专长。我不是个幽默作家。那么老老实实地说吧：我能确定的只是，她确实有一根手杖可挥。

===Original===
No ditch or wall encompassed my abode. The window was open; the door too stood open to that best friend of my work, the warm, still sunshine of the wide fields. They lay around me infinitely helpful, but truth to say I had not known for weeks whether the sun shone upon the earth and whether the stars above still moved on their appointed courses. I was just then giving up some days of my allotted span to the last chapters of the novel *Nostromo*, a tale of an imaginary (but true) seaboard, which is still mentioned now and again, and indeed kindly, sometimes in connection with the word “failure” and sometimes in conjunction with the word “astonishing.” I have no opinion on this discrepancy. It’s the sort of difference that can never be settled. All I know is that, for twenty months, neglecting the common joys of life that fall to the lot of the humblest on this earth, I had, like the prophet of old, “wrestled with the Lord” for my creation, for the headlands of the coast, for the darkness of the Placid Gulf, the light on the snows, the clouds on the sky, and for the breath of life that had to be blown into the shapes of men and women, of Latin and Saxon, of Jew and Gentile. These are, perhaps, strong words, but it is difficult to characterise otherwise the intimacy and the strain of a creative effort in which mind and will and conscience are engaged to the full, hour after hour, day after day, away from the world, and to the exclusion of all that makes life really lovable and gentle﻿—something for which a material parallel can only be found in the everlasting sombre stress of the westward winter passage round Cape Horn. For that too is the wrestling of men with the might of their Creator, in a great isolation from the world, without the amenities and consolations of life, a lonely struggle under a sense of overmatched littleness, for no reward that could be adequate, but for the mere winning of a longitude. Yet a certain longitude, once won, cannot be disputed. The sun and the stars and the shape of your earth are the witnesses of your gain; whereas a handful of pages, no matter how much you have made them your own, are at best but an obscure and questionable spoil. Here they are. “Failure”﻿—“Astonishing”: take your choice; or perhaps both, or neither﻿—a mere rustle and flutter of pieces of paper settling down in the night, and undistinguishable, like the snowflakes of a great drift destined to melt away in sunshine.

===Chinese===
我的居所四周没有壕沟，也没有围墙。窗开着；门也敞着，迎着我工作的那位最好的朋友——漫野温暖而静谧的阳光。它们在我四周无限有益地铺展着，但说实话，一连几个星期，我不知道太阳是否还照着大地，也不知道头上的星辰是否还沿着指定的轨道运行。我那时正把命数里拨给我的几天，奉献给小说《诺斯特罗莫》的最后几章——一个虚构（但真实）的海岸的故事，它至今还不时被人提起，而且提得相当客气，有时连着「失败」这个词，有时又连着「惊异」这个词。对这种分歧我没有意见。那是永远无法解决的分歧。我只知道，整整二十个月里，我把落到世上最卑微者头上的寻常人生乐趣一概撇开，像古代的先知那样，为我的创造物「与主角力」——为那段海岸的海岬，为普拉西多湾的幽暗，为雪上的光、天上的云，也为了那必须吹进男人女人、拉丁人和撒克逊人、犹太人和外邦人形体里去的生命之息。这些话或许说重了，但要另外怎样来刻画那种创造劳作的亲密与紧张，实在很难：心智、意志与良心全副投入，一小时又一小时，一天又一天，远离尘世，把一切使生活真正可爱温柔的东西都排除在外——这样的事，物质世界里唯一的对应，只有在合恩角以西的冬季西航里那种永无止境的阴郁苦斗中才能找到。因为那也是人与造物主之伟力的角力，在与世隔绝之中，没有生活的安逸与慰藉，在自觉螳臂当车的渺小感之下孤独地挣扎，不为任何相称的报偿，只为赢得一个经度。然而某个经度一旦赢得，便无可争辩。太阳、星辰和你脚下大地的形状，都是你所得的证人；而一小沓纸页，无论你把它们怎样变成自己血肉，至多不过是一宗暧昧可疑的、并不光彩的战利品。它们就在这里。「失败」——「惊异」：随你挑吧；或者两样都要，或者两样都不要——不过是黑夜里纸片簌簌落定的一片轻响，彼此无从分辨，像一场大雪的雪片，注定要在阳光里消融。

===Original===
“How do you do?”

===Chinese===
「你好啊？」

===Original===
It was the greeting of the general’s daughter. I had heard nothing﻿—no rustle, no footsteps. I had felt only a moment before a sort of premonition of evil; I had the sense of an inauspicious presence﻿—just that much warning and no more; and then came the sound of the voice and the jar as of a terrible fall from a great height﻿—a fall, let us say, from the highest of the clouds floating in gentle procession over the fields in the faint westerly air of that July afternoon. I picked myself up quickly, of course; in other words, I jumped up from my chair stunned and dazed, every nerve quivering with the pain of being uprooted out of one world and flung down into another﻿—perfectly civil.

===Chinese===
这是将军家女儿的问候。我什么也没听见——没有窸窣，没有脚步。只在片刻之前，我有过一种灾祸将临的预感；我觉察到一种不祥的临在——警告只有这么多，再无其他；接着便来了那个嗓音，和一声巨响，像从极高的地方可怕地跌落下来——姑且说，是从那个七月午后随微微的西风在田野上空徐徐列队飘行的最高那朵云上跌落。我当然一骨碌爬了起来；换句话说，我从椅子上跳起来，晕头转向，每根神经都因被从一个世界里连根拔起、摔进另一个世界的疼痛而战栗——客客气气。

===Original===
“Oh! How do you do? Won’t you sit down?”

===Chinese===
「哦！你好？请坐，好吗？」

===Original===
That’s what I said. This horrible but, I assure you, perfectly true reminiscence tells you more than a whole volume of confessions à la Jean Jacques Rousseau would do. Observe! I didn’t howl at her, or start upsetting furniture, or throw myself on the floor and kick, or allow myself to hint in any other way at the appalling magnitude of the disaster. The whole world of Costaguana (the country, you may remember, of my seaboard tale), men, women, headlands, houses, mountains, town, campo (there was not a single brick, stone, or grain of sand of its soil I had not placed in position with my own hands); all the history, geography, politics, finance; the wealth of Charles Gould’s silver-mine, and the splendour of the magnificent Capataz de Cargadores, whose name, cried out in the night (Dr. Monygham heard it pass over his head﻿—in Linda Viola’s voice), dominated even after death the dark gulf containing his conquests of treasure and love﻿—all that had come down crashing about my ears. I felt I could never pick up the pieces﻿—and in that very moment I was saying, “Won’t you sit down?”

===Chinese===
这就是我说的话。这段可怕的、然而我向你担保千真万确的回忆，比整整一卷让-雅克·卢梭式的忏悔录告诉你的还要多。请注意！我没有冲她嚎叫，没有掀翻家具，没有扑倒在地板上蹬腿，也没有听任自己用任何别的方式暗示这场灾难骇人的规模。科斯塔瓜纳的整个世界（你也许记得，那是我那部海岸故事的国度）——男人，女人，海岬，房屋，群山，城镇，原野（它的泥土没有一块砖、一粒石、一粒沙不是我用双手亲自安放到位的）；全部的历史、地理、政治、金融；查尔斯·古尔德那座银矿的财富，还有那位威风凛凛的装卸工首领（Capataz de Cargadores）的荣光——他的名字，在夜里呼喊出来（莫尼厄姆医生听见它掠过头顶——用的是琳达·维奥拉的嗓子），在他死后仍旧统治着那收容了他的财宝之征与爱情之征的黑暗海湾——这一切，都已轰然坍塌在我的耳边。我觉得我再也无法把碎片拾起来了——而恰恰在那个当口，我正说着：「请坐，好吗？」

===Original===
The sea is strong medicine. Behold what the quarterdeck training even in a merchant ship will do! This episode should give you a new view of the English and Scots seamen (a much-caricatured folk) who had the last say in the formation of my character. One is nothing if not modest, but in this disaster I think I have done some honour to their simple teaching. “Won’t you sit down?” Very fair; very fair indeed. She sat down. Her amused glance strayed all over the room. There were pages of MS. on the table and under the table, a batch of typed copy on a chair, single leaves had fluttered away into distant corners; there were there living pages, pages scored and wounded, dead pages that would be burned at the end of the day﻿—the litter of a cruel battlefield, of a long, long and desperate fray. Long! I suppose I went to bed sometimes, and got up the same number of times. Yes, I suppose I slept, and ate the food put before me, and talked connectedly to my household on suitable occasions. But I had never been aware of the even flow of daily life, made easy and noiseless for me by a silent, watchful, tireless affection. Indeed, it seemed to me that I had been sitting at that table surrounded by the litter of a desperate fray for days and nights on end. It seemed so, because of the intense weariness of which that interruption had made me aware﻿—the awful disenchantment of a mind realising suddenly the futility of an enormous task, joined to a bodily fatigue such as no ordinary amount of fairly heavy physical labour could ever account for. I have carried bags of wheat on my back, bent almost double under a ship’s deck-beams, from six in the morning till six in the evening (with an hour and a half off for meals), so I ought to know.

===Chinese===
大海是烈性的药。瞧吧，就连商船上的后甲板训练，也能造就这个！这段插曲应该让你对英格兰和苏格兰水手——一群常被漫画化的人——刮目相看，是他们最终说了算的那几句，塑成了我的性格。人不可不谦逊，但在这场灾难里，我想我没有辜负他们朴素的教诲。「请坐，好吗？」很不错；实在很不错。她坐下了。她那饶有兴味的目光在房间里四处逡巡。桌子上有手稿，桌子底下也有手稿，一把椅子上放着一沓打字副本，零散的书页已飘进远处的角落；那里有活着的书页，有涂画得伤痕累累的书页，有到天黑就要烧掉的死书页——一场残酷战场的狼藉，一场漫长、漫长而绝望的鏖战。漫长！我想我有时是上床睡了的，起床的次数与上床一样多。是的，我想我睡过觉，把摆到面前的饭吃过，在适当的场合同家里人连贯通顺地说过话。但我从不曾意识到日常生活的平稳流动——那由一种沉默的、警觉的、不知疲倦的疼爱替我弄得安稳而无声的流动。的确，在我看来，我仿佛已在那张桌子旁边、在那场绝望鏖战的狼藉中间，不分昼夜地坐了不知多少天。之所以如此，是因为那场打断让我骤然察觉的极度疲惫——一个心智突然意识到一项浩大工程之徒劳时的可怕幻灭，再加上一种身体的疲乏，一种任何寻常分量的相当繁重的体力劳动都无法解释的疲乏。我背过整袋的小麦，在船甲板横梁下几乎弯成对折，从早晨六点背到晚上六点（中间休息一个半小时吃饭），所以我该有数。

===Original===
And I love letters. I am jealous of their honour and concerned for the dignity and comeliness of their service. I was, most likely, the only writer that neat lady had ever caught in the exercise of his craft, and it distressed me not to be able to remember when it was that I dressed myself last, and how. No doubt that would be all right in essentials. The fortune of the house included a pair of grey-blue watchful eyes that would see to that. But I felt somehow as grimy as a Costaguana lepero after a day’s fighting in the streets, rumpled all over and dishevelled down to my very heels. And I am afraid I blinked stupidly. All this was bad for the honour of letters and the dignity of their service. Seen indistinctly through the dust of my collapsed universe, the good lady glanced about the room with a slightly amused serenity. And she was smiling. What on earth was she smiling at? She remarked casually:

===Chinese===
而我是爱文学的。我珍视它的名誉，关心它的服役的尊严与体面。我很可能是那位整洁的太太有生以来当场撞见的唯一一位正在行使本行的作家，而我竟想不起自己上一回是什么时候、怎样穿扮整齐的，这叫我着恼。毫无疑问，要紧的方面是错不了的。这个家的运气里包含着一双灰蓝色的、留意一切的眼睛，自会照应到那一点。但我不知怎么觉得，自己像一个在街巷里打了一整天仗的科斯塔瓜纳麻风病人那样浑身腌臜，从头到脚皱皱巴巴、蓬头垢面。而且我怕我还傻乎乎地直眨眼睛。这一切都有损文学的名誉与它服役的尊严。隔着我这坍塌宇宙的尘埃望去影影绰绰，那位好太太带着一丝被逗乐的安详，朝房间里四处打量。她在微笑。她究竟在笑什么？她随口说道：

===Original===
“I am afraid I interrupted you.”

===Chinese===
「恐怕我打扰你了。」

===Original===
“Not at all.”

===Chinese===
「哪里的话。」

===Original===
She accepted the denial in perfect good faith. And it was strictly true. Interrupted﻿—indeed! She had robbed me of at least twenty lives, each infinitely more poignant and real than her own, because informed with passion, possessed of convictions, involved in great affairs created out of my own substance for an anxiously meditated end.

===Chinese===
她完完全全地相信了这句否认。而这也千真万确。打扰——亏你说得出！她剥夺了我至少二十条性命，每一条都比她自己的真切、生动无限倍，因为那些性命浸透着激情，怀抱着信念，卷入了我用自己的血肉为着一个苦心冥想的结局创造出来的大事。

===Original===
She remained silent for a while, then said with a last glance all round at the litter of the fray:

===Chinese===
她沉默了一会儿，然后朝那场鏖战的狼藉最后环顾一周，说道：

===Original===
“And you sit like this here writing your﻿—your﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「你就这样坐在这儿，写着你的——你的……」

===Original===
“I﻿—what? Oh, yes! I sit here all day.”

===Chinese===
「我——什么？哦，是啊！我整天坐在这儿。」

===Original===
“It must be perfectly delightful.”

===Chinese===
「那一定美妙极了。」

===Original===
I suppose that, being no longer very young, I might have been on the verge of having a stroke; but she had left her dog in the porch, and my boy’s dog, patrolling the field in front, had espied him from afar. He came on straight and swift like a cannonball, and the noise of the fight, which burst suddenly upon our ears, was more than enough to scare away a fit of apoplexy. We went out hastily and separated the gallant animals. Afterwards I told the lady where she would find my wife﻿—just round the corner, under the trees. She nodded and went off with her dog, leaving me appalled before the death and devastation she had lightly made﻿—and with the awfully instructive sound of the word “delightful” lingering in my ears.

===Chinese===
我想，鉴于我已不再年轻，我本来很可能处在中风发作的边缘；但她把她的狗留在了门廊里，而我儿子的狗正在屋前的田野上巡逻，老远就望见了它。它像一颗炮弹似的又直又快地冲过来，那场突然在我们耳边炸响的打斗声，赶走一场中风绰绰有余。我们急忙出去，把两位勇士分开了。随后我告诉那位太太到哪儿能找到我妻子——就在拐角，树底下。她点了点头，带着她的狗走了，把我留在她轻描淡写制造出来的死亡与废墟面前，心里发怵——耳朵里还回荡着「美妙」这个词那可怕而富有教益的音响。

===Original===
Nevertheless, later on, I duly escorted her to the field gate. I wanted to be civil, of course (what are twenty lives in a mere novel that one should be rude to a lady on their account?), but mainly, to adopt the good sound Ollendorffian style, because I did not want the dog of the general’s daughter to fight again (encore) with the faithful dog of my infant son (*mon petit garçon*).﻿—Was I afraid that the dog of the general’s daughter would be able to overcome (*vaincre*) the dog of my child?﻿—No, I was not afraid.﻿ ﻿… But away with the Ollendorff method. However appropriate and seemingly unavoidable when I touch upon anything appertaining to the lady, it is most unsuitable to the origin, character and history of the dog; for the dog was the gift to the child from a man for whom words had anything but an Ollendorffian value, a man almost childlike in the impulsive movements of his untutored genius, the most single-minded of verbal impressionists, using his great gifts of straight feeling and right expression with a fine sincerity and a strong if, perhaps, not fully conscious conviction. His art did not obtain, I fear, all the credit its unsophisticated inspiration deserved. I am alluding to the late Stephen Crane, the author of *The Red Badge of Courage*, a work of imagination which found its short moment of celebrity in the last decade of the departed century. Other books followed. Not many. He had not the time. It was an individual and complete talent, which obtained but a grudging, somewhat supercilious recognition from the world at large. For himself one hesitates to regret his early death. Like one of the men in his “Open Boat,” one felt that he was of those whom fate seldom allows to make a safe landing after much toil and bitterness at the oar. I confess to an abiding affection for that energetic, slight, fragile, intensely living and transient figure. He liked me even before we met on the strength of a page or two of my writing, and after we had met I am glad to think he liked me still. He used to point out to me with great earnestness, and even with some severity, that “a boy *ought* to have a dog.” I suspect that he was shocked at my neglect of parental duties. Ultimately it was he who provided the dog. Shortly afterwards, one day, after playing with the child on the rug for an hour or so with the most intense absorption, he raised his head and declared firmly: “I shall teach your boy to ride.” That was not to be. He was not given the time.

===Chinese===
不过，随后我还是规规矩矩把她送到了院门口。我当然想礼数周全（区区一部小说里的二十条性命，哪值得为它们对一位女士失礼呢？），但主要是——采用奥伦多夫那种健全妥帖的句式来说——因为我不想让将军家女儿的狗再（encore）同我幼子（mon petit garçon）的忠犬打一架。——我是害怕将军家女儿的狗能够战胜（vaincre）我孩子的狗吗？——不，我不害怕。……可是让奥伦多夫教学法见鬼去吧。它对这位小姐沾边的一切固然贴切，似乎也难免，却最不适合这条狗的来历、性情与履历；因为这条狗是那人送给孩子的礼物，而词语之于那人，恰恰没有一丝奥伦多夫式的价值；这人凭着未受训导的天才那阵发性的举动，几乎像个孩子，是言语印象派里心眼最单纯的一位，把他直感与措辞的双重天赋，以一种可贵的真诚、一种或许并未全然自觉的坚固定信运用出来。他的艺术所得到的评价，恐怕配不上它那不假雕饰的灵感应得的全部声誉。我指的是已故的斯蒂芬·克莱恩，《红色英勇勋章》的作者；那部想象之作在逝去的那个世纪的最后十年里，有过短短一阵名声。其后还出了几本书。不多。他没有时间。那是一种个性分明而完整的才能，从世间得到的却只是一种勉强的、多少带着居高临下意味的承认。至于他本人，人们很难为他的早逝一味惋惜。就像他那篇《海上扁舟》里的人物之一，你会觉得他属于那样一种人：命运很少容许他们在桨上的许多劳苦与辛酸之后，安安稳稳地登岸。我承认，我对那个精力充沛、瘦削单薄、生命力炽烈而又转瞬即逝的身影，怀有一种经久不褪的深情。我们还没见面他就喜欢我了，凭的是我写的一两页纸；见了面之后，我很欣慰地想，他仍然喜欢我。他常向我不胜恳切、甚至颇为严厉地指出：「男孩子就*该*有一条狗。」我疑心他是为我疏于为父之责而震惊了。到头来，狗是他提供的。此后不久的一天，他以最专注的投入在地毯上同孩子玩了一个来钟头，抬起头来坚定地宣布：「我要教你儿子骑马。」那没有成。命运没有给他时间。

===Original===
But here is the dog﻿—an old dog now. Broad and low on his bandy paws, with a black head on a white body and a ridiculous black spot at the other end of him, he provokes, when he walks abroad, smiles not altogether unkind. Grotesque and engaging in the whole of his appearance, his usual attitudes are meek, but his temperament discloses itself unexpectedly pugnacious in the presence of his kind. As he lies in the firelight, his head well up, and a fixed, faraway gaze directed at the shadows of the room, he achieves a striking nobility of pose in the calm consciousness of an unstained life. He has brought up one baby, and now, after seeing his first charge off to school, he is bringing up another with the same conscientious devotion, but with a more deliberate gravity of manner, the sign of greater wisdom and riper experience, but also of rheumatism, I fear. From the morning bath to the evening ceremonies of the cot you attend, old friend, the little two-legged creature of your adoption, being yourself treated in the exercise of your duties with every possible regard, with infinite consideration, by every person in the house﻿—even as I myself am treated; only you deserve it more. The general’s daughter would tell you that it must be “perfectly delightful.”

===Chinese===
且说这条狗——如今已是条老狗了。弯弯的短腿撑着又宽又矮的身子，白身上顶着一颗黑脑袋，身子另一头还缀着一块滑稽的黑斑；它出门走动时，惹人发笑，那笑倒也并无多少恶意。它整个模样古怪又讨喜，平常的神态是恭顺的，可一当着同类，脾气便出其不意地露出好斗来。当它卧在炉火光里，头颅高高昂起，定定的、悠远的目光望着屋里的暗影时，它那不曾沾污的一生问心无愧的宁静意识，使它摆出一种惊人的高贵姿态。它带大过一个婴孩；如今，送走了第一任小主人上学之后，它又以同样尽责的挚爱带大第二个，只是神态更加郑重，那是更大的智慧与更熟的阅历的标志，只怕也是风湿的标志。从清晨的洗浴到晚间小床前的种种仪节，老朋友，你一路侍奉着你收养的那只两条腿的小东西，而你自己在尽忠职守时，受到这一家每一个人最周全的体恤、最无限的体谅——一如我自己所受的待遇；只是你比我更当得起。将军家女儿会告诉你，这一定「美妙极了」。

===Original===
Aha! old dog. She never heard you yelp with acute pain (it’s that poor left ear) the while, with incredible self-command, you preserve a rigid immobility for fear of overturning the little two-legged creature. She has never seen your resigned smile when the little two-legged creature, interrogated sternly, “What are you doing to the good dog?” answers with a wide, innocent stare: “Nothing. Only loving him, mamma dear!”

===Chinese===
啊哈！老狗。她从没听见过你因剧痛而发出的尖叫（是那只可怜的左耳朵），那时你以难以置信的自制，保持着僵直不动，生怕把那两条腿的小东西掀翻。她也从没见过你无可奈何的微笑，当那两条腿的小东西被厉声质问「你在对好狗干什么？」时，它睁大一双无辜的眼睛回答：「没干什么。就是爱抚它呀，亲爱的妈妈！」

===Original===
The general’s daughter does not know the secret terms of self-imposed tasks, good dog, the pain that may lurk in the very rewards of rigid self-command. But we have lived together many years. We have grown older, too; and though our work is not quite done yet we may indulge now and then in a little introspection before the fire﻿—meditate on the art of bringing up babies and on the perfect delight of writing tales where so many lives come and go at the cost of one which slips imperceptibly away.

===Chinese===
好狗啊，将军家的女儿不懂自加于己的使命有怎样的秘密条款，不懂在最严酷的自制所换来的报酬里，也可能潜藏着痛楚。可我们已一起生活了许多年。我们也一起老了；虽说我们的活计还没有干完，却也不妨时时在炉火前放任一点自省——想想带孩子的艺术，想想写小说的完美乐趣：那么多的生生死死来来去去，代价只是悄然从指缝间溜走的一条性命。

## VI / 六

===Original===
In the retrospect of a life which had, besides its preliminary stage of childhood and early youth, two distinct developments, and even two distinct elements, such as earth and water, for its successive scenes, a certain amount of naiveness is unavoidable. I am conscious of it in these pages. This remark is put forward in no apologetic spirit. As years go by and the number of pages grows steadily, the feeling grows upon one too that one can write only for friends. Then why should one put them to the necessity of protesting (as a friend would do) that no apology is necessary, or put, perchance, into their heads the doubt of one’s discretion? So much as to the care due to those friends whom a word here, a line there, a fortunate page of just feeling in the right place, some happy simplicity, or even some lucky subtlety, has drawn from the great multitude of fellow beings even as a fish is drawn from the depths of the sea. Fishing is notoriously (I am talking now of the deep sea) a matter of luck. As to one’s enemies, those will take care of themselves.

===Chinese===
回望这一生——它除了童年与少年时代那段序曲，还有两段判然有别的发展，甚至有两种判然有别的元素，比如土和水，作为先后相继的场景——某种程度的天真是难免的。我在这些书页里就意识到了它。这句话并非以辩解的口吻提出的。随着年岁流逝、书页数目稳步增长，一种感觉也日益强烈：人只能为朋友写作。那么，又何必逼得他们不得不申明（朋友自会这样）不必道歉，或者说不定往他们脑子里种下对你审慎的怀疑呢？关于该给那些朋友怎样的体贴，就说这么多——那些朋友，是被这里一个词、那里一行字、一页恰如其分的真情实感、某种可喜的质朴、或者甚至某种侥幸的微妙，从浩浩人海里钓出来的，正如一条鱼从深海里被钓出来。钓鱼是出了名的（我现在说的是深海钓鱼）碰运气的营生。至于敌人，他们自会照管好自己。

===Original===
There is a gentleman, for instance, who, metaphorically speaking, jumps upon me with both feet. This image has no grace, but it is exceedingly apt to the occasion﻿—to the several occasions. I don’t know precisely how long he had been indulging in that intermittent exercise, whose seasons are ruled by the custom of the publishing trade. Somebody pointed him out (in printed shape, of course) to my attention some time ago, and straightway I experienced a sort of reluctant affection for that robust man. He leaves not a shred of my substance untrodden: for the writer’s substance is his writing; the rest of him is but a vain shadow, cherished or hated on uncritical grounds. Not a shred! Yet the sentiment owned to is not a freak of affectation or perversity. It has a deeper, and, I venture to think, a more estimable origin than the caprice of emotional lawlessness. It is, indeed, lawful, in so much that it is given (reluctantly) for a consideration, for several considerations. There is that robustness, for instance, so often the sign of good moral balance. That’s a consideration. It is not, indeed, pleasant to be stamped upon, but the very thoroughness of the operation, implying not only a careful reading, but some real insight into work whose qualities and defects, whatever they may be, are not so much on the surface, is something to be thankful for in view of the fact that it may happen to one’s work to be condemned without being read at all. This is the most fatuous adventure that can well happen to a writer venturing his soul amongst criticisms. It can do one no harm, of course, but it is disagreeable. It is disagreeable in the same way as discovering a three-card-trick man amongst a decent lot of folk in a third-class compartment. The open impudence of the whole transaction, appealing insidiously to the folly and credulity of mankind, the brazen, shameless patter, proclaiming the fraud openly while insisting on the fairness of the game, give one a feeling of sickening disgust. The honest violence of a plain man playing a fair game fairly﻿—even if he means to knock you over﻿—may appear shocking, but it remains within the pale of decency. Damaging as it may be, it is in no sense offensive. One may well feel some regard for honesty, even if practised upon one’s own vile body. But it is very obvious that an enemy of that sort will not be stayed by explanations or placated by apologies. Were I to advance the plea of youth in excuse of the naiveness to be found in these pages, he would be likely to say “Bosh!” in a column and a half of fierce print. Yet a writer is no older than his first published book, and, notwithstanding the vain appearances of decay which attend us in this transitory life, I stand here with the wreath of only fifteen short summers on my brow.

===Chinese===
比如，有这么一位先生，打个比方说，常从双脚一齐朝我踩下来。这个形象毫无优雅可言，但极切合其场合——切合那好几个场合。我不确知他纵情于这种间歇性运动有多久了，运动的季节由出版业的行规调度。前些时候有人把他指给我看（自然是以铅印的形态），我立刻对那条结实的汉子生出了一种勉勉强强的好感。他没放过我的一丝一毫：因为作家的实体就是他的作品；其余的他，不过是一道虚妄的影子，被爱也好被恨也好，都全无批评的根据。一丝一毫都没有！然而我承认的这份情意，并非矫情或乖僻的怪念头。它有一个更深、而且我敢说更可敬的来由，深于感情无法无天的任性。它其实是合法的，因为它是有对价的（虽然给得勉强），有好几重对价。比如说有那副结实，那往往是道德平衡良好的标志。这就是一重对价。被人踩自然并不愉快，但那操作的彻底性——不仅意味着细读，而且意味着真正看进了作品内部，而那作品的优缺点无论是什么，都不怎么浮在面上——想想作品也常常根本没被读过就遭判死刑，这就很值得感激了。那是一个作家把灵魂押到批评家中间去所能碰上的最愚妄的冒险。它当然伤不了人，但令人不快。它令人不快，好比在三等车厢一群体面人中间发现一个玩三张牌戏法的。那整桩交易的露骨的无耻，狡猾地迎合着人类的愚蠢与轻信，那厚颜无耻、当场自认是骗局却又坚称赌局公道的连珠妙语，给人一阵翻胃的厌恶。而一个直性子人公公平平地玩一场公平的赌局时那种坦荡的暴力——哪怕他存心把你击倒——也许骇人，却仍在体面之内。它再怎么伤人，也毫不失礼。人对诚实总会怀几分敬意，哪怕那诚实施用在自己这具贱躯上。但很显然，那样一个敌人，解释止不住他，道歉也抚慰不了他。我若援引年轻来开脱这些书页里的天真，他多半会用一栏半激愤的铅字回敬一句「胡扯！」然而作家不比他出版的第一本书更老；尽管在这无常的人生里，衰朽的虚象伴随着我们，我如今站在这里，额头上不过戴着十五个短短夏天的花环。

===Original===
With the remark, then, that at such tender age some naiveness of feeling and expression is excusable, I proceed to admit that, upon the whole, my previous state of existence was not a good equipment for a literary life. Perhaps I should not have used the word literary. That word presupposes an intimacy of acquaintance with letters, a turn of mind and a manner of feeling to which I dare lay no claim. I only love letters; but the love of letters does not make a literary man, any more than the love of the sea makes a seaman. And it is very possible, too, that I love the letters in the same way a literary man may love the sea he looks at from the shore﻿—a scene of great endeavour and of great achievements changing the face of the world, the great open way to all sorts of undiscovered countries. No, perhaps I had better say that the life at sea﻿—and I don’t mean a mere taste of it, but a good broad span of years, something that really counts as real service﻿—is not, upon the whole, a good equipment for a writing life. God forbid, though, that I should be thought of as denying my masters of the quarterdeck. I am not capable of that sort of apostasy. I have confessed my attitude of piety towards their shades in three or four tales, and if any man on earth more than another needs to be true to himself as he hopes to be saved, it is certainly the writer of fiction.

===Chinese===
那么，说了这样年纪的情感与表达略带几分天真实属情有可原之后，我接着就承认：总的看来，我先前的生存状态并不是文学人生的好装备。或许我本不该用「文学」这个词。那个词预设了与文字的一番亲熟，预设了一种心智的取向与感受的方式，而我绝不敢妄称具备。我只是爱文字；可爱文字并不使人成为文人，正如爱海并不使人成为水手。而且也很可能，我爱文字的方式，恰如文人从岸上望见的他爱海的方式——一片丰功伟业、改换世界面貌的伟大场景，一条通往各种未发现国土的宏阔大道。不，或许我最好这样说：海上生涯——我指的不是浅尝辄止，而是实打实的一大段年头，真正称得上服役的东西——总的说来，并不是写作人生的好装备。不过，上天不容我被人看成忘恩于后甲板上的师长们。我干不出那种背弃的事。我已在三四篇小说里招认过我对他们亡灵的虔敬态度；而世上若有一人比谁都需要对自己真诚——像他指望得救那样——那无疑就是写小说的人。

===Original===
What I meant to say, simply, is that the quarterdeck training does not prepare one sufficiently for the reception of literary criticism. Only that, and no more. But this defect is not without gravity. If it be permissible to twist, invert, adapt (and spoil) M. Anatole France’s definition of a good critic, then let us say that the good author is he who contemplates without marked joy or excessive sorrow the adventures of his soul amongst criticisms. Far be from me the intention to mislead an attentive public into the belief that there is no criticism at sea. That would be dishonest, and even impolite. Everything can be found at sea, according to the spirit of your quest﻿—strife, peace, romance, naturalism of the most pronounced kind, ideals, boredom, disgust, inspiration﻿—and every conceivable opportunity, including the opportunity to make a fool of yourself﻿—exactly as in the pursuit of literature. But the quarterdeck criticism is somewhat different from literary criticism. This much they have in common, that before the one and the other the answering back, as a general rule, does not pay.

===Chinese===
我想说的其实很简单：后甲板的训练，并不足以让人准备好去承受文学批评。仅此而已，别无他意。但这缺陷并非无关紧要。倘若可以歪曲、倒转、改写（并糟蹋）阿纳托尔·法朗士先生对好批评家的定义，那么我们不妨说：好的作家，就是不带显著的欢喜、也不带过度的悲哀，静观自己的灵魂在批评中间历险的人。我决无误导细心公众的意思，让他们以为海上没有批评。那既不诚实，也不礼貌。海上一应俱全，全看你求索的精神——争斗，和平，浪漫，最地道不过的自然主义，理想，无聊，厌恶，灵感——以及一切想得到的机遇，包括出丑露乖的机遇——恰如在文学一行里那样。但后甲板的批评与文学批评略有不同。它们的共同点只有一条：在两者面前，照例回嘴是不上算的。

===Original===
Yes, you find criticism at sea, and even appreciation﻿—I tell you everything is to be found on salt water﻿—criticism generally impromptu, and always viva voce, which is the outward, obvious difference from the literary operation of that kind, with consequent freshness and vigour which may be lacking in the printed word. With appreciation, which comes at the end, when the critic and the criticised are about to part, it is otherwise. The sea appreciation of one’s humble talents has the permanency of the written word, seldom the charm of variety, is formal in its phrasing. There the literary master has the superiority, though he, too, can in effect but say﻿—and often says it in the very phrase﻿—“I can highly recommend.” Only usually he uses the word “We,” there being some occult virtue in the first person plural, which makes it specially fit for critical and royal declarations. I have a small handful of these sea appreciations, signed by various masters, yellowing slowly in my writing-table’s left-hand drawer, rustling under my reverent touch, like a handful of dry leaves plucked for a tender memento from the tree of knowledge. Strange! It seems that it is for these few bits of paper, headed by the names of a few Scots and English shipmasters, that I have faced the astonished indignations, the mockeries and the reproaches of a sort hard to bear for a boy of fifteen; that I have been charged with the want of patriotism, the want of sense, and the want of heart too; that I went through agonies of self-conflict and shed secret tears not a few, and had the beauties of the Furca Pass spoiled for me, and have been called an “incorrigible Don Quixote,” in allusion to the book-born madness of the knight. For that spoil! They rustle, those bits of paper﻿—some dozen of them in all. In that faint, ghostly sound there live the memories of twenty years, the voices of rough men now no more, the strong voice of the everlasting winds, and the whisper of a mysterious spell, the murmur of the great sea, which must have somehow reached my inland cradle and entered my unconscious ear, like that formula of Mohammedan faith the Mussulman father whispers into the ear of his newborn infant, making him one of the faithful almost with his first breath. I do not know whether I have been a good seaman, but I know I have been a very faithful one. And after all there is that handful of “characters” from various ships to prove that all these years have not been altogether a dream. There they are, brief, and monotonous in tone, but as suggestive bits of writing to me as any inspired page to be found in literature. But then, you see, I have been called romantic. Well, that can’t be helped. But stay. I seem to remember that I have been called a realist also. And as that charge too can be made out, let us try to live up to it, at whatever cost, for a change. With this end in view, I will confide to you coyly, and only because there is no one about to see my blushes by the light of the midnight lamp, that these suggestive bits of quarterdeck appreciation, one and all contain the words “strictly sober.”

===Chinese===
是的，海上也有批评，甚至有嘉许——我告诉你，咸水里什么都有——批评一般是即兴的，而且总是口头的，这是它与文学界那套操作外显的差异，随之带来一种新鲜与生猛，那是印出来的字里也许欠缺的。至于嘉许，它出现在结尾，在批评者与被批评者即将分手之际，情形就不同了。海上对我这微末之才的嘉许，有着书面文字的持久性，少有变化的魅力，措辞是一板一眼的。在这一点上文学的宗师占着上风，尽管他实际上也只能说——而且常正是用那句话说的——「我极力推荐」。只是他通常用「我们」这个词，因为第一人称复数里含有某种玄妙的美德，使它特别适合批评的与皇家的文告。我有一小把这样的海上嘉许，由各位船长签字，正在我写字台左手抽屉里慢慢泛黄，在我虔敬的触摸下沙沙作响，像一小把从知识之树上摘下作温柔纪念的枯叶。奇怪！仿佛正是为了这几小片纸——头上是几位苏格兰和英格兰船长的名字——我才直面过种种惊愕的义愤、讥嘲与责难，那对一个十五岁的男孩是难以承受的；我才被指为没有爱国心、没有头脑、也没有心肝；我才经历了自我交战的种种苦楚、洒过不少秘密的眼泪，连富尔卡山口的美景都为我糟蹋了，还被唤作「不可救药的堂吉诃德」，暗指那位骑士从书里带来的疯病。就为了这点战利品！它们沙沙作响，那几片纸——总共不过一打上下。在那微弱的、幽灵似的声音里，活着二十年的记忆，活着如今已不在的粗人的嗓音，活着永世长风的强音，还活着一种神秘咒语的低语，大海的喃喃——它必定是不知怎么到达了我内陆的摇篮，落进我无意识的耳朵，就像穆斯林父亲对着新生婴儿耳中低语的伊斯兰信条，让他差不多在第一口呼吸里就成了信众的一员。我不知道自己是不是个好水手，但我知道我一直是个非常忠诚的水手。而且毕竟还有那一把各条船上的「品行鉴定」可以作证：这些年头并非全然一场梦。它们就在那里，简短，声调单调，但在我看来，它们是和文学中任何神来之页一样耐人寻味的文字。可是话说回来，你知道，我一直被人叫做浪漫派。唉，那没法子。且慢。我好像记得，我也被人叫做现实派。既然这条罪名也成立，那就不妨勉力对得起它一次，不管付出什么代价，换换口味。本着这个目的，我要腼腆地向你吐露——只因为午夜灯下没有人在旁看见我脸红——这些耐人寻味的后甲板嘉许，一概都写着「确乎戒饮」几个字。

===Original===
Did I overhear a civil murmur, “That’s very gratifying, to be sure”? Well, yes, it is gratifying﻿—thank you. It is at least as gratifying to be certified sober as to be certified romantic, though such certificates would not qualify one for the secretaryship of a temperance association or for the post of official troubadour to some lordly democratic institution such as the London County Council, for instance. The above prosaic reflection is put down here only in order to prove the general sobriety of my judgment in mundane affairs. I make a point of it because a couple of years ago, a certain short story of mine being published in a French translation, a Parisian critic﻿—I am almost certain it was M. Gustave Kahn, in the *Gil Blas*﻿—giving me a short notice, summed up his rapid impression of the writer’s quality in the words *un puissant rêveur*. So be it! Who would cavil at the words of a friendly reader? Yet perhaps not such an unconditional dreamer as all that. I will make bold to say that neither at sea nor ashore have I ever lost the sense of responsibility. There is more than one sort of intoxication. Even before the most seductive reveries I have remained mindful of that sobriety of interior life, that asceticism of sentiment, in which alone the naked form of truth, such as one conceives it, such as one feels it, can be rendered without shame. It is but a maudlin and indecent verity that comes out through the strength of wine. I have tried to be a sober worker all my life﻿—all my two lives. I did so from taste, no doubt, having an instinctive horror of losing my sense of full self-possession, but also from artistic conviction. Yet there are so many pitfalls on each side of the true path that, having gone some way, and feeling a little battered and weary, as a middle-aged traveller will from the mere daily difficulties of the march, I ask myself whether I have kept always, always faithful to that sobriety wherein there is power and truth and peace.

===Chinese===
我是不是听见一声客气的低语：「那实在令人欣慰」？是啊，是令人欣慰——谢谢你。被证明为戒饮，至少和被证明为浪漫同样令人欣慰，尽管这样的证书既不够资格当戒酒协会的秘书，也当不上哪个气派的民主机构——比如伦敦郡议会——的官方游吟诗人。上面这段乏味的感想写在这里，只是为了证明我对世俗事务的判断总体是清醒的。我特意声明这一点，是因为两三年前，我的一篇短篇小说出了法译本，一位巴黎批评家——我几乎肯定是《吉尔·布拉斯》报上的居斯塔夫·卡恩先生——给了我一则短评，用「*un puissant rêveur*（一个强有力的梦想家）」几个字概括了他对这位作家品质的快速印象。就这样吧！谁会跟一位善意读者的用词计较呢？然而或许并不是那样无条件的一个梦想家。我斗胆说一句：无论在海上还是岸上，我从不曾失去责任感。让人迷醉的东西不止一种。即使在最勾人的迷梦面前，我也始终记着内心生活的那份清醒、情感上的那分苦修——唯有在其中，一个人所构想、所感受到的赤裸的真理之形，才能不受羞辱地呈现出来。借酒力而出的真理，不过是胡话，而且不成体统。我一生——我两生——都力求做一个清醒的劳动者。无疑我是出于趣味才这样，因为我本能地害怕失去完全自持的感觉；但也出于艺术的信念。然而正路的两侧有那么多陷阱，走了这一程，觉得有点遍体鳞伤、疲惫不堪，正如一个中年旅人仅凭每日行路的艰难也会如此，于是我问自己：我是否始终、始终忠实于那里面有力量、真理与安宁的清醒。

===Original===
As to my sea-sobriety, that is quite properly certified under the sign-manual of several trustworthy shipmasters of some standing in their time. I seem to hear your polite murmur that “Surely this might have been taken for granted.” Well, no. It might not have been. That august academical body of the Marine Department of the Board of Trade takes nothing for granted in the granting of its learned degrees. By its regulations issued under the first Merchant Shipping Act, the very word **sober** must be written, or a whole sackful, a ton, a mountain of the most enthusiastic appreciation will avail you nothing. The door of the examination rooms shall remain closed to your tears and entreaties. The most fanatical advocate of temperance could not be more pitilessly fierce in his rectitude than the Marine Department of the Board of Trade. As I have been face to face at various times with all the examiners of the Port of London, in my generation, there can be no doubt as to the force and the continuity of my abstemiousness. Three of them were examiners in seamanship, and it was my fate to be delivered into the hands of each of them at proper intervals of sea service. The first of all, tall, spare, with a perfectly white head and moustache, a quiet, kindly manner, and an air of benign intelligence, must, I am forced to conclude, have been unfavourably impressed by something in my appearance. His old thin hands loosely clasped resting on his crossed legs, he began by an elementary question in a mild voice, and went on, went on.﻿ ﻿… It lasted for hours, for hours. Had I been a strange microbe with potentialities of deadly mischief to the Merchant Service I could not have been submitted to a more microscopic examination. Greatly reassured by his apparent benevolence, I had been at first very alert in my answers. But at length the feeling of my brain getting addled crept upon me. And still the passionless process went on, with a sense of untold ages having been spent already on mere preliminaries. Then I got frightened. I was not frightened of being plucked; that eventuality did not even present itself to my mind. It was something much more serious, and weird. “This ancient person,” I said to myself, terrified, “is so near his grave that he must have lost all notion of time. He is considering this examination in terms of eternity. It is all very well for him. His race is run. But I may find myself coming out of this room into the world of men a stranger, friendless, forgotten by my very landlady, even were I able after this endless experience to remember the way to my hired home.” This statement is not so much of a verbal exaggeration as may be supposed. Some very queer thoughts passed through my head while I was considering my answers; thoughts which had nothing to do with seamanship, nor yet with anything reasonable known to this earth. I verily believe that at times I was lightheaded in a sort of languid way. At last there fell a silence, and that, too, seemed to last for ages, while, bending over his desk, the examiner wrote out my pass-slip slowly with a noiseless pen. He extended the scrap of paper to me without a word, inclined his white head gravely to my parting bow.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
至于我海上的戒饮，那是由几位当年颇有地位的、靠得住的船长亲笔签署、正式证明了的。我似乎听见你礼貌地咕哝：「这一点总该不言自明了吧。」呃，不。不见得。贸易部海务司那个威严的学术团体，在授予它那些渊博学位时，对什么都不言自明。根据第一部《商船航运法》颁布的规程，**sober（戒饮）**这个字是非写不可的，否则整整一袋、一吨、一座山那么热烈的嘉许，也于事无补。考场的门将对你的眼泪与恳求保持关闭。最狂热的戒酒鼓吹家，在其正直上也凶不过贸易部海务司。由于我这一辈子里曾先后同伦敦港的所有考官面对面打过交道，我的节制之坚定与持久是无可怀疑的。其中三位是航海术考官，命运又安排我隔着适当的海上服役间隔，逐一落进他们每人手里。头一位，瘦高个儿，满头白发，白胡子，举止安静和气，带着一副慈祥睿智的神气，我不得不认定，我的外貌里有什么东西给了他不妙的印象。他那双干瘦的老手松松交握，搁在盘起的腿上，开头用一个温和的声音问了初等问题，然后就问下去，问下去。……它持续了几个钟头，几个钟头。哪怕我是个对商船队潜藏着致命祸害的陌生微生物，受的检查也不会比这更显微。起初我被表面的和善大大安了心，回答得非常敏捷。但到后来，一种脑子渐渐成了一锅粥的感觉爬上心头。而那不动声色的程序仍在继续，带着一种光在预备阶段就已花掉亿万年之感。于是我害怕起来。我不是怕落第；那个结局甚至根本没进过我的脑子。那是件严重得多、也诡异得多的事。「这位古人，」我惊恐地对自己说，「离他的坟墓这么近，必定早把时间的观念丢光了。他正在以永恒为单位考量这场考试。他自己无所谓。他的赛程跑完了。可我呢，等我熬完这段无尽的经历，就算还记得回我那赁居之家的路，走出这间屋子时，也已是男人世界里的一个陌生人，没有朋友，连我的房东太太都把我忘掉了。」这话并不像看上去那样是多大的夸张。我在斟酌答案的时候，脑子里确实闪过一些非常古怪的念头；那些念头与航海术无关，也与这世上任何讲道理的东西无关。我真相信，我时有那么一种蔫蔫的神志恍惚。终于落下一阵沉默，而那沉默也仿佛持续了几个世代，这时考官俯在他的书桌上，用一支无声的笔，慢慢写我的通过单。他把那片纸递给我，一言不发，向我道别的鞠躬庄严地颔了颔他白发苍苍的头。……

===Original===
When I got out of the room I felt limply flat, like a squeezed lemon, and the doorkeeper in his glass cage, where I stopped to get my hat and tip him a shilling, said:

===Chinese===
走出那间屋子时，我觉得自己软塌塌的，像一只挤干了的柠檬；我在门房那玻璃笼子里停下取帽子，赏他一先令小费，他说：

===Original===
“Well! I thought you were never coming out.”

===Chinese===
「嘿！我还当你根本不出来了呢。」

===Original===
“How long have I been in there?” I asked faintly.

===Chinese===
「我在里面待了多久？」我有气无力地问。

===Original===
He pulled out his watch.

===Chinese===
他掏出怀表。

===Original===
“He kept you, sir, just under three hours. I don’t think this ever happened with any of the gentlemen before.”

===Chinese===
「他留住了您，先生，差一点三个钟头。从前的诸位先生里，我不记得有过这种事。」

===Original===
It was only when I got out of the building that I began to walk on air. And the human animal being averse from change and timid before the unknown, I said to myself that I would not mind really being examined by the same man on a future occasion. But when the time of ordeal came round again the doorkeeper let me into another room, with the now familiar paraphernalia of models of ships and tackle, a board for signals on the wall, a big long table covered with official forms, and having an unrigged mast fixed to the edge. The solitary tenant was unknown to me by sight, though not by reputation, which was simply execrable. Short and sturdy as far as I could judge, clad in an old brown morning suit, he sat leaning on his elbow, his hand shading his eyes, and half averted from the chair I was to occupy on the other side of the table. He was motionless, mysterious, remote, enigmatical, with something mournful too in the pose, like that statue of Giuliano (I think) de Medici shading his face on the tomb by Michelangelo, though, of course, he was far, far from being beautiful. He began by trying to make me talk nonsense. But I had been warned of that fiendish trait, and contradicted him with great assurance. After a while he left off. So far good. But his immobility, the thick elbow on the table, the abrupt, unhappy voice, the shaded and averted face grew more and more impressive. He kept inscrutably silent for a moment, and then, placing me in a ship of a certain size, at sea, under certain conditions of weather, season, locality, etc., etc.﻿—all very clear and precise﻿—ordered me to execute a certain manoeuvre. Before I was half through with it he did some material damage to the ship. Directly I had grappled with the difficulty he caused another to present itself, and when that too was met he stuck another ship before me, creating a very dangerous situation. I felt slightly outraged by this ingenuity in piling up trouble upon a man.

===Chinese===
直到走出那座大楼，我才开始脚下生风、飘然起来。而人这种动物既然厌恶改变、又惧怕未知，我便对自己说，下回再考，还是这位先生考我，我真不介意。可等到考验的时节再次到来，门房把我领进了另一个房间，里面是如今已看惯的那套行头：船和索具的模型，墙上挂着信号板，一张长大的桌子上铺满官方表格，桌沿上还固定着一根不装索具的桅杆。屋里那位独居者，我面生，名声却熟：简直糟透了。就我目力所及，他矮壮敦实，穿一套旧的棕色便服，坐着，一手支肘，手掌遮着眼睛，半个身子背着我该坐的桌子那一边的椅子。他一动不动，神秘，疏远，像个谜，姿态里还带着某种哀悼的意味，像米开朗琪罗墓雕上那尊（我想是）朱利亚诺·德·梅迪奇遮着脸的雕像——当然，他离美就差得很远很远了。他一上来就设法引我说胡话。但那恶魔般的癖性我早受过警告，于是信心十足地驳他。过了一阵他不搞了。到此为止一切尚好。可他那纹丝不动、桌上那粗壮的胳膊肘、那突兀而不快的嗓音、那遮着并偏向一边的脸，越来越慑人。他莫测高深地沉默了片刻，然后把我放进一艘一定吨位的船上，在海上，在一定的天气、季节、地点等等条件下——一切都交代得清楚而精确——命令我执行某个操作。我还没做到一半，他已给那条船造成实实在在的损坏。我刚应付好这个难题，他又生出下一个；那也应付过去了，他又在我面前横插进一条船，造出一个非常凶险的局面。这种往一个人身上堆祸事的发明才能，让我觉得受了点冒犯。

===Original===
“I wouldn’t have got into that mess,” I suggested, mildly. “I could have seen that ship before.”

===Chinese===
「我就不会陷进那团乱子里，」我温和地建言，「那条船我本来能提前望见的。」

===Original===
He never stirred the least bit.

===Chinese===
他纹丝没动一下。

===Original===
“No, you couldn’t. The weather’s thick.”

===Chinese===
「不，你望不见。天气有雾。」

===Original===
“Oh! I didn’t know,” I apologised blankly. I suppose that after all I managed to stave off the smash with sufficient approach to verisimilitude, and the ghastly business went on. You must understand that the scheme of the test he was applying to me was, I gathered, a homeward passage﻿—the sort of passage I would not wish to my bitterest enemy. That imaginary ship seemed to labour under a most comprehensive curse. It’s no use enlarging on these never-ending misfortunes; suffice it to say that long before the end I would have welcomed with gratitude an opportunity to exchange into the *Flying Dutchman*. Finally he shoved me into the North Sea (I suppose) and provided me with a lee-shore with outlying sandbanks﻿—the Dutch coast, presumably. Distance, eight miles. The evidence of such implacable animosity deprived me of speech for quite half a minute.

===Chinese===
「哦！我不知道。」我茫然地致歉。我想，我到底还是把那场覆灭应付了过去，够得上几分逼真，于是那场阴惨的营生继续进行。你必须明白，据我领会，他加于我的这套考题的总图样，是一趟回国的航程——那种航程，我连最仇的仇人也不愿许给他。那条想象中的船仿佛身负一道包罗万象的诅咒。这些没完没了的灾祸不必细表；只需说一句：远没到结尾，我就巴不得有个机会调到「漂泊的荷兰人」号上去，而且会感激不尽。最后他把我推进了北海（我猜），又给我配上一面下风岸，外加外围的沙洲——想来是荷兰海岸。距离，八英里。这种不共戴天的敌意，害我足足半分钟说不出话。

===Original===
“Well,” he said﻿—for our pace had been very smart indeed till then.

===Chinese===
「怎么样？」他说——因为直到那时，我们的航速实在快得很。

===Original===
“I will have to think a little, sir.”

===Chinese===
「我得稍微想一想，船长。」

===Original===
“Doesn’t look as if there were much time to think,” he muttered sardonically from under his hand.

===Chinese===
「看起来可不像还有多少时间想。」他手底下讥诮地咕哝道。

===Original===
“No, sir,” I said with some warmth. “Not on board a ship I could see. But so many accidents have happened that I really can’t remember what there’s left for me to work with.”

===Chinese===
「是的，船长，」我带几分热地说。「在我看得见的这船上是没时间想。可是出了这么多事故，我实在记不得还给我剩下什么家什可用了。」

===Original===
Still half averted, and with his eyes concealed, he made unexpectedly a grunting remark.

===Chinese===
他依旧半背着脸，眼睛遮着，冷不丁咕哝了一句。

===Original===
“You’ve done very well.”

===Chinese===
「你答得很好。」

===Original===
“Have I the two anchors at the bow, sir?” I asked.

===Chinese===
「我船头上有两只锚吧，船长？」我问。

===Original===
“Yes.”

===Chinese===
「有。」

===Original===
I prepared myself then, as a last hope for the ship, to let them both go in the most effectual manner, when his infernal system of testing resourcefulness came into play again.

===Chinese===
于是我做好了准备，要作为这条船最后的指望，用最得力的方式把两只锚一齐抛下去，这时他那套考机变的地狱体系又开动了。

===Original===
“But there’s only one cable. You’ve lost the other.”

===Chinese===
「可是锚链只有一条。另一条你已经丢了。」

===Original===
It was exasperating.

===Chinese===
真叫人火冒三丈。

===Original===
“Then I would back them, if I could, and tail the heaviest hawser on board on the end of the chain before letting go, and if she parted from that, which is quite likely, I would just do nothing. She would have to go.”

===Chinese===
「那我就倒车顶风，要是顶得住，在抛锚之前把船上最粗的一根缆索接在锚链尾上；要是连那也崩断了——多半会的——我就干脆什么都不做。船只好认命。」

===Original===
“Nothing more to do, eh?”

===Chinese===
「再没有办法了吧，嗯？」

===Original===
“No, sir. I could do no more.”

===Chinese===
「没有了，船长。我再无能为力。」

===Original===
He gave a bitter half-laugh.

===Chinese===
他苦笑了一声。

===Original===
“You could always say your prayers.”

===Chinese===
「你总还可以祷告祷告。」

===Original===
He got up, stretched himself, and yawned slightly. It was a sallow, strong, unamiable face. He put me in a surly, bored fashion through the usual questions as to lights and signals, and I escaped from the room thankfully﻿—passed! Forty minutes! And again I walked on air along Tower Hill, where so many good men had lost their heads, because, I suppose, they were not resourceful enough to save them. And in my heart of hearts I had no objection to meeting that examiner once more when the third and last ordeal became due in another year or so. I even hoped I should. I knew the worst of him now, and forty minutes is not an unreasonable time. Yes, I distinctly hoped.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
他站起身，伸了个懒腰，微微打了个呵欠。那是一张灰黄、强硬、不讨人喜欢的脸。他没精打采、老大不耐烦地把我过了一遍关于号灯和信号的例行问题，我便感激地逃出了房间——过了！四十分钟！我又一次沿着塔丘脚下生风，在那地方，那么多好人丢过脑袋，我想，是因为他们机变不够，救不下自己的脑袋。而在我的心底，一年多以后第三场、也是最后一场考验到期时，再见那位考官一面，我毫无异议。我甚至希望如此。他的底细我如今摸清了，而四十分钟也不算个不近情理的时长。是的，我清清楚楚地希望着。……

===Original===
But not a bit of it. When I presented myself to be examined for Master the examiner who received me was short, plump, with a round, soft face in grey, fluffy whiskers, and fresh, loquacious lips.

===Chinese===
可是全然不是那么回事。轮到应考船长的时候，接待我的那位考官是个矮胖子，一张圆圆的软脸埋在灰白的绒毛连鬓胡里，两片新鲜水灵、爱说话的嘴唇。

===Original===
He commenced operations with an easygoing “Let’s see. H’m. Suppose you tell me all you know of charter-parties.” He kept it up in that style all through, wandering off in the shape of comment into bits out of his own life, then pulling himself up short and returning to the business in hand. It was very interesting. “What’s your idea of a jury-rudder now?” he queried suddenly, at the end of an instructive anecdote bearing upon a point of stowage.

===Chinese===
他一上来就用闲适的口吻开了工：「让我想想。唔。你就把你知道的租船合同全说给我听听吧。」他从头到尾都保持这个风格，时不时以评点的名义岔进他自己生平的片断，随即猛地收住，回到手头的正题。那非常有趣。「那么你对应急舵有什么看法？」他在一段关于配载要点的有教益的轶事末尾，突然发问。

===Original===
I warned him that I had no experience of a lost rudder at sea, and gave him two classical examples of makeshifts out of a textbook. In exchange he described to me a jury-rudder he had invented himself years before, when in command of a three-thousand-ton steamer. It was, I declare, the cleverest contrivance imaginable. “May be of use to you some day,” he concluded. “You will go into steam presently. Everybody goes into steam.”

===Chinese===
我提醒他，海上失舵的事我没有经验，便从一本教科书里给他举了两个经典的应急代用装置的例子。作为交换，他向我描绘了一具他多年前自己发明的应急舵，那时他正指挥一艘三千吨的轮船。我敢说，那是可以想见的最巧妙的设计。「说不定哪天对你有用，」他总结道，「你不久就要上轮船的。人人都上轮船。」

===Original===
There he was wrong. I never went into steam﻿—not really. If I only live long enough I shall become a bizarre relic of a dead barbarism, a sort of monstrous antiquity, the only seaman of the dark ages who had never gone into steam﻿—not really.

===Chinese===
这一点他说错了。我从没有上过轮船——当真没有。只要我活得够久，我就会变成一种死去的蛮风遗留下来的古怪遗物，一种骇人的老古董，黑暗时代唯一从没上过轮船——当真没上过——的水手。

===Original===
Before the examination was over he imparted to me a few interesting details of the transport service in the time of the Crimean War.

===Chinese===
考试结束之前，他还向我传授了克里米亚战争时期运输业务的几处有趣细节。

===Original===
“The use of wire rigging became general about that time too,” he observed. “I was a very young master then. That was before you were born.”

===Chinese===
「钢丝索具的通行也是差不多那时候的事，」他说，「那年头我是个很年轻的船长。那还是你出生以前的事。」

===Original===
“Yes, sir. I am of the year of 1857.”

===Chinese===
「是的，船长。我是一八五七年生的。」

===Original===
“The Mutiny year,” he commented, as if to himself, adding in a louder tone that his ship happened then to be in the Gulf of Bengal, employed under a Government charter.

===Chinese===
「兵变那年。」他评点道，像是自言自语，又放高声音补充说，他那条船当时恰好在孟加拉湾，受政府雇运。

===Original===
Clearly the transport service had been the making of this examiner, who so unexpectedly had given me an insight into his existence, awakening in me the sense of the continuity of that sea-life into which I had stepped from outside; giving a touch of human intimacy to the machinery of official relations. I felt adopted. His experience was for me, too, as though he had been an ancestor.

===Chinese===
显然，正是运输业务成就了这位考官；他这般出其不意地让我窥见了他的存在，唤醒了我对那种海上生涯之延续性的意识——我正是从外面一步跨进那生涯里去的；他给官方关系的机器添了一抹人情的亲昵。我觉得自己被收养了。他的阅历之于我，仿佛他是一位先祖。

===Original===
Writing my long name (it has twelve letters) with laborious care on the slip of blue paper, he remarked:

===Chinese===
他费力而用心地把我那个长长的名字（有十二个字母）写在蓝纸条上，一边说道：

===Original===
“You are of Polish extraction.”

===Chinese===
「你是波兰血统。」

===Original===
“Born there, sir.”

===Chinese===
「生在那里，船长。」

===Original===
He laid down the pen and leaned back to look at me as it were for the first time.

===Chinese===
他放下笔，向后一靠，打量我，仿佛头一回看见我似的。

===Original===
“Not many of your nationality in our service, I should think. I never remember meeting one either before or after I left the sea. Don’t remember ever hearing of one. An inland people, aren’t you?”

===Chinese===
「我们的商船队里，你们那个族的人想来不多。我下海前后，就不记得见过一个。也没听说有过。你们是个内陆民族，对吧？」

===Original===
I said yes﻿—very much so. We were remote from the sea not only by situation, but also from a complete absence of indirect association, not being a commercial nation at all, but purely agricultural. He made then the quaint reflection that it was “a long way for me to come out to begin a sea-life”; as if sea-life were not precisely a life in which one goes a long way from home.

===Chinese===
我说是——非常内陆。我们远离大海，不仅是地理位置上，还因为我们同海连间接的瓜葛都完全没有：我们根本不是经商的民族，而纯粹务农。于是他发了一个古雅的感想，说我「跑这么远出来开始海上生涯，路可真不近」；仿佛海上生涯恰恰不是一种让人离家千里的生涯似的。

===Original===
I told him, smiling, that no doubt I could have found a ship much nearer my native place, but I had thought to myself that if I was to be a seaman then I would be a British seaman and no other. It was a matter of deliberate choice.

===Chinese===
我笑着告诉他，毫无疑问，我本可以在离家乡近得多的地方找到船，但我心里打定的主意是：既然要做水手，那就做一个英国水手，别的免谈。那是一桩深思熟虑的选择。

===Original===
He nodded slightly at that; and as he kept on looking at me interrogatively, I enlarged a little, confessing that I had spent a little time on the way in the Mediterranean and in the West Indies. I did not want to present myself to the British Merchant Service in an altogether green state. It was no use telling him that my mysterious vocation was so strong that my very wild oats had to be sown at sea. It was the exact truth, but he would not have understood the somewhat exceptional psychology of my seagoing, I fear.

===Chinese===
对此他微微点头；见他仍旧带着询问的目光望着我，我便多说了一点，招认自己半路上在地中海和西印度群岛消磨过一段时光。我不想把自己全然一身生青地送到英国商船队面前。至于告诉他，我那神秘的召唤强烈到连我的荒唐岁月都非得撒在海里不可——那是分毫不差的实话，可我恐怕，他不会懂得我下海这门心思里那点相当例外的心理。

===Original===
“I suppose you’ve never come across one of your countrymen at sea. Have you now?”

===Chinese===
「我想你在海上从没碰见过你的同胞吧。碰见过吗？」

===Original===
I admitted I never had. The examiner had given himself up to the spirit of gossiping idleness. For myself, I was in no haste to leave that room. Not in the least. The era of examinations was over. I would never again see that friendly man who was a professional ancestor, a sort of grandfather in the craft. Moreover, I had to wait till he dismissed me, and of that there was no sign. As he remained silent, looking at me, I added:

===Chinese===
我承认从没碰见过。考官已把自己交给了闲磕牙的懒散精神。至于我，一点也不急着离开那个房间。一点也不。考试的时代结束了。我再也不会见到这位和气的人了——他是一位行业上的先祖，这门行当里的一位祖父辈。况且，我也只得等他放我走，而那还没有一点影子。见他还沉默着望我，我补了一句：

===Original===
“But I have heard of one, some years ago. He seems to have been a boy serving his time on board a Liverpool ship, if I am not mistaken.”

===Chinese===
「不过我听说过一个，那是几年前的事。如果我没记错，他好像是个在一条利物浦船上当学徒的孩子。」

===Original===
“What was his name?”

===Chinese===
「他叫什么名字？」

===Original===
I told him.

===Chinese===
我告诉了他。

===Original===
“How did you say that?” he asked, puckering up his eyes at the uncouth sound.

===Chinese===
「你刚才是怎么念的？」他问道，听得那粗粝刺耳的音节，眼睛都眯了起来。

===Original===
I repeated the name very distinctly.

===Chinese===
我把那个名字清清楚楚地又说了一遍。

===Original===
“How do you spell it?”

===Chinese===
「怎么拼？」

===Original===
I told him. He moved his head at the impracticable nature of that name, and observed:

===Chinese===
我告诉了他。他朝那名字的无可措手处摇了摇头，说：

===Original===
“It’s quite as long as your own﻿—isn’t it?”

===Chinese===
「它和你自己的名字一样长——是不是？」

===Original===
There was no hurry. I had passed for Master, and I had all the rest of my life before me to make the best of it. That seemed a long time. I went leisurely through a small mental calculation, and said:

===Chinese===
不忙。我已考过了船长，余下的一生都摆在我面前，任我善加利用。那看来是很长的时间。我不慌不忙地在心里做了一道小小的算术，然后说：

===Original===
“Not quite. Shorter by two letters, sir.”

===Chinese===
「不完全一样。短两个字母，船长。」

===Original===
“Is it?” The examiner pushed the signed blue slip across the table to me, and rose from his chair. Somehow this seemed a very abrupt ending of our relations, and I felt almost sorry to part from that excellent man, who was master of a ship before the whisper of the sea had reached my cradle. He offered me his hand and wished me well. He even made a few steps towards the door with me, and ended with good-natured advice.

===Chinese===
「是吗？」考官把签好字的蓝纸条从桌上推给我，从椅子里站起身。不知怎么，这像是把我们之间的关系断得非常突兀，同这位出色的人分别，我几乎觉得难过——早在大海的低语传到我摇篮里之前，他就已是一船之长了。他向我伸出手，祝我好运。他甚至陪我朝门口走了几步，末了给了一句好心肠的忠告。

===Original===
“I don’t know what may be your plans but you ought to go into steam. When a man has got his master’s certificate it’s the proper time. If I were you I would go into steam.”

===Chinese===
「不知道你有什么打算，不过你该上轮船去。人拿到了船长证书，正是时候。我要是你，就上轮船去。」

===Original===
I thanked him, and shut the door behind me definitely on the era of examinations. But that time I did not walk on air, as on the first two occasions. I walked across the Hill of many beheadings with measured steps. It was a fact, I said to myself, that I was now a British master mariner beyond a doubt.
