It was not that I had an exaggerated sense of that very modest achievement, with which, however, luck, opportunity, or any extraneous influence could have had nothing to do. That fact, satisfactory and obscure in itself, had for me a certain ideal significance. It was an answer to certain outspoken scepticism, and even to some not very kind aspersions. I had vindicated myself from what had been cried upon as a stupid obstinacy or a fantastic caprice. I don’t mean to say that a whole country had been convulsed by my desire to go to sea. But for a boy between fifteen and sixteen, sensitive enough, in all conscience, the commotion of his little world had seemed a very considerable thing indeed. So considerable that, absurdly enough, the echoes of it linger to this day. I catch myself in hours of solitude and retrospect meeting arguments and charges made thirty-five years ago by voices now forever still; finding things to say that an assailed boy could not have found, simply because of the mysteriousness of his impulses to himself. I understood no more than the people who called upon me to explain myself. There was no precedent. I verily believe mine was the only case of a boy of my nationality and antecedents taking a, so to speak, standing jump out of his racial surroundings and associations. For you must understand that there was no idea of any sort of “career” in my call. Of Russia or Germany there could be no question. The nationality, the antecedents, made it impossible. The feeling against the Austrian service was not so strong, and I dare say there would have been no difficulty in finding my way into the Naval School at Pola. It would have meant six months’ extra grinding at German, perhaps, but I was not past the age of admission, and in other respects I was well qualified. This expedient to palliate my folly was thought of﻿—but not by me. I must admit that in that respect my negative was accepted at once. That order of feeling was comprehensible enough to the most inimical of my critics. I was not called upon to offer explanations; the truth is that what I had in view was not a naval career, but the sea. There seemed no way open to it but through France. I had the language at any rate, and of all the countries in Europe it is with France that Poland has most connection. There were some facilities for having me a little looked after, at first. Letters were being written, answers were being received, arrangements were being made for my departure for Marseilles, where an excellent fellow called Solary, got at in a roundabout fashion through various French channels, had promised good-naturedly to put *le jeune homme* in the way of getting a decent ship for his first start if he really wanted a taste of *ce métier de chien*.

===Chinese===
我谢过他，把考试的时代决然关在了身后的门里。但那一回我没有像头两回那样脚下生风。我迈着匀整的步子走过多番斩首之地的塔丘。如今千真万确，我是一名英国商船船长了——这是个事实，我对自己说。倒不是我对这桩极本分的成就抱有夸大的想法，何况运气、机遇或任何外来的影响，在这件事上都插不上手。这个本身既叫人满意又不起眼的事实，对我却有一种理想的意义。它是对某些直截了当的怀疑的回答，甚至是对一些不大厚道的诽谤的回答。我从人们斥之为愚蠢的执拗或荒唐的任性的名声里洗清了自己。我不是说一整个国家都被我下海的愿望震动了。但对一个十五六岁之间、天知道多么敏感的男孩来说，他那小世界的骚动实在已是件很可观的事。可观到荒唐的地步：它的回声一直荡到今天。在独处和回顾的时刻，我常发现自己还在迎击三十五年前的论辩与指控——那些嗓音如今已永远沉寂；还能找到当年那个四面受敌的孩子找不出的话，原因很简单：那些冲动对他自己也是个谜。我并不比那些要我解释的人懂得更多。没有先例可援。我真相信，在我这种国籍与出身的男孩里，我这还是孤例：可以说是从他的种族环境与交往中原地一跳，跳了出去。因为你必须明白，我的召唤里不含任何「前程」的念头。俄国或德国，想都不用想。国籍和出身使这不可能。反对奥地利海军的情绪没有那么强，我也敢说，要进普拉的海军学校，本不会有困难。那或许意味着再苦学六个月德语，但我还没超龄，其他方面我也完全合格。这个替我的荒唐减轻罪责的权宜之计，有人想到过——但不是我想的。我必须承认，在那一点上，我的否定立刻就被接受了。那一层情感，连我批评者中最怀敌意的人也完全能够理解。没人再要我解释；实情是，我着眼的本不是海军前程，而是海。而通向它的路，似乎只有法国一条。至少语言我是有的；而且欧洲各国中，波兰与法国的渊源最深。起初还有些门路，可以让人稍稍照看我一下。信在写，回信在收，动身去马赛的安排在一件件办妥；在那里，一位通过各条法国渠道七拐八绕够着了的、名叫索拉里的好人，已经好脾气地答应下来：只要 *le jeune homme*（这年轻人）当真想尝一尝 *ce métier de chien*（这份苦役行当）的滋味，他就设法给他头一回出海带上一条体面的船。

===Original===
I watched all these preparations gratefully, and kept my own counsel. But what I told the last of my examiners was perfectly true. Already the determined resolve, that “if a seaman, then an English seaman,” was formulated in my head though, of course, in the Polish language. I did not know six words of English, and I was astute enough to understand that it was much better to say nothing of my purpose. As it was I was already looked upon as partly insane, at least by the more distant acquaintances. The principal thing was to get away. I put my trust in the good-natured Solary’s very civil letter to my uncle, though I was shocked a little by the phrase about the *métier de chien*.

===Chinese===
这些筹备我都感激地看着，自己的主意却守口如瓶。但我对最后那位考官说的话，是千真万确的。那个铁打的决心——「做水手，就做英国水手」——那时已在我脑中成形，当然，是用波兰语成形的。我一句英文不识，六个词都不识，而我够精明，懂得对自己的目的只字不提为妙。事实上我已经被看成半个疯子了，至少在交情较远的人们眼里。要紧的是脱身走掉。我把信任押在好脾气的索拉里写给我舅舅那封客客气气的信上，虽然那个关于苦役行当的说法让我微微吃惊。

===Original===
This Solary (Baptistin), when I beheld him in the flesh, turned out a quite young man, very good-looking, with a fine black, short beard, a fresh complexion, and soft, merry black eyes. He was as jovial and good-natured as any boy could desire. I was still asleep in my room in a modest hotel near the quays of the old port, after the fatigues of the journey via Vienna, Zurich, Lyons, when he burst in, flinging the shutters open to the sun of Provence and chiding me boisterously for lying abed. How pleasantly he startled me by his noisy objurgations to be up and off instantly for a “three years’ campaign in the South Seas.” O magic words! “*Une campagne de trois ans dans les mers du sud*”﻿—that is the French for a three years’ deep-water voyage.

===Chinese===
这位索拉里（巴蒂斯坦），等我见到真人，原来相当年轻，仪表很好，蓄着漂亮的黑短须，面色红润，一双温柔快活的黑眼睛。他乐天和气，男孩想要多好就有多好。我取道维也纳、苏黎世、里昂，一路劳顿，还在老港码头附近一家朴素旅馆的房间里睡着，他闯了进来，把窗板哗啦推开，放进普罗旺斯的太阳，大声呵责我赖在床上。他那闹嚷嚷的呵责——叫我赶紧起来动身，「去南海干他三年」——把我惊醒得多舒坦。哦，有魔力的词语！「*Une campagne de trois ans dans les mers du sud*（南太平洋三年记）」——法文里，这说的就是一趟三年的远洋航行。

===Original===
He gave me a delightful waking, and his friendliness was unwearied; but I fear he did not enter upon the quest for a ship for me in a very solemn spirit. He had been at sea himself, but had left off at the age of twenty-five, finding he could earn his living on shore in a much more agreeable manner. He was related to an incredible number of Marseilles well-to-do families of a certain class. One of his uncles was a ship-broker of good standing, with a large connection amongst English ships; other relatives of his dealt in ships’ stores, owned sail-lofts, sold chains and anchors, were master-stevedores, caulkers, shipwrights. His grandfather (I think) was a dignitary of a kind, the Syndic of the Pilots. I made acquaintances amongst these people, but mainly amongst the pilots. The very first whole day I ever spent on salt water was by invitation, in a big half-decked pilot-boat, cruising under close reefs on the lookout, in misty, blowing weather, for the sails of ships and the smoke of steamers rising out there, beyond the slim and tall Planier lighthouse cutting the line of the windswept horizon with a white perpendicular stroke. They were hospitable souls, these sturdy Provençal seamen. Under the general designation of *le petit ami de Baptistin* I was made the guest of the Corporation of pilots, and had the freedom of their boats night or day. And many a day and a night too did I spend cruising with these rough, kindly men, under whose auspices my intimacy with the sea began. Many a time “the little friend of Baptistin” had the hooded cloak of the Mediterranean sailor thrown over him by their honest hands while dodging at night under the lee of Château d’If on the watch for the lights of ships. Their sea-tanned faces, whiskered or shaved, lean or full, with the intent wrinkled sea-eyes of the pilot-breed, and here and there a thin gold hoop at the lobe of a hairy ear, bent over my sea-infancy. The first operation of seamanship I had an opportunity of observing was the boarding of ships at sea, at all times, in all states of the weather. They gave it to me to the full. And I have been invited to sit in more than one tall, dark house of the old town at their hospitable board, had the bouillabaisse ladled out into a thick plate by their high-voiced, broad-browed wives, talked to their daughters﻿—thickset girls, with pure profiles, glorious masses of black hair arranged with complicated art, dark eyes, and dazzlingly white teeth.

===Chinese===
他给了我一个美妙的苏醒，他的友情从不倦怠；但我恐怕，他替我找船这件事，并没有本着很庄重的精神来进行。他自己下过海，二十五岁就上岸了，发现岸上谋生可以惬意得多。他的亲眷遍布马赛某一阶层的人家，多到令人难以置信。他的一位伯叔是位体面的船舶经纪人，和英国船有大宗往来；其他亲戚有做船用物料生意的，有开帆具场的，有卖锚链的，有当装卸承包商的、捻缝匠、造船木匠。他的祖父（我想）是个不大不小的贵人：引水员公会的会长。我在这些人中间结识了不少，但主要在引水员中间。我平生在咸水上度过的头一个整天，是应邀在一艘大的半甲板引水艇上：大雾刮风的天气，收紧了帆巡航守望，留意着远处——在又细又高、以一道白色垂直笔画截断风扫地平线的普拉尼耶灯塔之外——冒出来的船帆与轮船的烟。这些结实的普罗旺斯水手都是好客性子。在「*le petit ami de Baptistin*（巴蒂斯坦的小朋友）」这个总称号之下，我被尊为引水员公司的客人，他们的艇昼夜任我乘坐。许多个白昼、也许多个夜晚，我就同这些粗鲁而和善的人一起巡航，正是在他们的庇护之下，我同大海的亲密开始了。多少回，「巴蒂斯坦的小朋友」在夜里贴着伊夫堡的下风处伺望船灯时，被他们诚实的双手盖上地中海水手的连帽斗篷。他们那些让海晒黑的脸，有须的或剃净的，瘦的或饱满的，带着引水员一族专注而多皱的海的眼睛，多毛的耳垂上这里那里悬着一枚细细的金环，俯向我的海上婴儿期。我头一桩有机会观摩的航海作业，是在海上登船：任何时候，任何天气。他们把它给足了我。我还不止一次被邀进老城高而昏暗的宅子，坐在他们好客的餐桌旁，由他们嗓音高亢、额头宽阔的主妇们把马赛鱼汤舀进厚厚的盘子，同他们的女儿们攀谈——那些结实的姑娘，侧影纯正，一头讲究繁复手艺的黑发浓密华美，黑眼睛，牙齿白得耀眼。

===Original===
I had also other acquaintances of quite a different sort. One of them, Madame Delestang, an imperious, handsome lady in a statuesque style, would carry me off now and then on the front seat of her carriage to the Prado, at the hour of fashionable airing. She belonged to one of the old aristocratic families in the south. In her haughty weariness she used to make me think of Lady Dedlock in Dickens’s *Bleak House*, a work of the master for which I have such an admiration, or rather such an intense and unreasoning affection, dating from the days of my childhood, that its very weaknesses are more precious to me than the strength of other men’s work. I have read it innumerable times, both in Polish and in English; I have read it only the other day, and, by a not very surprising inversion, the Lady Dedlock of the book reminded me strongly of the *belle Madame Delestang*.

===Chinese===
我还有另外一些全然不同类的熟人。其中一位，德莱斯唐夫人，一位雕像般端庄的、专断而俊美的夫人，时不时在时兴兜风的钟点，用她的马车把我捎走，坐在前排，去普拉多大道。她出身南方一个古老的贵族之家。她那种高傲的倦怠常使我想起狄更斯《荒凉山庄》里的德德洛克夫人。对这位宗师的这部作品，我怀着的与其说是敬佩，不如说是一种自童年时代起就有的、炽烈而不由分说的爱，连它的弱点在我看来都比别人作品的长处更可贵。它我用波兰文和英文读过无数遍；前几天我还刚读过；而且，经一个不算惊人的倒转，书里的德德洛克夫人如今倒强烈地使我想起「*belle Madame Delestang*（美丽的德莱斯唐夫人）」来。

===Original===
Her husband (as I sat facing them both), with his thin, bony nose, and a perfectly bloodless, narrow physiognomy clamped together as it were by short formal side-whiskers, had nothing of Sir Leicester Dedlock’s “grand air” and courtly solemnity. He belonged to the haute bourgeoisie only, and was a banker, with whom a modest credit had been opened for my needs. He was such an ardent﻿—no, such a frozen-up, mummified Royalist that he used in current conversation turns of speech contemporary, I should say, with the good Henri Quatre; and when talking of money matters reckoned not in francs, like the common, godless herd of post-Revolutionary Frenchmen, but in obsolete and forgotten ecus﻿—ecus of all money units in the world!﻿—as though Louis Quatorze were still promenading in royal splendour the gardens of Versailles, and Monsieur de Colbert busy with the direction of maritime affairs. You must admit that in a banker of the nineteenth century it was a quaint idiosyncrasy. Luckily in the countinghouse (it occupied part of the ground floor of the Delestang town residence, in a silent, shady street) the accounts were kept in modern money, so that I never had any difficulty in making my wants known to the grave, low-voiced, decorous, Legitimist (I suppose) clerks, sitting in the perpetual gloom of heavily barred windows behind the sombre, ancient counters, beneath lofty ceilings with heavily-moulded cornices. I always felt on going out as though I had been in the temple of some very dignified but completely temporal religion. And it was generally on these occasions that under the great carriage gateway Lady Ded﻿—I mean Madame Delestang, catching sight of my raised hat, would beckon me with an amiable imperiousness to the side of the carriage, and suggest with an air of amused nonchalance, “*Venez donc faire un tour avec nous*,” to which the husband would add an encouraging “*C’est ça. Allons, montez, jeune homme.*” He questioned me sometimes, significantly but with perfect tact and delicacy, as to the way I employed my time, and never failed to express the hope that I wrote regularly to my “honoured uncle.” I made no secret of the way I employed my time, and I rather fancy that my artless tales of the pilots and so on entertained Madame Delestang, so far as that ineffable woman could be entertained by the prattle of a youngster very full of his new experience amongst strange men and strange sensations. She expressed no opinions, and talked to me very little; yet her portrait hangs in the gallery of my intimate memories, fixed there by a short and fleeting episode. One day, after putting me down at the corner of a street, she offered me her hand, and detained me by a slight pressure, for a moment. While the husband sat motionless and looking straight before him, she leaned forward in the carriage to say, with just a shade of warning in her leisurely tone: “*Il faut, cependant, faire attention à ne pas gâter sa vie.*” I had never seen her face so close to mine before. She made my heart beat, and caused me to remain thoughtful for a whole evening. Certainly one must, after all, take care not to spoil one’s life. But she did not know﻿—nobody could know﻿—how impossible that danger seemed to me.

===Chinese===
她的丈夫（我坐在他们俩对面）生着瘦削多骨的鼻子，一张全无血色、窄窄的脸，仿佛被两撇规规矩矩的短连鬓胡夹箍在一起；他全无莱斯特·德德洛克爵士那种「贵人气派」与宫廷式的庄严。他只属于高等市民阶层，是个银行家，为着我的用度，在他名下开了一笔小小的信用账户。他是那样一位热切的——不，那样一位冰冻的、木乃伊似的保王党：日常言谈里用的词句，我看是同贤明的亨利四世同一个时代的；谈起钱来，也不像大革命之后那群无法无天的寻常法国人那样用法郎计算，而是用早已废止、被人遗忘的埃居——天下的货币单位里偏偏是埃居！——仿佛路易十四还顶着帝王的排场在凡尔赛的花园里散步，科尔贝先生还在忙着主持海事。你必须承认，在一位十九世纪的银行家身上，这算得一种古雅的癖性。幸而在账房里（账房占了德莱斯唐城中宅邸底层的几间，在一条僻静背阴的街上），账目用的是现代货币，所以我向那些庄重、低声、守礼的（我猜想是正统派的）职员说明来意，从不觉得困难：他们坐在装着粗铁栏的窗户永恒的昏暗里，在幽暗的古旧柜台后，头顶是带厚重线脚檐口的高天花板。每次走出来，我都觉得自己刚从某座极尊严、然而全然尘世的宗教庙宇里出来。也多半是在这类场合，在那道高大的车马门洞底下，德德洛克夫人——我是说德莱斯唐夫人，一看见我举起的帽子，便带着和气的专断把我招到马车旁，用被逗乐了的漫不经心的神气提议：「*Venez donc faire un tour avec nous*（来跟我们一起兜一圈吧）」，丈夫便会补上一句鼓励：「*C'est ça. Allons, montez, jeune homme.*（这就对了。来吧，上车，年轻人。）」他有时问我时间是怎么打发的，问得意味深长，却分寸拿捏得极好、极细致，而且从不忘记表达这样一个希望：我一定在按时给「尊敬的舅舅」写信。我对自己打发时间的方式并不隐瞒，而且我倒觉得，我那些关于引水员们的朴拙故事颇给德莱斯唐夫人解了闷——就那位不可言说的夫人居然能被一个满脑子新见闻的孩子的絮叨解闷的程度而言：那些见闻，得自陌生的人群与陌生的感受。她不发表意见，同我说话也很少；然而她的画像却挂在我亲密记忆的画廊里，被一段短暂而倏忽的插曲钉在了那里。有一天，她让我在街角下了车，向我伸出手，用轻轻的一握留了我片刻。丈夫坐着不动，直视前方；她在马车里俯身向前，用那种从容的声调说了一句，里面恰含一丝告诫的意味：「*Il faut, cependant, faire attention à ne pas gâter sa vie.*（不过，可得上点心，别把这一生给糟蹋了。）」我从来没见过她的脸离我这么近。她使我心跳，让我整整一晚上若有所思。确实，人终归得上点心，别把一生糟蹋了。可她并不知道——谁也不可能知道——这危险在我看来是多么不可能。

## VII / VII

===Original===
Can the transports of first love be calmed, checked, turned to a cold suspicion of the future by a grave quotation from a work on Political Economy? I ask﻿—is it conceivable? Is it possible? Would it be right? With my feet on the very shores of the sea and about to embrace my blue-eyed dream, what could a good-natured warning as to spoiling one’s life mean to my youthful passion? It was the most unexpected and the last, too, of the many warnings I had received. It sounded to me very bizarre﻿—and, uttered as it was in the very presence of my enchantress, like the voice of folly, the voice of ignorance. But I was not so callous or so stupid as not to recognise there also the voice of kindness. And then the vagueness of the warning﻿—because what can be the meaning of the phrase: to spoil one’s life?﻿—arrested one’s attention by its air of wise profundity. At any rate, as I have said before, the words of *la belle Madame Delestang* made me thoughtful for a whole evening. I tried to understand and tried in vain, not having any notion of life as an enterprise that could be mismanaged. But I left off being thoughtful shortly before midnight, at which hour, haunted by no ghosts of the past and by no visions of the future, I walked down the quay of the Vieux Port to join the pilot-boat of my friends. I knew where she would be waiting for her crew, in the little bit of a canal behind the Fort at the entrance of the harbour. The deserted quays looked very white and dry in the moonlight and as if frost-bound in the sharp air of that December night. A prowler or two slunk by noiselessly; a customhouse guard, soldier-like, a sword by his side, paced close under the bowsprits of the long row of ships moored bows on opposite the long, slightly curved, continuous flat wall of the tall houses that seemed to be one immense abandoned building with innumerable windows shuttered closely. Only here and there a small dingy café for sailors cast a yellow gleam on the bluish sheen of the flagstones. Passing by, one heard a deep murmur of voices inside﻿—nothing more. How quiet everything was at the end of the quays on the last night on which I went out for a service cruise as a guest of the Marseilles pilots! Not a footstep, except my own, not a sigh, not a whispering echo of the usual revelry going on in the narrow, unspeakable lanes of the Old Town reached my ear﻿—and suddenly, with a terrific jingling rattle of iron and glass, the omnibus of the Jolliette on its last journey swung round the corner of the dead wall which faces across the paved road the characteristic angular mass of the Fort St. Jean. Three horses trotted abreast with the clatter of hoofs on the granite setts, and the yellow, uproarious machine jolted violently behind them, fantastic, lighted up, perfectly empty and with the driver apparently asleep on his swaying perch above that amazing racket. I flattened myself against the wall and gasped. It was a stunning experience. Then after staggering on a few paces in the shadow of the Fort casting a darkness more intense than that of a clouded night upon the canal, I saw the tiny light of a lantern standing on the quay, and became aware of muffled figures making towards it from various directions﻿—Pilots of the Third Company hastening to embark. Too sleepy to be talkative, they step on board in silence. But a few low grunts and an enormous yawn are heard. Somebody even ejaculates: “*Ah! Coquin de sort!*” and sighs wearily at his hard fate.

===Chinese===
初恋的狂热，难道能被一部政治经济学著作里搬出来的一句庄重引文平息、遏止，扭成一种对未来冷冰冰的疑虑吗？我要问——这可以想见吗？这可能吗？这说得通吗？我双足已经踏在大海的岸边，正要把我的蓝眼睛美梦揽入怀抱，一句关于「别糟蹋了自己一生」的好心告诫，对我年轻的激情又能意味着什么？在我收到的诸多告诫里，这是最出人意料的，也是最后的一句。在我听来，它非常古怪——而且，它偏偏是当着我那位魔女的面说出的，像愚妄之声，像无知之声。但我还不至于麻木或愚蠢到听不出那里面也有善良之声。再说，那告诫的语焉不详——因为「糟蹋一生」这话究竟能有什么意思呢？——又以它那副智慧的深邃气派，攫住了人的注意。无论如何，如我先前所说，*la belle Madame Delestang*（美丽的德莱斯唐夫人）的那几句话，让我整整思索了一个晚上。我努力想弄懂，却徒劳无功——我那时压根没有把人生看作一桩可能经营不善的事业的概念。但午夜前不久，我便不再思索了；那个时辰，不为往昔的鬼魂所缠，不为来日的幻象所扰，我沿着老港（Vieux Port）的码头走去，去我朋友们的引水船上入伙。我知道船会在哪里等它的船员：港口那座堡垒后面一小截运河里。月光下，空寂的码头显得又白又干，仿佛在十二月夜晚凛冽的空气里冻住了。一两个夜游的人悄没声地溜过去；一个海关警卫，一副军人做派，佩着剑，贴着长长一排船头对岸停泊的船只的斜桅底下走来走去，对面是高房子连绵不断、微微弯曲的长长平墙，看上去像一座巨大的被废弃的建筑，数不清的窗子都紧紧关着窗板。只有零零落落几家水手光顾的昏暗小咖啡馆，在石板的青白光泽上投出一抹黄光。走过时，听得见里面一阵低沉的人声嗡嗡——仅此而已。一切多么安静啊，在码头的尽头，在我作为马赛引水员们的客人出海执勤的最后一个夜晚！除了我自己的脚步，没有一点脚步声，没有一声叹息，老城区那些说不出口的窄巷里照常的喧闹，连一丝回音也没有传到我耳边——突然，随着一阵铁和玻璃骇人的哐啷乱响，若利埃特的公共马车跑它最后一趟车，从那堵死墙的拐角上猛地转了出来，隔着石铺的马路，正对着圣让堡（Fort St. Jean）那团特色鲜明的棱角黑影。三匹马并辔小跑，蹄声敲在花岗岩铺石上，那部黄澄澄、闹哄哄的机器在马后颠簸得厉害，荒诞不经，灯火通明，空空如也，车夫在那片惊人的喧嚣上方摇摇晃晃的座位上，看样子睡得正香。我把身子紧贴在墙上，倒抽了一口气。那是一次叫人目瞪口呆的经历。随后，我在堡垒投在运河上的、比阴云之夜更浓重的暗影里踉跄走了几步，看见码头上一盏小小的灯笼亮着，又觉察出一团团裹得严实的人影正从四面八方向它聚拢——第三引水公司的引水员们，匆匆赶来登船。困得没有谈兴，他们一言不发地上船。只听得见几声低低的咕哝和一个大大的呵欠。有人甚至冒出一句：「*Ah! Coquin de sort!*（啊！这该死的命！）」又为自己的苦命疲惫地叹了口气。

===Original===
The patron of the Third Company (there were five companies of pilots at that time, I believe) is the brother-in-law of my friend Solary (Baptistin), a broad-shouldered, deep-chested man of forty, with a keen, frank glance which always seeks your eyes. He greets me by a low, hearty “*Hé, l’ami. Comment va?*” With his clipped moustache and massive open face, energetic and at the same time placid in expression, he is a fine specimen of the southerner of the calm type. For there is such a type in which the volatile southern passion is transmuted into solid force. He is fair, but no one could mistake him for a man of the north even by the dim gleam of the lantern standing on the quay. He is worth a dozen of your ordinary Normans or Bretons, but then, in the whole immense sweep of the Mediterranean shores, you could not find half a dozen men of his stamp.

===Chinese===
第三引水公司的艇长（patron）（那时马赛共有五家引水公司，我想）是我朋友索拉里（巴蒂斯坦，Baptistin）的姻兄弟：四十岁，肩膀宽阔，胸膛深厚，目光锐利坦率，永远在寻找你的眼睛。他向我问好，声音低沉而热诚：「*Hé, l'ami. Comment va?*（嘿，朋友。近来可好？）」他蓄着修剪齐整的小胡子，一张厚实开朗的脸，表情既精力充沛又平和宁静，是沉静型南方人的一个漂亮标本。确有这样的类型：南方人轻浮躁烈的热情，在其中转炼成了坚实的力量。他头发是浅色的，但任谁也不会把他误认作北方人，哪怕只凭码头上那盏灯笼的微光。他抵得上一打寻常的诺曼底人或布列塔尼人；可是，沿着地中海两岸那整整一片浩渺的湾岸，你也找不出六个他这样的人物。

===Original===
Standing by the tiller, he pulls out his watch from under a thick jacket and bends his head over it in the light cast into the boat. Time’s up. His pleasant voice commands in a quiet undertone, “*Larguez.*” A suddenly projected arm snatches the lantern off the quay﻿—and, warped along by a line at first, then with the regular tug of four heavy sweeps in the bow, the big half-decked boat full of men glides out of the black, breathless shadow of the Fort. The open water of the *avant-port* glitters under the moon as if sown over with millions of sequins, and the long white breakwater shines like a thick bar of solid silver. With a quick rattle of blocks and one single silky swish, the sail is filled by a little breeze keen enough to have come straight down from the frozen moon, and the boat, after the clatter of the hauled-in sweeps, seems to stand at rest, surrounded by a mysterious whispering so faint and unearthly that it may be the rustling of the brilliant, overpowering moonrays breaking like a rain-shower upon the hard, smooth, shadowless sea.

===Chinese===
他立在舵柄旁边，从厚夹克底下掏出怀表，就着投进船里的光低下头去。时间到了。他那好听的声音以安静的低调下令：「*Larguez.*（解缆。）」一条手臂蓦地探出，一把抄走了码头上的灯笼——大船先是靠一根缆绳倒拉着挪出去，随后靠船头四支沉重大桨均匀的扳动，载满人的半甲板大船便滑出了堡垒那片屏息的黑影。*avant-port*（外港）的开阔水面在月光下闪烁，仿佛撒满了千万枚亮片；长长的白色防波堤闪耀着，像一厚条实心的白银。滑车一声急响，船帆一声丝绸似的轻拂，一股小风灌满了帆——那风凛冽得仿佛径直从那轮冻僵的月亮上刮下来；船在收桨的脆响过后，仿佛静止不动了，四下里围着一种神秘的低语，轻细得不似人间，也许那正是灿烂逼人的月光，如一阵骤雨碎落在坚硬、平滑、无影的海面上发出的沙沙声。

===Original===
I may well remember that last night spent with the pilots of the Third Company. I have known the spell of moonlight since, on various seas and coasts﻿—coasts of forests, of rocks, of sand dunes﻿—but no magic so perfect in its revelation of unsuspected character, as though one were allowed to look upon the mystic nature of material things. For hours I suppose no word was spoken in that boat. The pilots seated in two rows facing each other dozed with their arms folded and their chins resting upon their breasts. They displayed a great variety of caps: cloth, wool, leather, peaks, earflaps, tassels, with a picturesque round beret or two pulled down over the brows; and one grandfather, with a shaved, bony face and a great beak of a nose, had a cloak with a hood which made him look in our midst like a cowled monk being carried off goodness knows where by that silent company of seamen﻿—quiet enough to be dead.

===Chinese===
同第三引水公司的引水员们一起度过的那最后一夜，我自然记得。此后我也在许多海域和海岸领教过月光的魔力——森林的海岸、岩石的海岸、沙丘的海岸——却再没有见过这般完美的魔法，把事物深藏不露的性格揭示出来，仿佛人获准窥看物质世界的神秘本性。我想，有好几个钟头，那条船上没有说一句话。引水员们面对面坐成两排，抱着胳膊，下巴抵在胸前打盹。他们头上的帽子五花八门：布的、毛线的、皮的，有帽檐的、带护耳的、缀流苏的，还有一两顶压到眉毛上的圆贝雷帽，煞是好看；其中一位老爷爷，脸刮得精光、瘦骨嶙峋，长着一只大鹰钩鼻，披一件带风帽的斗篷，在我们中间，活像一位蒙头巾的修士，被这一船默不作声的水手——静得像死了一般——载往天知道什么地方去。

===Original===
My fingers itched for the tiller, and in due course my friend, the patron, surrendered it to me in the same spirit in which the family coachman lets a boy hold the reins on an easy bit of road. There was a great solitude around us; the islets ahead, Monte Cristo and the Château d’If in full light, seemed to float towards us﻿—so steady, so imperceptible was the progress of our boat. “Keep her in the furrow of the moon,” the patron directed me in a quiet murmur, sitting down ponderously in the stern-sheets and reaching for his pipe.

===Chinese===
我的手指发痒，想摸那舵柄；到得时候，我的朋友艇长便把它交给了我，那心情，同家里赶车的老仆让小少爷在好走的一段路上抓抓缰绳一模一样。四周是一片浩大的孤寂；前方的岛屿——基督山岛（Monte Cristo）和伊夫堡（Château d'If）——浴满月光，仿佛正向我们漂来——我们这条船行进得那样平稳，那样不着痕迹。「让她走在月亮的犁沟里。」艇长在艉座上沉甸甸坐下，伸手去够烟斗，低声这样指点我。

===Original===
The pilot station in weather like this was only a mile or two to the westward of the islets; and presently, as we approached the spot, the boat we were going to relieve swam into our view suddenly, on her way home, cutting black and sinister into the wake of the moon under a sable wing, while to them our sail must have been a vision of white and dazzling radiance. Without altering the course a hair’s-breadth, we slipped by each other within an oar’s-length. A drawling sardonic hail came out of her. Instantly, as if by magic, our dozing pilots got on their feet in a body. An incredible babel of bantering shouts burst out, a jocular, passionate, voluble chatter, which lasted till the boats were stern to stern, theirs all bright now and with a shining sail to our eyes, we turned all black to their vision, and drawing away from them under a sable wing. That extraordinary uproar died away almost as suddenly as it had begun; first one had enough of it and sat down, then another, then three or four together, and when all had left off with mutters and growling half-laughs the sound of hearty chuckling became audible, persistent, unnoticed. The cowled grandfather was very much entertained somewhere within his hood.

===Chinese===
这样的天气，引水站离岛屿只有西面一两英里；不多时，随着我们驶近那处所，我们要去换班的那条船，正在归途中，蓦地游进了我们的视野：它切开月亮的尾流，黑黢黢、凶巴巴，张着一面黑翼；而在他们眼里，我们的帆想必是一道白得耀眼的幻影。航向一丝一毫未改，我们在一支桨的距离内彼此滑过。一声拖腔拖调、冷嘲热讽的招呼从那条船上传来。刹那间，仿佛中了魔法，我们打盹的引水员们齐刷刷站起身来。一场难以置信的斗嘴喧腾爆发出来，一通打趣的、火辣辣的、滔滔不绝的聒噪，一直持续到两船船尾对船尾——那时在他们眼里，我们已变成一团黑影，张着黑翼渐渐离他们而去；而他们连人带帆，在我们眼里通体发亮。那场非同寻常的喧闹，几乎像它爆发时一样突然地消散了；先是有一个够了，坐下；接着又一个；然后三四个一道歇了；等大家都带着咕哝和嗡嗡的半笑收了场，一阵由衷的窃笑声变得清晰可闻，连绵不断，无人理会。那位戴风帽的老爷爷，正在他的帽兜深处自得其乐。

===Original===
He had not joined in the shouting of jokes, neither had he moved the least bit. He had remained quietly in his place against the foot of the mast. I had been given to understand long before that he had the rating of a second-class able seaman (*matelot léger*) in the fleet which sailed from Toulon for the conquest of Algeria in the year of grace 1830. And, indeed, I had seen and examined one of the buttons of his old brown patched coat, the only brass button of the miscellaneous lot, flat and thin, with the words *Equipages de ligne* engraved on it. That sort of button, I believe, went out with the last of the French Bourbons. “I preserved it from the time of my Navy Service,” he explained, nodding rapidly his frail, vulture-like head. It was not very likely that he had picked up that relic in the street. He looked certainly old enough to have fought at Trafalgar﻿—or at any rate to have played his little part there as a powder-monkey. Shortly after we had been introduced he had informed me in a Franco-Provençal jargon, mumbling tremulously with his toothless jaws, that when he was a “shaver no higher than that” he had seen the Emperor Napoleon returning from Elba. It was at night, he narrated vaguely, without animation, at a spot between Fréjus and Antibes in the open country. A big fire had been lit at the side of the crossroads. The population from several villages had collected there, old and young﻿—down to the very children in arms because the women had refused to stay at home. Tall soldiers wearing high, hairy caps, stood in a circle facing the people silently, and their stern eyes and big moustaches were enough to make everybody keep at a distance. He, “being an impudent little shaver,” wriggled out of the crowd, creeping on his hands and knees as near as he dared to the grenadiers’ legs, and peeping through discovered standing perfectly still in the light of the fire “a little fat fellow in a three-cornered hat, buttoned up in a long straight coat, with a big pale face inclined on one shoulder, looking something like a priest. His hands were clasped behind his back.﻿ ﻿… It appears that this was the Emperor,” the Ancient commented with a faint sigh. He was staring from the ground with all his might, when “my poor father,” who had been searching for his boy frantically everywhere, pounced upon him and hauled him away by the ear.

===Chinese===
喊笑话的行列里没有他，他也一动没有动过。他安安静静待在桅杆根下自己的位子上。早有人告诉我，1830 年，在那支从土伦（Toulon）扬帆去征服阿尔及利亚的舰队里，他的军衔是二等熟练水兵（*matelot léger*）。而且，我确实亲眼看过、掂过他那件打补丁的褐色旧外衣上的一颗扣子——那一把五花八门的扣子里唯一的一颗铜扣，扁扁薄薄，上面镌着 *Equipages de ligne*（战列舰水兵队）的字样。我相信，那种扣子是随末代法国波旁王室一起进了历史的。「我是从当年服海军役的时候留下来的。」他解释道，那颗老鹰似的、脆弱的脑袋急促地点着。那件遗物若说是他在街上捡的，可能性实在不大。论年纪，他确实老得足以在特拉法尔加（Trafalgar）打过仗——或者至少在那里当过火药小厮，尽过他小小的一份力。我们相识后不久，他就用一种法普混杂的土话告诉过我——没有牙的嘴巴颤颤地咕哝着——说他还是个「才那么高的小毛孩」的时候，亲眼见过拿破仑皇帝从厄尔巴岛（Elba）回来。那是夜里，他讲得恍恍惚惚，不带一点兴致，地点在弗雷瑞斯（Fréjus）和昂蒂布（Antibes）之间的旷野里。十字路口边燃起了一大堆火。好几个村子的人都聚到了那里，老老少少——连怀里的娃娃都抱来了，因为女人们不肯待在家里。高个子士兵戴着高高的毛茸茸的帽子，围成一圈，默默面对人群，他们严厉的眼睛和浓重的小胡子，足以让人人保持距离。他呢，「是个不知天高地厚的小毛孩」，从人堆里钻了出去，手脚并用地爬到他敢爬的、离掷弹兵的腿最近的地方，从缝隙里张望，发现火光里一动不动地站着「一个矮胖的家伙，戴三角帽，直统统的长外衣一直扣着，一张苍白的大脸偏向一边肩膀，看着有几分像个教士。他双手扣在背后。……据说，这位就是皇帝。」老前辈评了一句，轻轻叹了口气。他正趴在地上拼命张望，忽然「我可怜的父亲」——他正发了疯一样到处找他的儿子——一把逮住他，拎着耳朵把他拖走了。

===Original===
The tale seems an authentic recollection. He related it to me many times, using the very same words. The grandfather honoured me by a special and somewhat embarrassing predilection. Extremes touch. He was the oldest member by a long way in that Company, and I was, if I may say so, its temporarily adopted baby. He had been a pilot longer than any man in the boat could remember; thirty﻿—forty years. He did not seem certain himself, but it could be found out, he suggested, in the archives of the Pilot Office. He had been pensioned off years before, but he went out from force of habit; and, as my friend the patron of the Company once confided to me in a whisper, “the old chap did no harm. He was not in the way.” They treated him with rough deference. One and another would address some insignificant remark to him now and again, but nobody really took any notice of what he had to say. He had survived his strength, his usefulness, his very wisdom. He wore long, green, worsted stockings, pulled up above the knee over his trousers, a sort of woollen nightcap on his hairless cranium, and wooden clogs on his feet. Without his hooded cloak he looked like a peasant. Half a dozen hands would be extended to help him on board, but afterwards he was left pretty much to his own thoughts. Of course he never did any work, except, perhaps, to cast off some rope when hailed, “*Hé, l’Ancien!* let go the halyards there, at your hand”﻿—or some such request of an easy kind.

===Chinese===
这故事像是真切的第一手记忆。他给我讲过许多遍，用的字句一模一样。这位老爷爷对我怀有一种特殊的、多少叫人难为情的偏爱。两极相通。他是那个公司里年纪遥遥最大的成员，而我，请允许我这么说，是它临时收养的娃娃。他当引水员的年头，比船上任何人记得的都长；三十年——四十年。他自己似乎也拿不准，不过他说，这可以到引水员办事处的档案里去查。他多年前就领了养老金，却照旧出海，全出于习惯；正如我的朋友、公司的艇长有一次凑着我耳朵悄悄说的：「老家伙不碍事。他不挡道。」他们待他有一种粗鲁的敬重。时不时地，这个那个会对他讲句把无关紧要的话，可他说什么，其实没有一个人真听。他已经活过了自己的力气，活过了自己的用处，活过了自己的智慧本身。他穿长长的绿色绒线袜，拉到膝盖以上套在裤子外面，光光的脑壳上扣一顶毛线睡帽似的帽子，脚上是木屐。不披那件带风帽的斗篷，他看上去就是个庄稼人。上船时总有五六只手伸过来扶他，可上船之后，就大抵随他自己想心事去了。当然，他从来什么活也不干，至多，有人在喊一声「*Hé, l'Ancien!*（嘿，老前辈！）」的时候，松开手边的一根绳——「把那边扬帆索放了」——或者应承下诸如此类不费力的请求。

===Original===
No one took notice in any way of the chuckling within the shadow of the hood. He kept it up for a long time with intense enjoyment. Obviously he had preserved intact the innocence of mind which is easily amused. But when his hilarity had exhausted itself, he made a professional remark in a self-assertive but quavering voice:

“Can’t expect much work on a night like this.”

===Chinese===
帽兜暗影里的窃笑，谁也没有理会。他足足乐了好一阵子。显然，他那副容易逗乐的天真心态，保全得完好无损。等他的欢畅发泄尽了，他用一种自负而发颤的声音，发表了一句行家意见：

「这样的夜晚，可指望不上多少活儿。」

===Original===
No one took it up. It was a mere truism. Nothing under canvas could be expected to make a port on such an idle night of dreamy splendour and spiritual stillness. We would have to glide idly to and fro, keeping our station within the appointed bearings, and, unless a fresh breeze sprang up with the dawn, we would land before sunrise on a small islet that, within two miles of us, shone like a lump of frozen moonlight, to “break a crust and take a pull at the wine bottle.” I was familiar with the procedure. The stout boat emptied of her crowd would nestle her buoyant, capable side against the very rock﻿—such is the perfectly smooth amenity of the classic sea when in a gentle mood. The crust broken, and the mouthful of wine swallowed﻿—it was literally no more than that with this abstemious race﻿—the pilots would pass the time stamping their feet on the slabs of sea-salted stone and blowing into their nipped fingers. One or two misanthropists would sit apart perched on boulders like manlike sea-fowl of solitary habits; the sociably disposed would gossip scandalously in little gesticulating knots; and there would be perpetually one or another of my hosts taking aim at the empty horizon with the long, brass tube of the telescope, a heavy, murderous-looking piece of collective property, everlastingly changing hands with brandishing and levelling movements. Then about noon (it was a short turn of duty﻿—the long turn lasted twenty-four hours) another boatful of pilots would relieve us﻿—and we should steer for the old Phoenician port, dominated, watched over from the ridge of a dust-grey arid hill by the red-and-white striped pile of the Notre Dame de la Garde.

===Chinese===
没有人接茬。那不过是一句大实话。在这样一个慵懒的、梦般辉煌而心灵澄静的夜里，任何张帆的东西，都别指望能进一个港。我们只好闲闲地来回滑行，在指定的方位界线内守着站；除非黎明时刮起一阵新风，我们将在日出前登上一座小岛——它离我们不过两英里，闪亮得像一块冻结的月光——去「啃口面包皮，对瓶嘴抿一口酒」。这套程序我是熟门熟路。结实的船卸下一船人，便把她那富有浮力、能顶事的一侧，偎贴在礁石本身上——古典的海在性情温柔时，就是这样八面光滑的和善。面包皮啃过，那一口酒咽下——对这群节制的人，真的就只有这一口——引水员们便跺着海盐渍过的石板，往冻麻的手指上呵气，消磨时辰。一两个厌世的，远远坐开，蹲在圆石上，像习性孤僻的、人形的海鸟；好交际的，则一小团一小团比手画脚地编排别人的是非；而且总有一位或另一位我的东道主，拿望远镜的长铜管瞄准空荡荡的水平线——那是件沉甸甸、一脸凶相的公产，永远在一只只手里转来转去，比画着，瞄准着。然后，正午前后（那是短班——长班要值满二十四小时），另一船引水员来换我们——我们便把船驶向那座古老的腓尼基港口；它由守护圣母教堂（Notre Dame de la Garde）那红白条纹的身躯，从一座灰扑扑的干旱山冈的脊线上，俯临着，看护着。

===Original===
All this came to pass as I had foreseen in the fullness of my very recent experience. But also something not foreseen by me did happen, something which causes me to remember my last outing with the pilots. It was on this occasion that my hand touched, for the first time, the side of an English ship.

===Chinese===
这一切都照着我凭最新鲜的经验所作的预见发生了。但也发生了一件我没有预见到的事，一件让我记住同引水员们最后一次出航的事。正是在这一回，我的手头一回触到了一艘英国船的船侧。

===Original===
No fresh breeze had come with the dawn, only the steady little draught got a more keen edge on it as the eastern sky became bright and glassy with a clean, colourless light. It was while we were all ashore on the islet that a steamer was picked up by the telescope, a black speck like an insect posed on the hard edge of the offing. She emerged rapidly to her waterline and came on steadily, a slim hull with a long streak of smoke slanting away from the rising sun. We embarked in a hurry, and headed the boat out for our prey, but we hardly moved three miles an hour.

===Chinese===
黎明并没有带来新风，只有那股稳稳的小气流，随着东方的天空被一种明净无色的光照得亮而如玻璃，添了几分凛冽的锋芒。我们全都在岛上的当口，望远镜里捉到了一艘轮船：一个黑点，像一只昆虫，停在远海那道坚硬的边线上。它迅速露出水线，稳稳驶来，纤瘦的船身，一长绺煤烟，斜斜地拖离初升的太阳。我们急忙上船，把船头对准我们的猎物驶出去，可一个钟头几乎挪不了三英里。

===Original===
She was a big, high-class cargo-steamer of a type that is to be met on the sea no more, black hull, with low, white superstructures, powerfully rigged with three masts and a lot of yards on the fore; two hands at her enormous wheel﻿—steam steering-gear was not a matter of course in these days﻿—and with them on the bridge three others, bulky in thick blue jackets, ruddy-faced, muffled up, with peaked caps﻿—I suppose all her officers. There are ships I have met more than once and known well by sight whose names I have forgotten; but the name of that ship seen once so many years ago in the clear flush of a cold pale sunrise I have not forgotten. How could I﻿—the first English ship on whose side I ever laid my hand! The name﻿—I read it letter by letter on the bow﻿—was *James Westoll*. Not very romantic, you will say. The name of a very considerable, well-known and universally respected North-country shipowner, I believe. James Westoll! What better name could an honourable hardworking ship have? To me the very grouping of the letters is alive with the romantic feeling of her reality as I saw her floating motionless, and borrowing an ideal grace from the austere purity of the light.

===Chinese===
那是一艘高级的大型货轮，如今海上再也遇不到的那种类型：黑色的船体，低矮的白色上层建筑，强劲地装着三根桅杆，前桅上还带着许多横桁；两只手握着它那只硕大的舵轮——那些年头，蒸汽操舵装置还不是想当然的配置——舵轮边、舰桥上另有三个人，厚蓝夹克裹得臃肿，红脸膛，围得严实，戴着尖顶帽——想必是它全套的驾驶员。有些船我遇上过不止一次，看也看熟了，名字却忘了；可是那么多年前，在一个寒冷苍白的日出那澄澈的红光里见过一面的那条船，它的名字我没有忘。我怎么能忘——那是我平生把手上前搭过的第一艘英国船！船名——我在船头一个字母一个字母地读了——是 *James Westoll*（詹姆斯·韦斯托尔号）。不怎么浪漫，你会说。我相信，那是一位相当有分量、颇有名望、广受敬重的北方船东的大名。詹姆斯·韦斯托尔号！一艘正派勤恳的船，还能叫什么更好的名字呢？在我眼里，那几个字母的排列组合本身，就活生生带着她真实的浪漫意味——我记得她一动不动地浮着，从那光线严峻的纯净里，借得一身理想的秀气。

===Original===
We were then very near her and, on a sudden impulse, I volunteered to pull bow in the dinghy which shoved off at once to put the pilot on board while our boat, fanned by the faint air which had attended us all through the night, went on gliding gently past the black glistening length of the ship. A few strokes brought us alongside, and it was then that, for the very first time in my life, I heard myself addressed in English﻿—the speech of my secret choice, of my future, of long friendships, of the deepest affections, of hours of toil and hours of ease, and of solitary hours too, of books read, of thoughts pursued, of remembered emotions﻿—of my very dreams! And if (after being thus fashioned by it in that part of me which cannot decay) I dare not claim it aloud as my own, then, at any rate the speech of my children. Thus small events grow memorable by the passage of time. As to the quality of the address itself I cannot say it was very striking. Too short for eloquence and devoid of all charm of tone, it consisted precisely of the three words “Look out there!” growled out huskily above my head.

===Chinese===
那时我们离它已经很近，我一时心血来潮，自告奋勇到小艇上去划头桨——小艇立刻撑离了大船，送引水员登轮；我们的大船，被那一整夜不曾离开我们的微风扇着，继续轻轻地从船身那黑亮修长的旁边滑过去。划了几桨，我们靠上了它的船侧；正是在那时，我有生以来头一回听见有人用英语对我说话——那是我暗中选定的语言，我未来的语言，长久友谊的语言，最深挚感情的语言，劳作时辰与安闲时辰的语言，也是孤独时辰的语言，是读过的书、追索过的思想、记住了的情感的语言——是我梦境本身的语言！倘若（在我那不朽的部分被它如此塑造之后）我还不敢大声把它认作我自己的，那么，它至少是我儿女们的语言。小事就是这样随着时间流逝而变得难忘的。至于那声招呼本身的成色，我不能说它如何了不得。短得够不上雄辩，又毫无声调之美，它恰恰由三个词构成：「当心那儿！」在我头顶上沙哑地吼了出来。

===Original===
It proceeded from a big fat fellow (he had an obtrusive, hairy double chin) in a blue woollen shirt and roomy breeches pulled up very high, even to the level of his breastbone, by a pair of braces quite exposed to public view. As where he stood there was no bulwark but only a rail and stanchions, I was able to take in at a glance the whole of his voluminous person from his feet to the high crown of his soft black hat, which sat like an absurd flanged cone on his big head. The grotesque and massive aspect of that deck hand (I suppose he was that﻿—very likely the lamp-trimmer) surprised me very much. My course of reading, of dreaming and longing for the sea had not prepared me for a sea-brother of that sort. I never met again a figure in the least like his except in the illustrations to Mr. W. W. Jacobs’s most entertaining tales of barges and coasters; but the inspired talent of Mr. Jacobs for poking endless fun at poor, innocent sailors in a prose which, however extravagant in its felicitous invention, is always artistically adjusted to observed truth, was not yet. Perhaps Mr. Jacobs himself was not yet. I fancy that, at most, if he had made his nurse laugh it was about all he had achieved at that early date.

===Chinese===
说话的是个大胖子（他有一个招摇的、毛茸茸的双下巴），穿蓝法兰绒衬衫，一条宽大的裤子被一副完全暴露在众目睽睽之下的背带高高吊起，吊到了胸骨的高度。他站的地方没有舷墙，只有栏杆和栏杆柱，所以从他的脚到他软塌塌的黑帽子那高高的帽筒，他那庞大的全身，我能一眼收尽——那顶帽子扣在他大脑袋上，活像一只荒唐的带法兰边的圆锥。那个水手（我猜他是水手——多半是管点灯的）那副又怪又笨重的模样，让我大吃一惊。我那一整套读书、做梦、向往大海的经历，可没让我对这样一号海上兄弟有过半点思想准备。这样的形象，我后来再没有见过一个，除了在 W. W. 雅各布斯先生（Mr. W. W. Jacobs）那些写驳船和沿海小船的极为有趣的故事的插图里；只是雅各布斯先生那副天纵的才具——专爱拿可怜而无辜的水手开无穷无尽的玩笑，那支笔无论怎样妙想天开，总在艺术上拿捏着合乎观察到的真实——那时还没有长成。说不定雅各布斯先生本人都还没有呢。我琢磨着，至多，他那时要是曾逗得他的奶妈一笑，就已经是他全部的成就了。

===Original===
Therefore, I repeat, other disabilities apart, I could not have been prepared for the sight of that husky old porpoise. The object of his concise address was to call my attention to a rope which he incontinently flung down for me to catch. I caught it, though it was not really necessary, the ship having no way on her by that time. Then everything went on very swiftly. The dinghy came with a slight bump against the steamer’s side; the pilot, grabbing the rope ladder, had scrambled halfway up before I knew that our task of boarding was done; the harsh, muffled clanging of the engine-room telegraph struck my ear through the iron plate; my companion in the dinghy was urging me to “shove off﻿—push hard”; and when I bore against the smooth flank of the first English ship I ever touched in my life, I felt it already throbbing under my open palm.

===Chinese===
所以，我再说一遍，旁的短处且不去说，我原本也不可能对看见那条嗓音沙哑的老海豚有所准备。他那简明扼要的招呼，用意是要我注意一根缆绳——他不由分说地把绳子抛了下来让我接。我接住了，虽说实在没有必要，因为那时轮船已经没有余速了。随后一切都进行得飞快。小艇轻轻一磕，碰上轮船的船侧；引水员抓住绳梯，等我明白过来我们送人登轮的任务已经完成时，他已经爬上去一半了；机舱传令钟那刺耳的、闷闷的哐哐声，穿过铁板敲进我的耳朵；小艇里的同伴正催我「撑开——用力推」；而当我向平生触到的第一艘英国船那光洁的侧腹抵过去时，我感到它已经在我摊开的手掌底下悸动起来。

===Original===
Her head swung a little to the west, pointing towards the miniature lighthouse of the Jolliette breakwater, far away there, hardly distinguishable against the land. The dinghy danced a squashy, splashy jig in the wash of the wake and turning in my seat I followed the *James Westoll* with my eyes. Before she had gone in a quarter of a mile she hoisted her flag as the harbour regulations prescribe for arriving and departing ships. I saw it suddenly flicker and stream out on the flagstaff. The Red Ensign! In the pellucid, colourless atmosphere bathing the drab and grey masses of that southern land, the livid islets, the sea of pale glassy blue under the pale glassy sky of that cold sunrise, it was as far as the eye could reach the only spot of ardent colour﻿—flame-like, intense, and presently as minute as the tiny red spark the concentrated reflection of a great fire kindles in the clear heart of a globe of crystal. The Red Ensign﻿—the symbolic, protecting warm bit of bunting flung wide upon the seas, and destined for so many years to be the only roof over my head.

===Chinese===
它的船头微微转向西面，指向若利埃特防波堤那座小小的灯塔——远远的，衬着陆地，几乎分辨不出来。小艇在尾流的涌浪里跳着一支湿漉漉、水花四溅的吉格舞；我在座位上转过身，目送「詹姆斯·韦斯托尔号」。它还没驶进四分之一英里，便按港口规章对进出港船舶的规定，升起了它的旗。我看见那旗在旗杆上蓦地一抖，随即展开飘扬。红船旗（英国商船旗）！在澄澈而无色的空气里——那空气沐浴着南方那片土地灰蒙蒙的土色巨块、青灰的岛屿，沐浴着冷清日出之下淡玻璃色天空底下淡玻璃色的大海——放眼所及，那是唯一一点炽热的颜色——像火焰，浓烈，不多时便小得像一场大火汇聚的反光，在一只水晶球澄澈的心里点燃的那粒小小的红火星。红船旗——那面有象征意味的、护佑人的、温暖的小旗，在四海之上远远地展开，并注定要在那么多年里，做我头顶唯一的屋顶。
